# Khenrab Jamyang's Wish-Fulfilling Jewel Lamp for the Fortunate: A Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising ^0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^0-1]]

Herein lies the text entitled "The Wish-Fulfilling Jewel Lamp for the Fortunate: A Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising." ^0-1

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^0-2]]

By Khyenrab Jamyang Lozang Trinley: May the venerable guru, who is the embodiment of the knowledge and love of all the guides of the fortunate aeon without exception, a treasury of the light of good explanations, who causes the grove of the white lotuses of the holy Dharma of scripture and realization to flourish, bestow virtue and excellence. May the unequaled omniscient Śākyasiṃha, who has become the god of gods, the lord of beasts whose ten powers are perfectly developed, who widely bares the fangs and claws of the four fearlessnesses, whose eyes of great love and compassion move, who shakes the turquoise mane of omniscient knowledge, and who loudly roars the melodic roar of emptiness and dependent arising, thereby shattering all at once the cranial bumps of the hundreds of thousands of elephants of the evil speakers who are opponents, bestow all the glory of goodness here today. I bow my head to the great loving Lord of Dharma, the regent of the Conqueror, the invincible guru of the gods, whose golden lotus-like toenails beautifully adorn the tips of the jeweled crowns which are the head-ornaments of hundreds of gods. May the deity who is a single molten mass of the moon beautifully endowed with the major and minor marks in sixteen-year-old youth, the immaculate water-crystal element of the omniscient wisdom of all the omniscient ones, who entirely removes the torments of existence and peace with the nectar rays of completely white enlightened activities, and who opens the hundred petals of the night-lily of intelligence, always protect me with the wheel of steadfastness. May the goddess of the ocean, born from the Brahmā-ocean of the secret speech of all Conquerors, possessing a white smile like a swaying white moon-creeper, who grants all desires through the melodies of poetic speech, beautify the lake of my mind. I bow the crown of my head to the feet of Nāgārjuna and Asaṅga, the ornaments beautifying Jambudvīpa, who illuminate the Sage's teachings, having widely opened the grove of the hundred-petaled lotuses and night-lilies of the two systems with the light of the sun and moon of scripture and reasoning. Victorious is that resting place which is like a jewel crown-ornament upon the crowns of all the extremely learned supreme scholars, the great sun of speech, the all-pervading lord whose melodious roar of sweet fame pervades all three levels, known as the second Conqueror, the lord of accomplishment, Lozang, whose excellent aspirations were excellently accomplished exactly as he had aspired when he promised to spread the extremely profound view in the presence of the Conqueror Indraketu. I bow to the beautiful ornaments of the Yellow Hat teachings: the regent of Dharma Darma Rinchen, the sun of scholar-adepts and speakers Gelek Pal, the emanation of Mañjughoṣa Chökyi Gyaltsen, and so forth. I bow with respect to the venerable gurus who, having held all the pith instructions of the Conquerors' Dharma treasury, dispel the poverty of the mind and bestow the supreme benefit and bliss through the jewels of profound and vast well-spoken advice. How could the flapping of the sparrow's wings of my childish mind reach the far limit of the heavenly path of the meaning of the Mother? Nevertheless, in order to increase the power of the imprints of the excellent path, my mind is inspired to explain it as best as I am able. Furthermore, among the infinite collections of Dharma taught by our Teacher, the great guide of gods and humans, the unequaled omniscient Śākyasiṃha, in accordance with the temperaments, inclinations, and faculties of numerous disciples, the most excellent, supreme, and principal ones are the three—extensive, medium, and concise—sūtras of the perfection of wisdom, which are the great highway that is the sole path traversed by all Conquerors of the three times. And among those, the essence is this Verse Summary of the Precious Qualities. Its stages of emptiness, which are the explicitly taught meaning, are exactly as they were elucidated by the great trailblazer, the protector Nāgārjuna. Its stages of clear realization, which are the hidden meaning, are exactly as they were illuminated by the regent, the invincible protector, in his treatise, the Ornament of Clear Realization. Following how Mañjughoṣa in person, the omniscient Dharma King Tsongkhapa and his spiritual sons, synthesized the sūtras and the treatises, ^0-2

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^0-3]]

and relying on the pith instructions of their excellent explanations, the explanation consists of four parts: the meaning of the title, the translator's homage, the meaning of the main text, and the concluding meaning. ^0-3

## Meaning of the Title ^1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^1-1]]

The meaning of the title. The first has three parts: translating the title, explaining the meaning of the title, and the purpose. ^1-1

### Translation of the Title ^1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^1-2]]

Translating the title. Regarding the first, although there are many ways to explain the term "rgya gar" (India), it is said: "Because the country is vast (rgya che) and its people wear black hats and clothes during mourning and white (dkar) at other times, it is called 'rgya gar'; and because the Chinese (rgya nag) wear the opposite, it is called 'rgya nag'. Thus, although it means 'white' and 'black', the reason it is written as 'gar' rather than 'dkar' is because the neuter prefix 'da' of 'dkar' was elided by the rule of final letters, and the root letter 'ka' was changed to the feminine letter of its own class, producing 'ga'." When giving an explanation by way of etymology, this seems to be reasonable. However, our own tradition asserts that just as it says in the Verses on the Vinaya, "Śrāvastī, Sāketa, Campā, Vārāṇasī, Vaiśālī, and Rājagṛha as the sixth: these are known as the great cities." To the country where these six great cities are located, the term "rgya gar" was designated conventionally. Among the four major language families existing there, in Sanskrit, the well-formed language, the title of this sūtra is Āryaprajñāpāramitāsañcayagāthā. ^1-2

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^1-3]]

Translated into Tibetan, it is "Phags pa shes rab kyi pha rol tu phyin pa sdud pa tshigs su bcad pa." Furthermore, ārya means noble, prajñā means wisdom, pāramitā means perfection, sañcaya means summary, and gāthā means verse. ^1-3

### Explanation of the Meaning of the Title ^1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^1-4]]

Explaining the meaning of the title. Second, the meaning of the title. Because it is exalted above the sūtras of the inferior vehicle and so forth by way of how it teaches its subject matter, it is called "noble." When perfection of wisdom is divided by way of the classes of what is expressed by the term, there are four: textual, path, fruition perfection of wisdom, and natural perfection of wisdom. From among them, the Mother of fruition, the omniscient wisdom, is the actual or principal perfection of wisdom, while the others are secondary. It is called "perfection of wisdom" because it teaches as its principal subject matter the natural perfection of wisdom, which is the object, emptiness, and the path and fruition perfections of wisdom, which are the subject, the non-conceptual wisdom directly realizing suchness; thus, it is named after its subject matter. Because by relying on this text one gathers and holds all the precious qualities of the path and fruition of the Great Vehicle, such as the generation of the mind of awakening, it is called a "summary." Because it is bound by the noble meter of indravajrā with eleven syllables, it is called "verse." ^1-4

### Purpose ^1-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^1-5]]

The purpose. Third, the purpose of stating the Sanskrit language first is to place latencies for the language, to cause blessings to arise in the mind-stream, and so forth. The purpose of stating the Tibetan language is to acknowledge the deeds of the translators and scholars and repay their kindness, and juxtaposing the two languages is for the purpose of easily understanding the distinctions of both languages. ^1-5

## Translator's Homage ^2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^2-1]]

The translator's homage. Second, the translator's homage is: "Homage to noble Mañjuśrī." Because he is exalted above ordinary beings by directly realizing emptiness, he is "noble." Because his mind-stream is free from the rough touch of the two obscurations and possesses the glory of the two accumulations, or because he possesses the glory of the major and minor marks, he is "Mañjuśrī"; to him, one bows with respect through the three doors. Since this sūtra is from the Abhidharma basket which primarily teaches the higher training of wisdom, and since the venerable Mañjughoṣa is the special deity of wisdom, and for the reason that realizing the profound Middle Way view necessarily depends on his enlightened activity, the translators placed this homage in order to identify the basket in accordance with the decree of the early Dharma Kings, as well as to bring the translation to completion and increase merit. ^2-1

## Meaning of the Text ^3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-1]]

The meaning of the text. Third, regarding the explanation of the meaning of the text, just as it is said in the Principles of Explanation: "Those who discourse on the meaning of the sūtras / Should explain it with the purpose, the summarized meaning, / The meaning of the words, the connections, / And the objections and answers." Because it is said there that one must explain it by way of the five branches of the method of explanation, ^3-1

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-2]]

Therefore, there are three [parts]: the purpose, the summarized meaning, and the meaning of the words. ^3-2

### Essential Meaning ^3-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-3]]

The purpose. Furthermore, from among them, first one must state the purpose and connection. When individuals seeking liberation engage in sūtras and treatises by way of the three—hearing, reflecting, and meditating—they must have an exceptional purpose, and that must be something they are capable of accomplishing, and it must be something desired by supreme beings, and the subject matter must be well-connected. It must not be like the treatise examining the teeth of a crow, which has no purpose; or like the treatise on taking the crest-jewel of Takṣaka, which has a purpose but cannot be accomplished; or like the treatise on taking one's mother as a bride, which is not an object to be taken up by supreme beings; or like the speech about ten pomegranates, which is unconnected. The text on taking one's mother as a bride is a certain awareness-mantra text; it is said in the Detailed Explanation of the Perfection of Wisdom that when one accomplishes it, applies it to a substance, and throws it into the fire, a sound of "suitable" arises, and a sound of "this broad thing is not suitable here" arises. The speech about ten pomegranates is this: "Ten pomegranates, six flatbreads, a bowl, / Deerskin, a ball of grass. / Then, this daughter of a liar, / Belongs to me, Spayagrata." Therefore, this precious sūtra possesses the four exceptional qualities, such as purpose. Its explicitly taught subject matter is emptiness, and its implicitly taught subject matter is the eight clear realizations and seventy topics; in short, its subject matter is the completely perfect three trainings to be practiced in the three vehicles. ^3-3

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-4]]

The purpose is that disciples with sharp faculties who delight in brief summaries can easily realize, by means of these summarized words, the previously explained meanings of the noble sūtra of the Perfection of Wisdom in One Hundred Thousand Lines. The ultimate purpose or the purpose of the purpose is the fruition perfection of wisdom, which is similar to all sūtras and treatises of the Great Vehicle. The connection is that the latter are connected with the former. The words "Then the Transcendent Conqueror, in order to make those four retinues thoroughly rejoice..." explicitly teach the first three, such as the purpose, and implicitly teach the connection. Thus, it is said in the Master's Difficult Points Commentary: "Regarding that, it is stated that the connection should be taught first: the accomplishing treatise and...", down to, "Furthermore, it is taught precisely by stating the purpose. Since that which the Teacher taught as 'this is its purpose' is accomplished by this, the connection need not be stated separately. The subject matter is omniscience which contains the eight clear realizations." From "What was taught immediately before..." down to "...is the ultimate purpose. Just as it is said: 'Whatever path to omniscience / The Teacher explained from this...'" and so forth. ^3-4

### Summary ^3-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-5]]

The summarized meaning. Second, the summarized meaning is as follows. Just as it is stated in the Difficult Points Commentary: "In order that the listening individuals may realize the detailed divisions of the meaning of the sūtra without confusion, and in order that the expounder may easily and readily explain it, the summarized meaning will be stated first." If one were to explain the topics taught in the eight chapters—the count of the eight topics and the count of their individual characterizing phenomena, etc.—without first teaching them, one would not know where each of the eight topics begins and ends, and the earlier and later parts would become mixed up. To eliminate this, and in order to realize the boundaries of each topic without confusion, one must first explain by means of a brief indication following a structural outline. Furthermore, if divided by way of the expressing words, there are eight chapters. If divided by way of the expressed meaning, there are the eight topics. If further divided from those, there are seventy topics. Among them, first, from the Ornament: it briefly indicates them from "The perfection of wisdom / Is eight topics" up to "These are the eight"; and it extensively teaches the seventy topics by listing them from "Generation of the mind and instruction" up to "Four are correctly stated." In accordance with that, stating the summarized meaning here as well: Furthermore, since there are the three exalted knowledges as the object to be practiced, the four applications as the means of practice, and the Truth Body as the result to be achieved through practice, disciples who desire to attain Buddhahood must see the benefits of the resultant omniscience and generate a seeking for it, so teaching omniscience first is from: "Having established supreme joy, respect, and faith..." up through "...If one fully knows phenomena to be without nature, / This is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom." Since in order to attain that omniscience one must train in its uncommon cause, the knowledge of paths, the knowledge of paths is from: "Whoever does not abide in form, does not abide in feeling..." up through "...The pure result of omniscience and pure form, / Are like the element of space, indivisible and unseverable." Since to attain the knowledge of paths one must also be preceded by the knowledge of bases, explaining the knowledge of bases after that is from: "Of the heroic perfection of wisdom where one courses..." up through "...Like the sun free from the eclipse of Rāhu, remaining brilliant, / And like setting fire and burning grass, wood, and forests." Since to attain the three exalted knowledges one must be preceded by attaining the four applications, after that the four applications are explained; and in order to gain mastery over the aspects of the three exalted knowledges, explaining the application of complete aspects first is from: "Since all phenomena are by nature pure and completely pure..." up through "...A non-returner does not teach it, the Dharma / Is not explained, that should be known as undeclared." After that, having attained and meditated on the complete aspects, realization goes increasingly higher, and so the culmination or the peak clear realization occurs, so explaining the peak application below that is from: "The grounds of an arhat and the wisdom of a pratyekabuddha..." up through "...If one departs from the vehicles of arhats and solitary realizers and is assured of supreme awakening, / The hero should be known to be always in meditative equipoise." After that, for the purpose of attaining stability in the aspects of the three exalted knowledges, explaining the gradual application which meditates on the aspects of the three exalted knowledges sequentially is by one verse starting from: "A perfectly pure thought for the sake of other sentient beings and persons..." Because by culminating the familiarization of the gradual clear realization one attains the momentary application which attains stability in the aspects of the three exalted knowledges, explaining that itself is by two verses starting from: "Whether abused or even constantly beaten..." Having thus practiced the four applications, the result—the manifest awakening into the Truth Body free from all obscurations—will arise, so below that, teaching the resultant Truth Body is by one verse starting from: "Having given up one's own happiness, with a mind free of hope..." After that, having sought a spiritual friend possessing the characteristics in order for the eight clear realizations to arise in one's mind-stream, teaching the way one must engage in hearing, reflecting, and meditating, such as listening to the Mother, is from: "Whatever bodhisattva desires to cycle for a long time..." up through "...Thinking 'I will endure all the suffering of beings,' / The bodhisattva abides in the perfection of patience." After that, granting instructions exactly as they are to the fortunate person who has properly relied upon a holy spiritual friend is from: "By discipline, those desiring peace become noble..." up through "...In them, roots of virtue will increase with great power, / Like the waxing phase of the cloudless, luminous moon disk." After that, the sequence of practicing the path by one possessing the instructions is from: "By the bodhisattva's generosity, the realm of hungry ghosts is cut off..." up through "...They completely uphold the lineage of the Buddha and the lineage of the Dharma; / Likewise, they completely uphold the lineage of the Sangha and all dharmas." After that, entrusting this Dharma discourse so that it remains for a long time is taught in one verse starting "Healing the illness of beings..." Those constitute the summarized meaning. ^3-5

### Meaning of the Words ^3-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-6]]

Meaning of the words: Third, the extensive explanation of the branches of the meaning of the words has two parts: the introductory setting from which the sūtra arises, and the explanation of the sūtra that arises from it. ^3-6

#### The Setting of the Originating Sutra ^3-3-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-7]]

The introductory setting from which the sūtra arises: First: From "Then the Bhagavān..." up to "...spoke." The meaning: "Then" (atha) is a connective word; immediately after having finished teaching the One Hundred Thousand [Stanza] Mother and so forth, then again having vanquished the four māras without exception, as it is said: "Endowed with the perfections of sovereignty, fine form, glory, renown, wisdom, and joyous effort—these six are called 'qualities' (bhaga)." By that very Bhagavān [endowed with the six great qualities], the Sugata, "those fourfold retinues, the monks and nuns, laymen and laywomen"—by these specifying words, it teaches that eunuchs and so forth, because of their great afflictions, are not the retinue, and because the monastic community of monks and so forth are the permanent or stable retinue they are explicitly mentioned, while the bodhisattvas, being an unstable retinue, are not mentioned. In order that those fortunate retinues might easily realize the great meaning, the meaning of the Mother, with little difficulty, and thereby perfectly and fully rejoice by way of generating all the qualities of the Mahāyāna paths and fruitions within their own mind-streams, once again he teaches this profound perfection of wisdom, a text whose expressed subject matter is the perfection of wisdom of the path and fruition, and at that time when the retinue suitable to be taught the meaning in summary was assembled, he "spoke" these expressing verses that appear below, beginning with "With joy and respect..." To explain the manner of the introductory setting slightly more extensively: In our own monastic textbooks it is said that the extensive, middling, and short Mothers were taught sequentially, and the Verse Summary (Ratnaguṇasañcaya) sūtra is said to be the eighty-fourth of the eighty-seven chapters in the Eighteen Thousand [Stanza]. Butön Rinpoche stated: "The extensive is the One Hundred Thousand. The extensive of the middling is the Twenty-Five Thousand. The middling of the middling is the Eighteen Thousand. The short of the middling is the Ten Thousand. The extensive of the short is the Eight Thousand. The short of the short is the Precious Qualities Collection (Ratnaguṇasañcaya). Among those, the first five were taught simultaneously, because the questioners all appear in agreement, and they are identical in their prophecies of the Goddess of the Ganges in the chapter on pure fields. There is a pervasion, because it is unsuitable for a single Buddha to prophesy a single person to awakening again and again. As for the Verse Summary, it appears in the commentary that it was taught afterwards in the Magadhi language to delight the fourfold retinue." The Great Geshe of Lithang and others said: "These extensive, middling, and short sūtras appear as distinct due to the Buddha's enlightened activities and the dispositions of the trainees, but they are not distinct sūtras, as they were taught in a single speech"; they did not elaborate on the reasons. If one asks which of these discordant ways of explaining is acceptable: As is taught in the Sūtra of Inconceivable Secrets and the Chapter of the Truth-Speaker: "Thus, when many ask questions simultaneously in the certain words of all beings, He comprehends them in a single instant of mind, and with a single melodious voice He gives answers to each individually," and so forth; accordingly, I think it is unsuitable to categorically ascertain the inconceivable secret of the Buddha's speech, which transcends the domain of ordinary beings' minds. Therefore, the master Haribhadra explained the introductory setting by following the extensive and middling sūtras, and it is said that this is also the intention of master Dignāga; thus, apart from whether it is expressed extensively or not in words, all the introductory settings are the same, and that too is taught by "Thus have I heard at one time..." and so forth. Master Bhāviveka and others assert that the compiler of this Verse Summary sūtra was Vajrapāṇi, while master Haribhadra, relying on the Entrustment chapter and so forth, asserts that it was the noble Ānanda, who adopted the manner of a śrāvaka. With this phrase "Thus", the entirety of the meaning from the beginning to the end of the sūtra is held; this is the same crucial point as what is explained in the tantras: that the eighty-four thousand heaps of dharma are included within evaṃ. "At one time" indicates: "at one time I heard this; at other times I heard other things," thereby teaching that he himself possessed great learning; and "I heard this sūtra at one time, not at other times," teaching that the Dharma is rare; and "even though I heard it in a single instant of time, I have not forgotten it," thereby demonstrating him to be an inconceivable, authoritative person. Furthermore, since the meaning of the sūtra without addition or omission is exactly this, the teaching word "Thus" indicates the inner condition of wisdom; the reflexive teaching word "by me" demonstrates that he himself heard it directly from the presence of the Bhagavān, thereby indicating the outer condition of possessing the pith instructions of the prior master; and the word "heard" indicates "it was merely heard, I have not realized the meaning," thereby applying it to humbleness—one should know that there are many such profound key points. In short, Ānanda demonstrated himself to be an authoritative person so as to generate confidence in trainees of future generations; for the sake of them respectfully hearing, retaining, and so forth this sūtra, he taught it as endowed with the five perfections: "at the perfect time, in the perfect place, from the perfect teacher, together with the perfect retinue, I heard this perfect Dharma, this sūtra." Accordingly, the perfect place was Rājagṛha, at Vulture Peak. The purpose of stating both of these is to gather both groups, householders and renunciates, thereby showing it to be sublimely superior to both places associated with affliction and places associated with complete purification. As for "Rājagṛha" (House of the King), when they were unable to abide in what was previously renowned as Kuśinagara because non-humans repeatedly set it on fire, the king decreed: "From now on, whoever's house catches fire must build their house and live in the Śītavana charnel ground." By the force of karma, the king's palace caught fire first. Then the king went there and took up residence, whereby the name Rājagṛha came about—so says the scholar Ngawang Tendar, based on the explanation in the Śālistamba Sūtra. As for naming it Vulture Peak Mountain: either the mountain's peak is shaped like a vulture; or it is shaped like a heap of many vultures; or because many vultures ate corpses there, it was named so in terms of guarding that mountain; or scholars say it was so named because when the perfection of wisdom was taught, tenth-bhūmi bodhisattvas arrived in the form of vultures. Not only did most ordinary people of that time fail to see the miraculous arrays during the teaching of the perfection of wisdom sūtra exactly as they were, but the major marks and so forth appeared in various ways due to the pure or impure karma of the trainees. By inferring from the fact that this mountain also appeared to be filled with many vultures and so forth, one should generate the firm conviction that even though the spiritual friend teaching Dharma to oneself is a supreme nirmāṇakāya or the like, due to one's own impure perception they appear as an ordinary person, whereas in reality they are an actual Buddha; one should become skilled in the key point of practice by applying this to the root of the path, the manner of relying on a spiritual friend, and so forth. The perfect time: The three Oceans of the Phugpa tradition assert that he taught the perfection of wisdom sūtra on the full moon of the Caitra month and passed into nirvāṇa on the fifteenth of the following month. Although there are many ways of asserting this by Chim, Chag Lotsawa, and others, our own system is that it was that special time of the fifteenth of the Caitra month in the year following the Teacher's enlightenment. The perfect teacher: The supreme omniscient King of Śākyas, the agent who emanates and withdraws all the Jinas. The perfect retinue: Although there were limitless monks and bodhisattvas, here it mentions only the summary of the ordinary retinue who constantly remained with him: "together with"—that is, in the company of—"a great monastic community of twelve hundred and fifty monks." As for "monastic community": As it is said in a sūtra: "Because the Buddha, Dharma, and Saṅgha cannot be split apart even by a hundred ten-million māras, therefore they are called monastic community." Expressing their qualities: Because they have destroyed the gross four māras, they are "arhats"; Because they have exhausted the three defilements of desire, ignorance, and existence, they are "those whose defilements are exhausted"; Because they are without the three thorough afflictions of karma, afflictions, and birth, they are "without afflictions"; Because they have abandoned misdeeds through the heap of ethical discipline, they are "those who have attained mastery over their conduct"; Because they have abandoned craving, bad views, and the bonds of existence through the three heaps of concentration, wisdom, and liberation respectively, they are "those who have attained mastery over the world and their own minds"; Because they have abandoned the obscurations of meditative absorption, they are "those whose minds are completely liberated"; Because they have abandoned the afflictive obscurations, they are "those whose wisdom is completely liberated"; "Thoroughbreds" means fearlessness; Thoroughbred horses and elephants are fearless because they know whether or not there are dangers on the paths they travel, places they stay, etc.; but in this context, because they have abandoned suffering, they have no fear of sorrow and the like; they lack the seven fears such as self-reproach. The seven fears are: "Self-reproach, reproach by others, lack of livelihood, fear of punishment, lack of verses, and fear of bad migrations and death." Because they have abandoned worldly dharmas, they have no fear of the eight worldly dharmas. Because they have abandoned doubt regarding their own realizations, they have no fear of non-realization, wrong realization, or doubt; being free from the four fears is the meaning of being a thoroughbred arhat. Generally, five thoroughbreds are also explained: having no fear of falling thunderbolts and so forth is the thoroughbred supreme bull; having no fear of battle are the two thoroughbreds, horse and elephant; having no fear because of lacking harm from others is the thoroughbred lion; having no fear of death are the thoroughbred arhats. Because they are foremost in possessing realization of special dharmas such as the three knowledges, they are "great elephants"; They have accomplished the goal of asceticism, attaining liberation; Because they have done what is appropriate to do, such as the qualities of purification, they are "those who have done what had to be done"; Because they have cast off the four burdens—the burden of the aggregates, the burden of afflictions, the burden of pledges, and the burden of effort—they are "those who have cast off their burdens"; Because they have attained their own goal, such as the qualities of abandonment and realization, by following the Teacher; Because they have fully exhausted the nine fetters that continuously bind persons to existence; Because they perfectly and unmistakably know the abiding nature of the four truths directly through the paths of seeing and meditation, afflictions do not arise again in their minds, thus they are "completely liberated"; They have attained mastery over all mental states, such as the nine meditative absorptions, and have all entirely reached the perfection of the supreme families. Among those śrāvakas, one who is filled with the latencies of virtuous and non-virtuous karma and takes birth in accordance with them is [subject to] defilements. If one asks who is the single person excluded from possessing those qualities, who does not possess them: it is said to be the elder Ānanda, who possesses a life or lifespan dear to all, who is learned in all scriptures, and who has attained the joy of venerating the Buddha. The perfect Dharma: It is this very precious sūtra of the perfection of wisdom, which teaches all the stages of practice of the paths of the three vehicles without omission within the context of the causal perfections. As for the manner in which it was taught: By means of the three miracles—the miracle of magical abilities, the miracle of declaring all, and the miracle of subsequent instruction—or by means of the inconceivable enlightened activities of body, speech, and mind, he gathered those trainees who had not been gathered into his retinue, ripened those who were unripened, and liberated those who were ripened, in order to establish them all in the supreme fruition. For this, there are the explanations of the miracle of the mind's concentration, the miracle of the body's magical abilities, and the miracle of speech that illuminates the Dharma. Among those, the first has two parts: First, regarding teaching the miracle of the king of concentrations: Initially, the Teacher expressed his gratitude to the perfection of wisdom sūtra, dispelled the mental unhappiness of future follower trainees who, when teaching, listening to the Dharma, and meditating on the stages of the path, lack veneration from a retinue and lack livelihood, and in order for them to rely on having few desires and being content, the Teacher arranged the Dharma seat himself. In order to convey that one must realize the meaning of this sūtra by resting in meditative equipoise, he sat squarely and straight in the vajra posture. This signifies the way of correcting one's motivation during the preparatory practices, the seven dharmas of Vairocana for the body, and so forth. Making the mudrā of turning the wheel of Dharma, he directed his gaze to the tip of his nose, and entered into the meditative equipoise of the king of concentrations that subsumes all concentrations. Then, arising from that, he looked upon migrating beings with eyes of love and compassion, and, in order to convey that "the Buddha's teaching of the Dharma appears as various vehicles in accordance with the minds of the trainees," he emanated sixty hundred-thousand nayutas of ten-million light rays and natural light from the parts of his body, thereby illuminating all world systems and establishing sentient beings in unsurpassed awakening. Then, as a harbinger of teaching the Mother, he extended his tongue to cover the three-thousandfold world system, and from it he emanated a limitless host of light rays upon which rested many bodies of Tathāgatas, teaching the Dharma as a preliminary, and thereby establishing sentient beings in unsurpassed awakening as before. These constitute the first miracle of concentration. ^3-7

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-8]]

Second, regarding the teaching of the miracle of the lion's play: this is in order to make it understood that, following this, the Mother should not be taught in places where the Dharma teachers are impure or to Dharma listeners who lack the wisdom that knows the two truths. Thus, having entered into the meditative absorption called the "lion's yawn," which is free from the fears and timidity of a retinue, he caused the eighteen great miraculous signs such as the shaking of the earth to precede it. By clearing away all the unfree states of the world systems and purifying the environment, the sentient beings residing there were born in happy destinies and remembered their past lives; utterly joyful, each of them bowed down and paid homage to the Buddha. The incomplete faculties of sentient beings became complete, and by abiding in the ten virtues, they rejoiced through the dhyānas and immeasurables, making their minds workable. By purposely proclaiming, "Giving, taming, pacifying, perfect restraint, pure conduct, and non-violence toward living beings are excellent," they became endowed with the suitable wisdom of a vessel to listen to the Mother. This is the second miracle of meditative absorption. ^3-8

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-9]]

Second, from the two parts explaining the physical miracles of supernatural power, regarding the first, the teaching of the miracle of emanation: subsequent to that, in order to generate reverence in the disciples by conveying that a Teacher like this who has discovered omniscient wisdom is capable of teaching this sutra, just as before, he was beautified by the light rays of his body. His body color glowed like refined gold, he was radiant with brilliance, and he clearly manifested by outshining all the worlds with his splendor, just as Mount Meru outshines the lesser mountains. This is the first miracle of supernatural power. ^3-9

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-10]]

Second, regarding the teaching of the miracle of blessing: furthermore, clearing away the discouragement of thinking, "Only a Teacher like this can realize the meaning of the sutra, while others cannot," and in order to generate the enthusiasm that "those who are human are capable of accomplishing it," he displayed an ordinary body, matching the physical form and manner of the humans of this world. At that time, the disciples were exceedingly joyful, and those who had previously seen the Tathāgatas teach the Mother and were familiar with it made offerings of flowers and so forth to the Bhagavān. The Teacher enjoyed what they had offered, and they too rejoiced, multiplying their virtues further through their sympathetic joy. Also, in order that the surrounding retinue, having attained extreme physical pliancy due to their experience of bliss, might increase their collection of wisdom, through his blessing those flowers and so forth transformed above his head into a multistoried palace equal in size to the three-thousandfold world system, beautified by various kinds of ornaments. In order to dispel the thought that he was not impartial toward other disciples, he made himself appear to be seated and teaching the Dharma right in front of each individual being; this is the second miracle of supernatural power. These are the two deeds of clearing away pride and discouragement. One should understand that these teach many things, such as the spreading out of the seats, the unwavering offerings, and the exhortation to turn the wheel of Dharma. ^3-10

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-11]]

Third, from the two parts on the miracle of the illumination of the Dharma of speech, regarding the first, the teaching of the miracle that reveals the world systems: then, in order to show that although the Teacher himself was born in an impure field he is equal to the other Jinas, and in order to teach the method for completing the accumulations to his own disciples who were born in other fields, he smiled. By doing so, the beings of this field saw the Buddhas along with their retinues in the world systems of the ten directions, and the fortunate disciples of other fields saw the Teacher and his retinue in this Sahā world system. This is the first miracle of the illumination of the world systems. ^3-11

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-12]]

Second, regarding the teaching of the miracle of gathering the retinue: then, through the previous signs, he exhorted the bodhisattvas of the world systems of the ten directions, such as the Bodhisattva Samantaraśmi, the principal of the retinue of the Tathāgata Ratnākara in the eastern world system Ratnavatī, and the Bodhisattva Aśoka, the principal of the retinue of the Tathāgata Aśokaśrī in the southern world system Aśokā. In order to make it understood that it is improper to listen to the Dharma from others without having asked one's own guru, those bodhisattvas paid homage to their respective Jinas and asked for the reason. Also, having heard those Jinas speak the praises of the Teacher, they requested permission to go to this field and obtained it accordingly. In order for those Jinas to make offerings to this Teacher, they bestowed upon them gifts of lotuses made of various jewels, words inquiring about his health, and instructions saying, "Be heedful in that impure field." Having received these, they worshipped the Jinas along the way, arrived in this field, and with paying homage to the Teacher preceding it, they blessed the collections of offerings. Having taken their respective seats, they offered the earlier words inquiring about his health and the lotuses; this is the second miracle of illumination. Then, when the Teacher scattered those lotuses in their respective directions in order to make offerings to the sublime Dharma, emanations sat upon them, and having realized that the disciples endowed with the fortune to be taught the Mother had gathered, he spoke this precious sutra of the perfection of wisdom that is virtuous in the beginning, middle, and end. Because I have not seen the introductory setting explained extensively in the manuals of our own tradition, with the thought of hoping to benefit slightly myself and others of equal fortune, I have explained it by gathering from various texts and slightly elaborating. ^3-12

#### Explanation of the Actual Sutra ^3-3-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-13]]

Explanation of the actual sutra: Second, regarding the explanation of the actual sutra, there are three parts: the three knowledges which are the objects of knowledge, the four applications which are the practice, and the explanation of the dharmakāya which is the result. ^3-13

##### The Three Knowledges to be Known ^3-3-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-14]]

The three knowledges which are the objects of knowledge: Regarding the first, there are three: omniscient knowledge, knowledge of the path, and knowledge of the basis. ^3-14

###### Knowledge of All Aspects ^3-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-15]]

Omniscient knowledge: Regarding the first, the wisdom that has perfected the direct realization of the ten dharmas such as the generation of the mind is the definition or essence of omniscient knowledge. Regarding the dharmas that characterize it, just as it is taught in the Ornament from "The generation of the mind and the instructions..." up to "...omniscient knowledge itself," there are ten: the intention which is the generation of the mind; the instruction which is the specific feature of its increasing further; the branches of definite discrimination which are the specific features of application; the naturally abiding disposition which is the basis of practice; the objects of practice; the purpose of practice; and the essence of practice itself: the practice of armor, the practice of engagement, the practice of accumulation, and the practice of definite emergence. From among these ten, as for the initial teaching of the generation of the mind here: for example, just as a painter of deities strikes a plumb line so that faults such as crookedness do not occur in the deity's body, this is in order to know that whatever dharmic activity is done, so that it does not go astray but becomes solely the cause for Buddhahood, one must initially clearly invoke at least an artificial generation of the mind as a motivation. This is according to what Mañjughoṣa Gungtang stated, "This is an oral instruction I heard from Yongdzin Rinpoche." Thus, the generation of the mind. ^3-15

###### **Generation of the Mind of Awakening** ^3-3-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-16]]

Regarding the first, the teaching on the generation of the mind, there are two parts: the actual teaching and the incidental. ^3-16

###### **Actual** ^3-3-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-17]]

Actual: The first is taught by a single verse starting with "With joy and reverence..." Even though Buddhaśrī explains that the first two lines exhort suitable vessels to listen, and the remaining two lines teach the generation of the mind, the master's assertion is like the former. ^3-17

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-18]]

As for the meaning of the words: 'Joy' is the faith of clear confidence in the Mahāyāna path along with its result; 'and reverence' is the faith of longing that desires to attain unsurpassed awakening; and having established the supreme faith of conviction that is certain of the causal relationship—that unsurpassed awakening will also be attained from practicing the perfection of wisdom—you should listen. Because faith is like the mother that generates and precedes all wholesome qualities, having cleared away the obscurations such as grasping at true existence, which act as hindrances to the Mahāyāna generation of the mind and the rest of the path arising in one's continuum, and the afflictions such as the three poisons, because they cause the person possessing them in their continuum to suffer, you have passed beyond the stains that are to be overcome by their antidotes. You great beings whose continuums are 'gentle'—made gentle, or peaceful and certain, through the path whose essence is emptiness and compassion, who are the sublime beings truly engaged in accomplishing the purpose of all beings without exception out of great compassion (the temporary higher rebirths and the ultimate definite goodness, unsurpassed awakening)—'listen to' that perfection of wisdom which is the path subsumed by the generation of the mind, the Mahāyāna path that is supremely exalted above the path of the śrāvakas, and which has become the domain of practice of the 'heroes', the bodhisattvas, in meditative equipoise and post-meditation. Thus it means. Regarding that, as it is stated in the Ornament of Clear Realization: 'The generation of the mind is the desire for perfectly complete awakening for the sake of others' welfare.' In accordance with this, that distinctive primary Mahāyāna mental cognition, which arises in association with the intention focused on perfectly complete awakening for the welfare of others that acts as its companion, and which is differentiated as abiding in the class of the path that constitutes the entrance door to the Mahāyāna path, is the definition of the Mahāyāna conventional generation of the mind. When divided by way of its essence, there are two: aspiring and engaging. When divided by way of the manner of generating the mind, there are three: the generation of the mind like a king, and so forth. When divided by way of its boundaries, there are four: the generation of the mind of behavior of resolute engagement, and so forth. When divided by way of concordant analogies, there are twenty-two, from the generation of the mind like earth up to the generation of the mind like a cloud. As for how these are taught by this sutra: the first phrase teaches how to rely on a spiritual friend and the paths common to the persons of small and medium capacity, and so forth, as the preliminaries to the generation of the mind. The second teaches the intention seeking awakening, and 'truly engaged in the welfare of beings' teaches that the desire for awakening is for the sake of others. 'Heroes' teaches the support, the bodhisattvas, and 'practice' teaches the manner of training on the path. In the Aṣṭasāhasrikā, this statement teaches the generation of the mind: 'Subhūti, taking the perfection of wisdom as your starting point, generate eloquence so that the bodhisattva great beings will come to definitely emerge in the perfection of wisdom.' Since these words of the sutra teach a complete progression of the Mahāyāna path—by which supporting person, for what purpose, toward what object, and in what manner one trains—the gurus have stated that the entire meaning of the sutras on the perfection of wisdom is subsumed within this, that the entire Mahāyāna path is subsumed within the generation of the mind, and that it is like the root and summary of the Mahāyāna path. Also, the Commentary on Difficult Points states: 'Therefore, the generation of the mind, being illusion-like in nature, subsumes the knowledge of all aspects and the eight clear realizations; thus it is ascertained from scripture.' Also from the Gaṇḍavyūhasūtra: 'Son of noble family, the mind of awakening is the root of all the qualities of a Buddha.' And: 'Because it subsumes all the practices and aspirations of bodhisattvas, it is like a summary.' Furthermore, regarding how the path is subsumed within the generation of the mind: having generated the mind toward supreme awakening, it is not enough to merely focus on the two objects of focus—awakening and the welfare of others; one must know the methods to accomplish it. Moreover, having pleased a Mahāyāna spiritual friend through both thought and action, and having listened to the profound and vast instructions without exception, one encompasses the entire Mahāyāna path within the focal object of the generation of the mind. Having made that very generation of the mind the foundational support and the core axis of the Mahāyāna path, one practices by uniting method and wisdom, making the six perfections such as generosity and the four means of gathering disciples and so forth its branches or parts. Thereby, the mind of awakening along with its branches increases and expands higher and higher, and by gradually traversing the ten bhūmis and the five paths, one becomes a Buddha. By using as a seed this understanding of how the generation of the mind is taught as the summary of the Mahāyāna path, one should rely on a learned spiritual friend and study the scriptures in detail, thereby gaining an unmistakable understanding. Furthermore, not leaving it merely as an object of intellectual understanding, as the Lord Guru stated: 'The generation of the mind is the core axis of the Mahāyāna path; it is the basis and support of all great waves of conduct; it is like an alchemical elixir for all the two accumulations; it is a treasury of merit that gathers infinite collections of virtue. Knowing it to be thus, the heroic offspring of the Jinas hold this supreme precious mind as the core of their practice. I, a yogi, have also practiced in this way; you who desire liberation should also cultivate it thus.' In accordance with this statement, by combining the sutras and the stages of the path, one should hold the precious mind of awakening as the core of one's practice and spontaneously accomplish the two purposes of oneself and others. ^3-18

###### **Ancillary** ^3-3-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-19]]

Incidental topic. Secondly, demonstrating through an example the incidental point that the śrāvakas' teaching of the perfection of wisdom is due to the power of the Buddha: The meaning taught by the three verses beginning with 'Here in Jambudvīpa' is as follows. By way of example, it is called Jambu because it is located to the south of Mount Meru and when the fruit of the Jambu tree falls into the lake it makes the sound 'jambu'; and it is called Jambudvīpa because it is a continent surrounded by water at its borders. Since its shape resembles a chariot, three of its sides are two thousand yojanas each, and one is three and a half yojanas, as the Abhidharmakośa states: 'Jambudvīpa has three sides / of two thousand, the shape of a chariot; / one has three and a half yojanas.' Here in this central southern continent, however many rivers flow, such as the Gaṅgā, the Pakṣu, the Sindhu, and the Sītā, and whatever flowers there are such as lotuses and kumudas, and whatever fruit-bearing trees there are such as mango trees, and whatever medicines and forests are produced and flourish—they all rely and depend upon that great lord, whoever is the chief or lord of the nāgas dwelling in the great lake Anavatapta—Anavatapta meaning that which overcomes heat because it is filled with water possessing the eight qualities—and upon the nāga Vāsuki and so forth. All of that arises from the power and majesty of that great lord of the nāgas, who is preeminent among those nāgas, who is free from the fears of hot sand and so forth, and who has attained independence. ^3-19

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-20]]

Likewise, similar to the example, whenever the Jina's śrāvakas, Subhūti and so forth, teach whatever dharmas there are—such as the twelve branches of scripture—saying 'This is the meaning of this' in order to generate the wisdom arisen from hearing through the three valid cognitions and authentic reasoning; and when they explain the words and meanings extensively in order to generate the wisdom arisen from reflection; and when they express them verbally along with the four categories of reasoning—such as the reasoning of performing a function—and the methods for clearing away hindrances and enhancing practice, in order to generate the wisdom arisen from meditation; and when the ultimate fruition of teaching those dharmas brings about the attainment of the supremely noble bliss of a Buddha, non-abiding nirvāṇa; and furthermore, when the temporary fruition of teaching those dharmas brings about the attainment of the four fruitions of stream-enterer, once-returner, non-returner, and śrāvaka arhat: all of these arise from the power—that is, the blessing—of the person of the Tathāgata, who has realized dharmatā, suchness, and teaches it just as he has seen it. ^3-20

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-21]]

If one asks, for what reason are all those the power of the Buddha? As an answer: Since [the Buddha] has been victorious over all four māras without exception, whatever dharma system of the selflessness of persons and phenomena is taught, Subhūti and the others who have become disciples of the supreme human, the Buddha Bhagavat, actualize their training in it through the stages of hearing, reflecting, and meditating, and having realized it directly, they also teach it to other trainees exactly as they themselves have trained in it. They do all of that through the power of the Buddha, and not through the power of the śrāvakas' own strength. Furthermore, having come to know the inconceivable enlightened activities and qualities—such as how, by the power of the Buddha's blessing, the sound of dharma emerges even from trees swaying in the wind and from the sky, thereby establishing trainees in ripening and liberation—one should take refuge from the depths of one's heart. Regarding how this text serves as an incidental topic of the generation of the awakening mind (bodhicitta): Tri Gungtangpa states, 'Śrāvakas [are born from] the Buddha, and he too is born from the generation of the awakening mind, which is like the summary of the perfection of wisdom; therefore, wishing to become a Buddha who independently teaches the perfection of wisdom, one should train in it.' Thus, it is established from the perspective of the result. ^3-21

###### **Instruction** ^3-3-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-22]]

Instructions. Secondly, regarding instructions: The Mahāyāna utterance that teaches the methods for attaining the objective sought for the sake of the Mahāyāna generation of the awakening mind is the definition of a Mahāyāna instruction. Regarding that, if divided by way of essence, there are two: instructions given so that qualities already attained do not degenerate, and ensuing instructions given so that qualities not yet attained may be attained. If divided by way of the expressed meaning, as stated in the Ornament: 'Practice and the truths / ... / should be known,' there are ten instructions: instructions given on the essence of practice itself, on the four truths as objects of observation, on the Three Jewels as the basis of practice, on the three types of diligence as the companions of practice, on the five eyes that make practice independent of others, on the six superknowledges as the causes for fully perfecting the practice, on the path of seeing that newly sees the truths, and on the path of meditation that habituates what has already been seen—making ten instructions. From among these, the principal instruction—the instruction given so that practice does not transgress the stages of the two truths in its own essence—is taught by one verse beginning with 'For whomever wisdom'. The meaning is as follows: For whatever bodhisattva who is thus instructed, regarding the object of instruction: when one searches for the designated meaning of the conventional designation of the supreme perfection of wisdom—which realizes the selflessness of phenomena and persons with respect to the six perfections, giving and so forth, which are the objects to be practiced—and analyzes it, one does not observe it as established in its own essence or established by its own nature, and, through realizing this, it abides merely as a designation. Furthermore, if one searches with reasoning for whether the practitioner, the bodhisattva, is established as identical to or different from the aggregates that are their basis of designation, and whether they are established by their own nature, one does not find the essence of the person in the slightest, and not observing it, they are like an illusion merely posited by names and symbols. The essence of the awakening mind, which is to be practiced, is also not observed in the slightest as truly established. ^3-22

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-23]]

Having heard this speech that teaches that the agent, the object, and the action are not truly existent, the bodhisattva who—while there is no ultimate existence of the bodhisattva and so forth—is unconfused about practicing the perfection of wisdom merely conventionally, and who is 'not frightened'—meaning without the slightest fear—at the teaching that they are empty of inherent nature or true establishment, but practices the two truths in union; that bodhisattva possesses the fortune to practice the profound path of the perfection of wisdom taught by the Sugata. Thus it is instructed that when practicing Mahāyāna practice, one does not transgress the practice of the union of the two truths: the stages of the path based on conventional truth, which is to practice the practitioner, the objects to be practiced, and the results in an illusion-like manner conventionally; and the stages of the path based on ultimate truth, which is to practice having realized those to be empty of true existence. As for the instruction on practice, having explicitly taught the basis of qualities or the general isolate, the others—being its qualities or particulars—are implicitly indicated. Likewise, as stated in the Sixty Stanzas on Reasoning: 'By this virtue, may all beings / accumulate the provisions of merit and wisdom, / and attain the two supreme kāyas / arising from merit and wisdom.' Accordingly, one should know that the supreme vital point of practice is to practice without transgressing the union of the two truths as the basis, the union of method and wisdom as the path, and the union of the two kāyas as the result. ^3-23

###### **Path of Preparation** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-24]]

Path of preparation. Thirdly, regarding the explanation of the path of preparation, which is the beginning of what arises from meditation on the meaning of the instruction: the definition of the Mahāyāna path of preparation is that Mahāyāna mundane path that accords with the clear realization of the truths, which abides in the class of clear realizations that arise after the completion of its cause, the Mahāyāna aids to liberation. Such a Mahāyāna path of preparation is exceptionally superior to the Hīnayāna path of preparation by way of five or six qualities. This is because it is exceptionally superior by way of its object of observation, its aspect, its manner of acting as a cause, its manner of being completely held, its manner of possessing the conceptualization of true apprehension as the principal object to be abandoned, and its division into small, medium, and great, as stated in the Ornament: 'Object of observation and aspect,' and so forth. Regarding it, by way of its essence, there are four—heat and so forth; and by way of occasions, there are twelve, as each has three—small, medium, and great. In the Eight Thousand Line and the Twenty Thousand Line and so forth, there is a sūtra section on the objects of observation, aspects, and causes of preparation for each of all [the stages of] the paths of preparation, whereas here, since there is only one sūtra section for each of the four—heat, peak, patience, and supreme phenomenon: ^3-24

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-25]]

[There are] three [parts]: the distinction of the three—essence, object of observation and aspect, and cause; the distinction of abandonment and realization associated with the result; and the distinction of the spiritual friend who completely holds [one]. ^3-25

###### **Distinctions of the Three: Essence, Object and Aspect, and Cause** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-26]]

The distinction of the three—essence, object of observation and aspect, and cause. Regarding the first, from among the four: heat, peak, patience, and supreme phenomenon: ^3-26

###### **Object of Heat** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-27]]

The object of observation of heat. The object of observation of heat is taught by [the verse] from 'There is no form, no feeling,' up to 'consciousness.' The meaning is as follows: Here, the five faculties, five objects, and so forth, subsumed by the aggregate of form, are a collection and accumulation of many particles, and so they possess many parts. Furthermore, if one analyzes with the reasoning that analyzes the ultimate—asking whether they are established as identical to or different from their parts by their own nature—they are not established by their own essence in the slightest. And because the parts, the subtle particles, also possess directional parts, if one analyzes them as before, they are not truly established in the slightest; therefore, although this form appears, it does not truly exist. Likewise, regarding these feelings of pleasure, pain, and neutrality subsumed by one's own and others' continuums, if one analyzes them with the reasoning of neither one nor many, they are not truly established. And similarly, the aggregate of discrimination, and the aggregates of compositional factors such as the mental factor of intention that makes the mind move toward objects, do not truly exist either. Towards such a manner, consciousness or true establishment does not exist in the slightest. This teaches that the five aggregates, form and so forth, which are the principal aspects of the truth of suffering, are the object of observation of the wisdom of heat. As for the aspect, it is taught by 'if abides even as a mere particle': this means that abiding truly in that—that is, true clinging—does not exist even as a mere particle. By this, it is taught that this wisdom of heat that is non-conceptual regarding true existence is the aspect. ^3-27

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-28]]

The distinction of the cause is taught by the two lines, 'He does not abide in all phenomena' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: In that way, these phenomena, the five aggregates and so forth, are established conventionally, pleasant when unexamined; but when examined and analyzed, a special ascertainment arises seeing them as like dreams and illusions, without even a mere particle of establishment by their own essence. At that time, that bodhisattva who abides on the Mahāyāna paths of preparation—heat and so forth—practices, that is, experiences, the perfection of wisdom without abiding in all phenomena from form up to omniscience in the manner of true clinging, and without abiding in any extreme of permanence or annihilation. Thus, by relying on the very expansion and increase, higher and higher, of that wisdom realizing the emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects, without the meditatively poised wisdom that is single-pointedly poised in meditative equipoise on reality completely holding any conventional phenomena, form and so forth, the great unexcelled awakening in the mental continuums of the Sugatas will be attained. ^3-28

###### **Object of Peak** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-29]]

The object of observation of peak. The object of observation of peak is taught by one stanza, 'Just as' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: For example, just as the wandering ascetic Śreṇika, or the one known as the Garland-bearer, knowing through the power of his own wisdom that he could not attain liberation freeing him from all the sufferings of saṃsāra by relying merely on the view of his own tradition asserting the existence of a personal self and the meditative absorptions of the actual states of the concentrations and the formless [realms] he had attained, came into the presence of the teacher Buddha and asked what the path for attaining liberation was; and when the teacher taught the subtle selflessness of persons and phenomena, he had exceptional inclination and belief in it, and by relying on the path of the perfection of wisdom that realizes the absence of even the slightest focal target of true apprehension, there arose a nirvāṇa in which the appropriated aggregates propelled by karma and afflictions are completely destroyed, or the continuum of cycling again in saṃsāra is severed—likewise, any bodhisattva who thus knows all phenomena, form and so forth, to not truly exist, because he is embraced by special methods such as great compassion, does not touch the extreme of peace, the nirvāṇa without remainder of aggregates, and he will abide in the wisdom that realizes existence and peace as equal in being empty of true existence. This teaches that form and so forth, devoid of marks, are the object of observation. ^3-29

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-30]]

As for the aspect, it is taught by one stanza, 'Moreover this wisdom' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: Moreover, if one examines and analyzes, thinking thus: 'As to this sole path for traveling to liberation called the wisdom that realizes all phenomena to not exist by their own nature, what is it—whether it is the aggregates of form and so forth, or a substance, or a quality, or an action, or a universal, or a particular, or an inherence? Whose, which person's, is it? From which of the four extremes is it produced?'—it is none of these, because it is ultimately unproduced. By this very reasoning, it examines and analyzes all these phenomena from form up to omniscience as being empty of their own essence. Thus, that bodhisattva who, when closely investigating with authentic reasonings such as [the reasoning of] neither one nor many and the diamond slivers and so forth, sees them as empty of nature, and is undaunted and unafraid of the meaning of the abiding nature, is near to perfect awakening. This teaches the distinction of the cause; and that seeing phenomena as lacking nature when thoroughly analyzed is the aspect. ^3-30

###### **Object of Patience** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-1-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-31]]

The object of observation of patience. The object of observation of patience is taught by one stanza, 'If one does not know' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: If, while not knowing the meaning of all phenomena, form and so forth, being empty of nature from the very beginning, one manifestly conceptualizes form as form established by its own essence, and likewise practices with the perception of true clinging to feeling, and volition, that is, the aggregate of perception and the aggregate of consciousness; not only is one practicing on marks, but even if, when single-pointedly poised in meditative equipoise on emptiness, one conceptualizes, 'This aggregate of form is empty of nature', one is practicing on volitions that manifestly condition, and because the appearance of the five aggregates is not blocked, even if one meditates in that way, such a bodhisattva is practicing on marks, but has not attained the faith of believing in the state of being unproduced by nature, and does not abide in the genuine perfection of wisdom. This teaches that form and so forth, distinguished by being ultimately without essence, are the object of observation. Some think of the meaning of this sūtra and assert that the emptiness that is merely a non-affirming negation negating the object of negation, true establishment, is not the ultimate abiding nature, and assert that it is inappropriate to apprehend, 'Form and so forth are empty of true existence', and so forth—these are incorrect, because they are invalidated by valid reasonings and scriptures. From the Great Commentary on the Root of Wisdom: 'Likewise, when it is taught, "Things do not exist by their essence", it is not that it is inappropriate to apprehend, "These are empty of true existence", but rather it is inappropriate to apprehend, "This emptiness of things of true existence is true". Therefore, asserting that this text teaches that even apprehending "Phenomena are empty" is incurable, is greatly unexamined speech. Both masters stated that the meaning of "view of emptiness" is clinging to emptiness as a thing, and that is apprehending emptiness as true, or apprehending emptiness as existing by its essence; how could it be merely apprehending, "It lacks nature"?' And it states: 'If one wrongly apprehends the meaning of the text in that way, one deprecates ultimate truth by saying it is impossible, and even if it is possible, by apprehending that maintaining the ascertainment ascertaining it leads to apprehending marks, one creates a great obstacle to maintaining the view that properly ascertains the authentic meaning.' Also from the Heart of Wisdom: In answer to the question of how to practice the profound perfection of wisdom, it is stated, 'He rightly sees those five aggregates also as empty of nature'—stating that one must see them as empty of nature; and also from this very sūtra: 'Whenever, analyzing conditioned and unconditioned and white and black phenomena with wisdom, not even a mere particle is observed; at that time it counts as the perfection of wisdom in the world'—because it states that if, by analyzing phenomena with wisdom, one sees that not even a mere particle is observed, it counts as the perfection of wisdom. There are certainly many other scriptures and reasonings, but fearing too many words, I have not expanded upon them. ^3-31

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-32]]

As for the aspect, it is taught by one stanza, 'Whoever [apprehends] not form' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: Whoever does not apprehend form as form established by its own essence, and likewise does not apprehend feeling as established by nature, and does not apprehend perception, volition, and so forth themselves as established from their own side, and also does not practice or abide by way of apprehending consciousness as established by its own mode of abiding or essence; in short, that meditative equipoise, which, having blocked the appearance of the five aggregates from the perspective of meditative equipoise poised in emptiness, practices without abiding with true clinging to any object whatsoever, is not observed as [thinking], 'I practice on emptiness', because the wisdom that is free from all conceit of the three spheres being true has become stable. Having taught the aspect thus, the manner in which it acts as a cause is: Endowed with the mind that realizes the three—agent, action, and object—as truly unproduced in that way, one attains the supreme utter pacification of distraction and so forth in the samādhis named 'heroic stride' and so forth, which is to say one touches the Mahāyāna path of seeing that completely obtains the classes of realization of the three vehicles. This teaches that realizing that what is ultimately without essence is conventionally the essence of form and so forth is the aspect. The term 'patience' is because one is free from manifest fear regarding the profound unproduced phenomena. ^3-32

###### **Object of Supreme Dharma** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-1-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-33]]

The object of observation of supreme dharma. The object of observation of supreme dharma is taught by one stanza, 'Whatever bodhisattva' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: Whatever bodhisattva, when meditating on the perfection of wisdom that has become the essence of the path of preparation in the manner explained previously, at this time when the manifest apprehension of self of phenomena and persons in their own continuum is appeased, abides in the samādhi of supreme dharma of the path—they are prophesied by the former Tathāgatas to not turn back from unexcelled perfect awakening; at that time, for that bodhisattva, there arises no conceit thinking, 'I am poised in meditative equipoise on emptiness' or 'I have arisen from it', or 'I am prophesied'. If one asks for what reason, it is because they completely know the nature of all phenomena to be the equality of emptiness of true existence. By this, it is taught that form and so forth, indivisible in essence from the special cause by which one is prophesied by the Buddhas when one realizes its nature, are the object of observation. ^3-33

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-34]]

As for the aspect, it is taught by one stanza, 'If one practices in that way' and so forth. The meaning is as follows: If one practices in that way without conceit of truth regarding any phenomenon whatsoever, one is practicing the wisdom of the Sugatas, because that bodhisattva thoroughly knows that the very fact that there is ultimately no practicing regarding any phenomenon is the holy practice of a conqueror's child. Regarding any phenomenon of the path of preparation and so forth, one does not observe as true the object of practice which is the perfection of wisdom, the practitioner which is the bodhisattva, or the action of practicing which is the practice; this practice without observing the three spheres is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom. Thus, this teaches the aspect as being without conceit, and also teaches the distinction of the cause. Each of the path of preparation's [levels of] heat and so forth has three—small, middling, and great—making twelve; and although there are distinctions of dissimilar objects of observation and aspects of these to be correlated with the extensive and middling sūtras and so forth, here, owing to the abbreviated words of the sūtra, they cannot be explicitly correlated, but they should be known to be taught implicitly. ^3-34

###### **Distinctions of Result, Concomitance, Abandonment, and Realization** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-35]]

Distinctions of abandonment and realization associated with the result. Second, the explanation of the distinction of the conceptualizations to be abandoned has two: the conceptualization of the apprehended and the conceptualization of the apprehender which are cognitive obscurations. ^3-35

###### **Apprehended Conceptuality of Cognitive Obscurations** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-36]]

The conceptualization of the apprehended, a cognitive obscuration. If the first is also divided by way of its root, there are two: the conceptualization of the apprehended regarding the thoroughly afflicted and the conceptualization of the apprehended regarding the completely purified. If divided by way of its branches: for the conceptualization of the apprehended regarding the thoroughly afflicted which is a cognitive obscuration, there are nine, such as the clinging awareness that, observing afflicted ignorance, clings to it as truly being an object to be enjoyed. For the conceptualization of the apprehended regarding the completely purified which is a cognitive obscuration, there are also nine, such as the clinging awareness that, observing the completely purified aggregates, clings to them as truly being objects to be enjoyed. As for these two conceptualizations of the apprehended, by one stanza, 'Whatever does not exist' and so forth, from the opposite side of explicitly teaching the object of observation of the conceptualization of the apprehended regarding the completely purified, it teaches ignorance that is deluded regarding the mode of existence of the two truths as the object of observation of the conceptualization of the apprehended regarding the thoroughly afflicted. Moreover, whatever apprehended object of the thoroughly afflicted or completely purified, when analyzed by reasonings such as the refutation of production from the four extremes and so forth, is unable to withstand analysis and so is not truly existent; this is called 'not established by nature', because it is established by scripture and reasoning. Childish ordinary beings, having properly analyzed those phenomena, posit them as existing merely conventionally and not existing ultimately, and these two—existing conventionally and not existing ultimately—are also phenomena that are not truly existent, because both are merely posited by the power of conceptuality and cannot withstand analysis by reasoning. Therefore, if whatever bodhisattva knows this manner in which, just as pebbles and sticks in an illusion appearing as horses and elephants does not contradict their being empty of that, phenomena existing conventionally and not existing ultimately not only do not contradict one another but act as mutually helpful companions, they will definitely emerge from the extremes of existence and peace and will obtain non-abiding nirvāṇa. ^3-36

###### **Apprehending Conceptuality of Cognitive Obscurations** ^3-3-2-1-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-37]]

The conceptualization of the apprehender, a cognitive obscuration. Second, for the conceptualization of the apprehender which is a cognitive obscuration, if divided by way of its root, there are two: the conceptualization of the apprehender of the substantial and the conceptualization of the apprehender of the imputed. If divided by way of its branches: for the conceptualization of the apprehender of the substantial which is a cognitive obscuration, there are nine, such as the true apprehension of an enjoyer observing the apprehension of a self as independent. For the conceptualization of the apprehender of the imputed which is a cognitive obscuration, there are also nine, such as the true apprehension of an enjoyer observing the apprehension of a person as imputed. These two conceptualizations of the apprehender are taught by one stanza, 'Whoever here [apprehends] the five aggregates' and so forth. Furthermore, from the opposite side of explicitly teaching the 'I' imputed upon the five aggregates—the first of the nine objects of observation—as the object of observation of the apprehension of the imputed, it teaches the self independent of the aggregates as the object of observation of the apprehension of the substantial. Whoever, here during the time of training on the path, knows all phenomena such as the five aggregates to be like an illusion, for example, appearing yet not truly existent, and regarding that also, does not make a distinction of true and untrue between those two by treating the illusion as one thing that is false and the aggregates as another thing that is true, but knowing the five aggregates themselves to be like an illusion, a dream, a mirage, and so forth, is free from the perception of the various conceptualizations of the apprehender and practices the thorough pacification of all fabrications; this is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom. In this context, being free from the conceptualizations of the apprehended and apprehender is not abandoning their seeds, but being free from their manifestation. ^3-37

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-38]]
