Third, teaching the distinction of the spiritual friend who is a spiritual mentor is taught by one stanza, 'Endowed with a spiritual friend' and so forth. Regarding this, from the two spiritual mentors, outer and inner: the outer spiritual mentor, being skillful in means, teaches, 'There are faults of the māras and actions such as this,' and turns [one] away from them, and then, in accordance with the mind of the disciple, gradually guides them on the paths of the lesser and middling persons, such as impermanence, suffering, and so forth, and when their continuum is well-ripened, acts as a spiritual mentor by way of teaching the meaning of the perfection of wisdom, such as emptiness and so forth. The inner spiritual mentor, being endowed with distinguished means and wisdom, holds [one] back from the two: attention of the lower vehicles and clinging to true existence. ^3-38

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-39]]

The meaning of the words: Bodhisattvas are endowed with a spiritual friend who guides them on the virtuous path by way of completely teaching phenomena of means and wisdom, such as the profound mode of existence, emptiness, and mind generation, dedication, aspiration prayers, and so forth; and being endowed with the two spiritual mentors, outer and inner, such as special insight that is the nature of the wisdom realizing emptiness embraced by distinguished means, by having heard the perfection of wisdom, the generating mother of the Victors, they will absolutely never become frightened, will comprehend the meaning of the mode of existence, and will not degenerate from the Mahāyāna. Having thus taught the distinction of the spiritual mentor by way of forward sequence, furthermore teaching it by way of reverse sequence: Whichever beginner who, not being inclined toward the Mahāyāna, is endowed with a non-virtuous friend who turns them away from it, and is devoid of the inner spiritual mentor of realizing the meaning of the perfection of wisdom by their own power, is left to the kindness of others, that is, dependent on others—for example, just as an unbaked vessel falls apart when touched by water, like water being poured into a clay pot not fired in a fire, causing it to spill and the clay pot to be destroyed, because they too, being frightened by emptiness, will discard the perfection of wisdom, and like the destroyed clay pot, all their previously existing conduct and practice will be destroyed. ^3-39

###### **Mahayana Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-40]]

Mahāyāna practice. Fourth, the naturally abiding affinity which is the support for Mahāyāna practice: The Ornament says, 'Realization is / the six phenomena,' down to, 'is called the affinity.' Regarding this, there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: the true nature of the mind with stains that has become its own support phenomenon, which is suitable to transform into the nature body, is the definition of the naturally abiding affinity. The common locus of being the true nature of the continuum of a bodhisattva and being the actual support basis for Mahāyāna practice is the definition of the naturally abiding affinity which is the support for Mahāyāna practice. If the affinity is divided by way of explicitly expressed affinities, there are two: the naturally abiding affinity and the developing affinity. If the naturally abiding affinity which is the support for Mahāyāna practice is divided, there are thirteen naturally abiding affinities which are the supports for: the six phenomena of realization, which are the four such as heat and so forth, and the two, seeing and meditation; the practice of antidotes; the practice of abandonment; the practice reaching the end of those objects of abandonment; the practice endowed with wisdom and compassion; the practice uncommon with disciples such as śrāvakas and so forth; the practice accomplishing the welfare of others in sequence; and the practice of engaging in pristine wisdom without effort. The boundaries are from the Mahāyāna path of accumulation up to the end of the continuum. ^3-40

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-41]]

Although the thirteen are taught individually in the extensive sutras, here, since the words are summarized, teaching just the general naturally abiding affinity is taught by the two lines 'For what reason is this' and so forth. Furthermore, if one asks, 'For what reason is this person to be designated as a bodhisattva?' The answer is: if the mind were truly established, it would not provide the space for the arising of qualities, so one would not obtain the name 'bodhisattva'; however, since the naturally luminous mind, the basis from which all the qualities of a Buddha arise, is suitable for the abandonment of faults and suitable for the arising of qualities, by means of the wisdom of hearing, thinking, and meditating that realizes the true nature of the mind, one cuts off without remainder clinging to true existence or attachment to all phenomena such as forms and so forth; and having completely abandoned the conceptualizations of attachment through clinging to true existence toward all phenomena of objects of knowledge along with their seeds, by desiring their exhaustion, and because one will gradually obtain great awakening, one obtains the name 'bodhisattva'. For example, just as space provides room for other things with form, on the occasion when the sun gradually rises in space, it obtains the names of 'morning', 'noon', and so forth. From the Uttaratantra: 'Impure, both impure and pure, / and completely pure, in sequence, / are expressed as sentient beings, bodhisattvas, / and tathāgatas.' Thus, on the occasion of being impure with stains, they are designated as ordinary beings; when partially pure, as bodhisattvas; and when completely pure, they are designated with the name 'tathāgata'. Here too, all the thirteen bodhisattvas designated based on the different occasions of practice should be known to be designated based on the occasions of the gradual purification of the stains of that naturally abiding affinity—the true nature of the mind that provides the space or acts as the basis for the arising of qualities—and the occasions when qualities have gone on to increase. ^3-41

###### **Object of Mahayana Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-42]]

Mahāyāna practice's focal object. Fifth, the focal object of Mahāyāna practice: The Ornament says, 'The focal object is all phenomena,' down to, 'is uncommon to the Sage.' ^3-42

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-43]]

Regarding this, there are two: the definition and the divisions. The first: the basis upon which superimpositions are to be cut off by Mahāyāna practice is the definition of the focal object of Mahāyāna practice. ^3-43

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-44]]

Second, if that is divided, there are eleven: the three, virtuous, non-virtuous, and unspecified; the aggregates of worldly ordinary beings; the concentrations of the continuums of supramundane noble ones; the contaminated appropriated aggregates; the uncontaminated ones such as mindfulness and so forth; the conditioned path truth; the unconditioned cessation truth; the qualities common to the three vehicles, the concentrations; and the uncommon ones, the ten powers. Although all phenomena are focal objects of Mahāyāna practice, one must primarily treat the ultimate focal object of the completely pure path [as the focal object], because if one meditates by focusing on the mind as empty of true existence, just as clouds exhaust in the sky and a water bubble dissolves into water, such things as seeing cessation upon itself, stains exhausting into the dharmadhātu, and positing the cessation truth as the ultimate truth come about, and because they have the same vital point; and if one asks, 'If emptiness is primarily explicitly taught here, how is there no fault of repetition with the text teaching the affinity?' Gungthang Jamyang said there is no fault, because it is explained that the affinity is primarily the nature and the focal object is primarily the manifestation, so on the occasion of the affinity, by establishing emptiness of true existence as the basis of attributes and all other things as its attributes, it is expressed in the manner of the subject, whereas on the occasion of the focal object, all phenomena are expressed as the basis of attributes and the lack of true existence as like the attribute. ^3-44

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-45]]

Having taught the affinity, to teach the focal object of practice, the two lines 'of the Victors' and so forth were spoken. The meaning is: By realizing and habituating to all phenomena as empty of true existence, one abandons the grasping at true existence along with its seeds and attains contact with 'the great awakening of the Victors,' which has become free from attachment through clinging to true existence; therefore, this person obtains the name 'bodhisattva'. Thus, on this occasion, since the two bodies are taught as the result, it is established that at the time of the cause one focuses on both the mode of being and the multiplicity; and if so, it is established that all phenomena are posited as the focal object of practice. ^3-45

###### **Objective of Mahayana Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-6-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-46]]

Mahāyāna practice's objective. Sixth, the objective of Mahāyāna practice: The Ornament says, 'The mind of the supremacy of all sentient beings,' down to, 'are to be known here.' ^3-46

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-47]]

Regarding this, there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: the ultimate result for the sake of which one engages in practice is the definition of the objective of Mahāyāna practice. If divided, there are three: great being, great abandonment, and great realization. The boundaries exist only at the Buddha stage. The meaning taught by the one verse 'For what reason is that' and so forth concerning such three great objectives is this: Furthermore, if one asks, 'For what reason is that person to be designated with the name "great being"?' Because this one has become the protector and ally of a great host and many assemblies of sentient beings, and thus has become supreme and has become great, it is the great mind; and due to the respective inclinations of sentient beings with different elements and inclinations, there are sixty-two bad views rooted in the view of the transitory collection—forty-eight relying on the past limit and fourteen relying on the future limit—and limitless bad views such as the three hundred bad views and so forth; because they have been habituated since beginningless time, they are difficult to destroy, and because they are many, therefore they are great; in order to sever their continuum, by teaching phenomena such as emptiness and so forth, one clears them away; and since one must first abandon them oneself, it is the great abandonment. Having explicitly taught those two, just as one sees forms when freed from things that obscure, by explicitly teaching the abandonment of the obscurations that hinder the realization of all phenomena, the great realization of the exalted wisdom of omniscience that realizes them is also implicitly understood. Therefore, because one has entered into practicing the path for the sake of obtaining the three bodies as the result, 'for that reason it is fully expressed as "great being".' ^3-47

###### **Armor Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-7-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-48]]

The practice of armor. Seventh, the practice of armor: The Ornament says, 'Giving and so forth, in each of those,' down to, 'are explained accordingly by the six.' The yoga of a bodhisattva possessing the six greatnesses, which accomplishes the two purposes perfectly based on the Mahāyāna mind generation, is the definition of Mahāyāna practice. If divided, for example, akin to donning armor for waging war, engaging in the battle, the allies and weapons that make one victorious over the enemy, and being victorious over the enemy, there are four: the practice of armor, the practice of engagement, the practice of accumulation, and the practice of definite emergence. First, regarding the practice of armor, there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: the yoga of a bodhisattva held by the vast mental activity of wishing to practice by completely subsuming all six perfections within each perfection such as giving and so forth is the definition of the practice of armor. That and the knowledge of a bodhisattva are equivalent. Second, if that is divided, there are six, from the practice of the armor of giving up to that of wisdom. If divided extensively, there are six for each such as giving and so forth, thus thirty-six. Third, the boundaries exist from the Mahāyāna path of accumulation up to the end of the continuum. That practice of armor is taught by the two lines 'with great giving' and so forth. The meaning is: Not only because of what was just explained, but also because of having donned a 'great armor,' one is called a 'great being.' If one asks, 'What is that armor?', just as the Bodhicaryāvatāra says, 'With the mind of giving to all beings / All possessions along with the results,' giving all one's body, resources, and roots of virtue of the three times is the practice of the armor of giving, possessing 'great giving'; the practice of the armor of ethical discipline of a bodhisattva that abandons faulty discipline such as the ethical discipline of restraining from misconduct, the ethical discipline of gathering virtuous dharmas, and the ethical discipline of working for the welfare of sentient beings, or [that abandons] small-mindedness; and 'great power' refers to the latter four perfections; furthermore, even though returning harm in response to help given and repaying kindness wrongly and so forth are difficult to bear, the patience of not being overcome by them, and the joyous effort of lacking weariness for the difficult practices of a conqueror's child such as giving away one's limbs, and the single-pointed concentration unmixed with the mental engagements of the lower vehicles, and the practice of the armor of wisdom realizing the three spheres of agent, action, and object to be empty of true existence, and so forth, possess the 'great power' of destroying their respective discordant classes; and because one is endowed with entering into the practice of the path along with its result from mind generation up to omniscience in 'the supreme Mahāyāna of the Victors,' one has donned the great armor. Although Buddhaśrī applied this latter line to the practice of engagement, it is just as the Master Haribhadra applied it to the practice of armor. ^3-48

###### **Engagement Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-8-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-49]]

The practice of engagement. Eighth, the practice of engagement: The Ornament says, 'The concentrations, formless [absorptions], giving and so forth,' down to, 'should be known.' Regarding this there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: the yoga of a bodhisattva held by wisdom engaging in the practice of whatever Mahāyāna causal and resultant phenomena principally by way of peak application is the definition of the practice of engagement. Second, if that is divided, there are nine: the practice of engaging in the concentrations and formless [absorptions] of a bodhisattva, that engaging in the perfections, that engaging in the noble paths, that engaging in the four immeasurables, that engaging endowed with non-apprehension, that engaging in the complete purity of the three spheres, that engaging in the purpose, that engaging in the clairvoyances, and the practice of engagement engaging in omniscience; thus there are nine. Third, the boundaries are from heat of the path of preparation up to the end of the continuum. Although the specifics are taught individually in the Śatasāhasrikā and so forth, here, due to the brevity of the words, in order to indicate merely the general nature of the practice of engagement, the meaning of the two lines spoken, 'having donned the great armor' and so forth, is: in practicing without exception the Mahāyāna paths that are limitless like space, such as the six perfections, having donned the great armor of the thought of irreversible fortitude, dispelling the bad views and suffering of sentient beings residing in the worldly realms of the ten directions, and striving to subdue all the magical arts of the Devaputra demons—because of that, one is fully proclaimed a 'great being.' Having thus taught the practice of engagement in general, the specifics are implicitly projected. ^3-49

###### **Accumulation Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-9-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-50]]

The practice of accumulation. Ninth, the practice of accumulation: The Ornament says, 'Compassion and the six, giving and so forth,' down to, 'should be known.' Regarding this there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: the yoga of a bodhisattva that, by way of being held by the vast two accumulations, is exalted above the middling supreme dharma of the Mahāyāna path of preparation and below, and that brings about its own result of great awakening, is the definition of the practice of accumulation. Second, if that is divided, there are seventeen: the accumulation of great compassion, the accumulation of giving, ethical discipline, patience, joyous effort, concentration, and wisdom, the accumulation of calm abiding, the accumulation of special insight, the accumulation of their union, the accumulation of skillful means, the accumulation of pristine awareness, the accumulation of merit, the accumulation of the path, the accumulation of dhāraṇī, the accumulation of the grounds, and the accumulation of antidotes. Third, the boundaries are from the great supreme dharma of the Mahāyāna path of preparation up to the end of the continuum. Furthermore, in answer to the question arising from the practice of armor, 'How does one don the armor?', [it is taught as] the first ten practices of accumulation; and in answer to the questions arising from the practice of engagement, 'What is that Mahāyāna which one engages in? By how much has one engaged? From what does one emerge? Where does one abide? What is it that emerges?', the first is [answered by the accumulations] from skillful means up to dhāraṇī; the second, by the accumulation of the grounds; the answer to the third, by the accumulation of antidotes to what is abandoned by seeing; to the fourth, by the antidotes to the first conceptuality abandoned by meditation; and in answer to the fifth, it is taught as the antidotes to the remaining three abandoned by meditation. Here as well, the two verses starting with 'For example, by a magician' and so forth teach the first ten practices of accumulation in answer to the question, 'By how much is the armor donned?' Furthermore, the first verse teaches the accumulation of great compassion. Then, the single verse up to 'this is the supreme armor of the genuine ones' teaches the nine accumulations ending with the union [of calm abiding and special insight]. The three verses from 'Why is this?' down to 'practicing the supreme perfection' teach the remaining seven accumulations. The meaning of those is: For example, just as an expert skilled in performing illusions or 'a magician', having fully 'manifested' a 'great crowd of people' 'at a crossroads', a place for manifesting illusions, and when demonstrating the magic of 'severing' the 'many tens of millions of heads' of 'most'—i.e., the vast majority of—'people', because those who are to be killed are merely magical manifestations and have no mind, there is no fear or suffering, and no sin and so forth occurs for the killer; just like that, the bodhisattva too, having completely understood that 'all these' transmigrators afflicted by immeasurable suffering are completely without true establishment and are like 'manifestations', and by way of knowing the manner in which conventionally it is suitable to establish transmigrators without exception in happiness and protect them from suffering and so forth, that bodhisattva has not the slightest fear or terror in acting for the benefit of transmigrators with love and great compassion. The reason why 'sentient beings' are like magical manifestations is thus: what are called 'sentient beings' are merely imputed in dependence upon these 'five aggregates' which are their basis of imputation; in reality, 'form, feeling, perception, intention, and consciousness' are 'not bound' by the ignorance of true grasping, nor are they 'liberated' by anything such as the awareness that realizes lack of true existence. The reason for that is because there are no valid cognitions establishing that these five aggregates exist ultimately. Having understood this way, having no discouraged mind toward the manner of engaging in the accomplishment of perfect awakening merely conventionally, even though it does not exist ultimately, 'this is the supreme armor of the genuine ones'—the bodhisattvas. ^3-50

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-51]]

If one asks, "Why is this" path "named the 'Great Vehicle' of the bodhisattva?" Since the word "vehicle" also applies to a mount, whichever person mounts that Great Vehicle, which is comprised of the practices of accumulation of love, giving, and so forth, makes all sentient beings to be trained go beyond sorrow, that is, pass away from it, and therefore in the sense of a mount it is called a vehicle. This is because this path makes oneself and all others travel to the ground of a Buddha. The nature of "this Great Vehicle" is "like space" because it opens up the refuge of liberation for immeasurable sentient beings. Because it possesses an infinite array of qualities such as dhāraṇīs, eloquent confidence, concentrations, superknowledges, and magical manifestations, it is like a "mansion", therefore a great mansion. Because it causes the persons who have entered such a path of the Great Vehicle to temporarily attain mental "joy" and physical "bliss" and "happiness", and to manifestly attain the distinguished bliss of non-abiding nirvāṇa as the ultimate result, it is called the "supreme vehicle" or "Great Vehicle". Ultimately, "whoever" mounts "whatever" Great Vehicle and "goes" in the direction of the result of the ten grounds and so forth is "unobservable". ^3-51

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-52]]

If one asks, "Well then, how is it that, in dependence upon this vehicle, it is taught that one goes from saṃsāra to the result of nirvāṇa?" It is not that one goes from a truly established saṃsāra to a truly established nirvāṇa; rather, since going to nirvāṇa is unobservable when both saṃsāra and nirvāṇa are sought by reasoning, the attainment of nirvāṇa must be posited upon the mere aspect of the exhaustion of saṃsāra. "For example", just as upon the mere aspect of the "fire" ceasing because the "firewood is exhausted", it is imputed that it is extinguished, but there is nowhere else for that fire to go; "just like that", due to the mere cause of the exhaustion of being born in saṃsāra by the power of karma and afflictions, that person is "fully declared" to have "passed beyond sorrow"—that is, to have attained liberation. ^3-52

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-53]]

The practitioner of the path, "the bodhisattva", is "unobservable" in the "past limit"—that is, the phase of an ordinary being in the past time—and the "future limit"—that is, the phase of a Buddha in the future time—"and also" the "presently arisen" phase of a noble bodhisattva. This is because they have not arisen from their own side, nor will they arise later. Therefore, being free from a truly established one or many in the three times, they are "naturally pure", and there is no distinguishing them by any time. ^3-53

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-54]]

"Those" called "bodhisattvas" are "unconditioned" because they are not found when analyzed by reasoning; because they are empty of intrinsic nature, for that very reason there are "no fabrications" of grasping at true existence regarding form and so forth. Knowing in that way that there is not even the slightest focal target for true grasping, "this is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom." Thus it is said. ^3-54

###### **Definite Emergence Practice** ^3-3-2-1-1-10-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-55]]

Progressing practice. The tenth, progressing practice: The *Ornament* says, "Objective, equality," up to "is called progressing practice." Regarding this, there are three [topics]: definition, divisions, and limits. The first: the definition of progressing practice is a yoga of the pure grounds that infallibly causes progression to omniscience. The second, dividing it, there are eight: progressing practice for the sake of the three great objectives, that of equality, that of the welfare of sentient beings, that of spontaneous accomplishment, that of passing beyond extremes, progressing practice to omniscience, and progressing practice having the path as its object. The third, the limits: it is present on the three pure grounds. In the extensive sūtra, these eight are taught by way of being the bases of attributes, and emptiness by way of being the attribute; to know that this must also be practiced by way of unobservability from beginning to end, Gungtang Jamyang states, "It is progression by means of unobservability itself." Although Buddhaśrī applies [the text] from "whoever mounts" onwards to progressing practice, our own system follows the master [Haribhadra]. The meaning taught by the five verses starting with "A" bodhisattva "skilled..." is this: "Whatever bodhisattva" is "skilled" in the manner of positing conventionalities, and "at the time" of engaging in the conduct of a conqueror's son, such as the progressing practice for the objective, having investigated the abiding nature, suchness, with whatever reasoning, such as the refutation of arising from the four extremes, "reflects" upon the meaning of naturally non-arising, and having realized the meaning of non-arising, "practices in this way" and abides. ^3-55

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-56]]

Furthermore, the meaning of stating that the words "at the time" refute Kaṇāda and others' assertion of time as permanent is: "For example, when one says in the world, 'Previously, right around such-and-such,' it is understood that the present has changed from that previous occasion; likewise, by understanding from 'at the time' that time changes, it is able to refute time as permanent," as taught in the *Detailed Exegesis of the Perfection of Wisdom*. If one asks, "Furthermore, how do they abide?" Even though they generate great "love" and "compassion" wishing for "all beings" to be free from suffering, not conceptualizing them as sentient beings by way of manifestly adhering to self and sentient beings as truly existent, "this is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom," which is in nature the progressing practice for the sake of "all" objectives. If one asks, "What fault is there in observing them as sentient beings?" "If", generating the concept that oneself and "sentient beings" truly exist, "and likewise" the concept that "suffering" also truly exists, they practice "with hardship" the perfections such as giving, and think, "'I shall' benefit beings and 'abandon their suffering'"; that bodhisattva who "thoroughly conceives" them "as true" by way of manifestly adhering to self and sentient beings is bound by true grasping in their continuum, and "because of that", from that fault of apprehending self and other, all faults such as attachment and hostility arise; as the Lord of Reasoning says: "If self exists, one cognizes other. / From the division of self and other, grasping and hostility. / In complete connection with these, / all faults will arise." "Therefore, practicing in this way is not thoroughly practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom." ^3-56

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-57]]

If one asks, "Then how should they practice?" Because sentient beings and suffering are not truly existent, it is reasonable to accomplish the welfare of others and liberate them from suffering; and furthermore, regarding that lack of true existence, between the two, phenomena and persons, examining persons, and between the two, self and other, looking initially at the manner in which this appears to one's own mind, the object of negation is easy to recognize. By that vital point, emptiness, the non-affirming negation that refutes that object of negation, is also easy to ascertain. Therefore, initially one "should know oneself," the bodhisattva, as "without intrinsic nature," and "then," just as self is not established by intrinsic nature, "in that manner" one "should know all sentient beings" also as not established by intrinsic nature; and just as all sentient beings are without intrinsic nature, "in that manner," having known "all phenomena" such as the generation of the mind as without intrinsic nature, one does "not conceptualize" either ultimate non-arising or conventional arising as truly existent. Having realized them as a unity, the indivisible essence of the two truths, and having known the abiding nature of the two truths just as it is, abiding in the meaning of the lack of true existence, "this is practicing the supreme" progressing practice, the topic of "the perfection of wisdom." ^3-57

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-58]]

If one asks, "What is to be attained by that non-dual, non-conceptual pristine wisdom?" For all phenomena—whether the ten phenomena such as the generation of mind, or all phenomena from form up to omniscience, whatever names of phenomena are suitable to be fully expressed in the nine worlds—having abandoned the extreme of ultimate "arising and" perfect "passing away" or ceasing, whatever non-conceptual pristine wisdom of the Buddha's mind abides in the equality of being empty of true existence, without ultimate arising or ceasing, having abandoned all decline and harm without exception, and because it does not degenerate from that, it is "deathless," "and" there is no other that is "supreme" compared to that "holy pristine wisdom" of omniscience. Since it causes the attainment of that very excellent and unsurpassed phenomenon, "therefore" this path that realizes all phenomena as ultimately unobservable is called progressing practice for the objective, "the supreme" of "wisdom," the "perfection." "Holy deathless pristine wisdom" and so forth teach the two—abandonment and realization—for one's own welfare within progressing practice. ^3-58

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-59]]

"Whatever bodhisattva," having found certain knowledge through valid cognition regarding their own grounds such as progressing practice, practices and experiences exactly as taught previously "without doubt" and hesitation, they are said to be "endowed with the supreme wisdom" embraced by extraordinary methods, and one should know that person as abiding in the equality of existence and peace being empty of true existence. ^3-59

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-60]]

Thus, to completely know the phenomena of the basis, path, and fruit as the equality of lacking intrinsic nature, as the single taste of being empty of true existence, like this "is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom" which is in nature the progressing practice. Thus it is said. Because this text summarizes the entire meaning of the One Hundred Thousand, it would be appropriate to align the chapters in accordance with it, but since those of lesser intellect would not understand, they were not aligned; and similarly, it was not made to accord with the Twenty-five Thousand. The Difficult Points Commentary states, "In order to make it easy to realize and to know the meaning as one, the chapters were aligned in accordance with the Eight Thousand." Thus, having finished teaching the ten phenomena characterizing omniscience, the teaching of the name of the chapter and the conclusion of the topic by correlating it with the Ornament of Clear Realization is: From the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection: The Jewel Lamp That Illuminates the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, the first chapter, Engaging in Omniscience, and the commentary on the first topic. ^3-60

###### Path Knowledge ^3-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-61]]

Knowledge of the path. Since, in order to attain that omniscience, one must train in its uncommon cause, the knowledge of the path, regarding the second, the explanation of the knowledge of the path: As the Ornament states from "Making obscure and so forth" up to "The knowledge of the path itself is explained like that," the definition of the knowledge of the path is: a Mahāyāna noble one's knowledge embraced by the wisdom that directly realizes emptiness in the continuum of a person who possesses it in their own continuum. There are eleven phenomena characterizing the knowledge of the path, because they exist as: the branches of the knowledge of the path, the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the śrāvakas, the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the pratyekabuddhas, the Mahāyāna path of seeing, the function of the path of meditation, the path of meditation of aspiration, its benefits—praise, respect, and commendation, the path of meditation of dedication, the path of meditation of rejoicing, the path of meditation of progressing practice, and the perfectly pure path of meditation. ^3-61

###### **Branches of Path Knowledge** ^3-3-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-62]]

Branches of the knowledge of the path. To teach the first, it is stated from "In order to make the gods suitable," up to "Its function." For this, there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. ^3-62

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-63]]

The definition. The first: the definition of the branches of the knowledge of the path is that extraordinary quality embraced by great compassion which is subsumed by any of the three—the cause, essence, or fruit of the knowledge of the path. ^3-63

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-64]]

The divisions. Second, if that is divided, there are five: freedom from the hindrance to the basis, manifest pride; the generating cause, the generation of the mind; being pervaded by the causal disposition; the nature of not abandoning cyclic existence; and the function of gathering others into one's retinue and so forth. ^3-64

###### **Boundaries** ^3-3-2-1-2-1-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-65]]

The boundaries. Third, the boundaries: they exist from before entering the path up to the buddha ground. That branch of the knowledge of the path is taught by one stanza, "Whoever in form..." and so forth. According to Buddhapāda's Commentary on the Verse Summary, it appears appropriate to correlate this with the aids to penetration, which are the cause of the knowledge of the path. However, here, the master's Commentary on the Verse Summary is mainly taken as the basis, and the literal meaning of this is: "Whatever bodhisattva" does "not abide" in the manner of clinging as truly existent "to the aggregate of form," and "similarly" does "not abide in feeling," and "whoever" does "not abide" by manifestly clinging as truly existent "to perception," "and similarly" does "not abide in volition," and does "not abide" by way of clinging as truly existent to "consciousness" or any object whatsoever, having directly realized them as empty of true existence and free from proliferation, resting one-pointedly in equipoise is to "abide in the suchness," the dharmatā of those things; like this "is practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom" which is the nature of the knowledge of the path. Thus it is said. ^3-65

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-66]]

Regarding how this teaches the branches of the knowledge of the path, in the Difficult Points Commentary, although there is no clarification other than merely saying "abiding, that is, five aspects," if one looks at the flow of the speech: Because it teaches that, abiding in the dharmatā of the five aggregates, one must train in higher paths without being satisfied or being proud merely by practicing the perfection of wisdom and attaining qualities, it teaches freedom from the hindrance to the basis. Because the word "abiding" implicitly teaches that merely abiding in the aspect of wisdom is not sufficient, but one must also add the aspect of method on top of that, it teaches that the knowledge of the path arises for one who practices complete method and wisdom, and does not arise for others; thus it teaches the certainty of individual occasions. Furthermore, although "abiding" certainly also applies to meditating by making dharmatā the object of the mode of apprehension, the Detailed Explanation of the Perfection of Wisdom states that it is applied to the naturally abiding disposition, and thus being pervaded by the causal disposition, as well as the nature and the function, are taught. ^3-66

###### **Śrāvaka Path Knowledge** ^3-3-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-67]]

The knowledge of the path of the śrāvakas. Second, regarding the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the śrāvakas, there are two: its actual essence and its cause, the aids to penetration. ^3-67

###### **Its Actual Nature** ^3-3-2-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-68]]

Its actual essence. The first: as the Ornament states from "In the method of the knowledge of the path itself," up to "This śrāvaka path should be known." For this, among the three—the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries: ^3-68

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-69]]

The definition. The first: the definition of the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the śrāvakas is that exalted knowledge of a Mahāyāna noble one abiding in the category of clear realization to be known, for the sake of caring for trainees of the śrāvaka disposition by means of being embraced by the three—generation of the mind, dedication, and wisdom realizing emptiness. ^3-69

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-70]]

The divisions. Second, if that is divided, there are sixteen by way of aspects, and three by way of paths: the path of seeing, the path of meditation, and the path of no more learning that have become that. ^3-70

###### **Boundaries** ^3-3-2-1-2-2-1-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-71]]

The boundaries. Third: they exist from the Mahāyāna path of seeing up to the buddha ground. That is taught by one stanza, "Permanent and impermanent" and so forth. Regarding that, although the Detailed Explanation of the Perfection of Wisdom says, "I wonder whether 'permanent,' 'impermanent,' 'happiness,' 'suffering' and so forth teach the aspects of the first two truths, and 'obtaining the fruit' and 'the ground of an arhat' and so forth teach the aspects of cessation and path?", if one examines the master's Commentary on the Verse Summary, I wonder whether the aspects of the truth of suffering are taught from "permanent" and "impermanent" up to "selfless," and the aspects of the latter three truths are taught by "suchness" and "empty of that." Those endowed with discernment should analyze this in detail. The literal meaning is: whoever does "not abide" in the manner of clinging as truly existent to "permanent" which does not disintegrate, "and impermanent" which is its opposite, "happiness" having the aspect of soothing feeling, "and" contaminated "suffering," "attractive" because of being agreeable to the mind, "and unattractive" because of being disagreeable to the mind, an existing "self" of a person that has become one's own essence, "and selfless," "and in suchness" meaning ultimately existent, "and" although appearing truly existent, "empty" of existing "like that" or being established like that, and so forth, regarding any dharma; "and" does "not abide" with manifest clinging to "obtaining the three fruits" of stream-enterer, once-returner, and non-returner, "and the ground of a śrāvaka arhat"; "and" does "not abide" with manifest clinging "to the ground of a pratyekabuddha, and similarly to the buddha ground"; in short, "does not abide" by manifestly clinging as truly existent to this and that, regarding any phenomena subsumed by the three—the basis, path, and fruit. Thus it is said. This means that that śrāvaka path of directly realizing the sixteen aspects such as impermanence is a path to be known by bodhisattvas, but they do not practice it by way of striving for the enlightenment of the inferior vehicle, and it teaches that they perform the three—generating, knowing, and completing—in order to care for trainees of the śrāvaka disposition by unifying method and wisdom, such as the generation of the mind, dedication, and wisdom realizing emptiness. ^3-71

###### **Its Cause, the Aids to Penetration** ^3-3-2-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-72]]

Its cause, the aids to penetration. Second, to teach its cause, the aids to penetration, the meaning of what is stated in the one stanza "Just as the Guide" and so forth is: for example of not abiding in any dharma, "just as" the mind of "the Guide," the Bhagavan Buddha, does "not abide in the unconditioned realm," the basic space of nirvāṇa wherein mere arising in cyclic existence is pacified, "and similarly" does "not abide" by way of clinging as truly existent "to the conditioned" side of "the three realms" of cyclic existence, "practicing" without abiding in any extreme of existence or peace; "likewise," "a bodhisattva" having realized all phenomena as lacking inherent existence, "abides" on paths such as the aids to penetration, but "abiding without" abiding by "clinging" as truly existent "even to that" means abiding without abiding ultimately in any dharma, but rather abiding in the suchness free from proliferation; "like this" manner, the King of Dharma, "the Victor, has stated" is to "abide in" the definitive "perfection of wisdom." Thus it is said. ^3-72

###### **Pratyekabuddha Path Knowledge** ^3-3-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-73]]

The knowledge of the path of the pratyekabuddhas. Third, the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the pratyekabuddhas: as the Ornament states from "In order to realize the self-arisen nature," up to "Should be known as perfectly subsumed." For this, there are three: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. ^3-73

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-74]]

The definition. The first: the definition of the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the pratyekabuddhas is that exalted knowledge of a Mahāyāna noble one abiding in the category of clear realization to be known, for the sake of caring for trainees of the pratyekabuddha disposition by means of being embraced by the three special qualities. ^3-74

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-75]]

The divisions. Second, if that is divided, there are the three paths of seeing, meditation, and no more learning. ^3-75

###### **Boundaries** ^3-3-2-1-2-3-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-76]]

The boundaries. Third, the boundaries: they exist from the Mahāyāna path of seeing up to the buddha ground. Here, in teaching the knowledge of the path that knows the path of the pratyekabuddhas, among the two—the actual and the incidental: ^3-76

###### **Actual** ^3-3-2-1-2-3-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-77]]

The actual. First, it is taught by one stanza, "Whoever" "Sugata" and so forth. Moreover, because the two—śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas—are similar in striving for liberation only for their own sake and so forth, in order to clear away the doubt thinking it is inappropriate to teach the path of pratyekabuddhas separately from that of śrāvakas, if one first shows that the pratyekabuddha is distinguished from the śrāvaka, one is able to deduce the presentation of their vehicle; thus the difference between those two is: śrāvakas are endowed with the two distinctions of obtaining their own enlightenment by listening to instructions from others during their final existence, and having obtained the fruit, teaching Dharma to others vocally; whereas the pratyekabuddha is distinguished by the two special qualities of manifestly actualizing enlightenment by their own power without depending on others such as buddhas teaching Dharma to them during their final existence, and thus being called "self-arisen," and teaching Dharma to others non-vocally through physical miracles. Having thus stated how they are distinguished, because it is necessary to teach the essence of the knowledge of the path of the pratyekabuddhas, teaching its essence is as follows: as explained before, because one cannot obtain any of the three enlightenments without realizing all bases, paths, and aspects as empty of true existence, having to train in the perfection of wisdom of the definitive essence means: "whoever" among people thinks, "I shall become a śrāvaka" "disciple" "of the Sugata," "and" wants to become a "pratyekabuddha" who mainly abandons the conceptualization grasping at external apprehended objects, "and likewise" wants to become the King of Dharma, the Bhagavan "Buddha"—in short, whichever of the three enlightenments one desires to obtain, without relying on "this patience" unafraid of the definitive profound natural state of emptiness, the path realizing all phenomena as lacking inherent existence, one is unable to obtain any of those fruits; "just as" without depending on a "boat," one does "not see the landing" for going to "the further shore and the near shore" of the ocean. Thus it is said. Although this teaches the knowledge of the path as having the objects of the paths of the three vehicles, it mainly teaches that the pratyekabuddha abandons the conceptualization of the apprehended. As for the assertion by some that śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas lack the realization of subtle selflessness of phenomena and so forth, it directly contradicts this sūtra, and the Aṣṭasāhasrikā: "Sons of the gods, therefore, whoever wishes to obtain the fruit of a stream-enterer and wishes to abide in the fruit of a stream-enterer will not accomplish it without relying on this patience. Applying it similarly: whoever wishes to obtain the fruit of a once-returner and wishes to abide in the fruit of a once-returner, and ... up to ... wishes to abide in pratyeka-enlightenment, will not accomplish it without relying on this patience." And from the Vajracchedikā: "Subhūti, what do you think? Does a stream-enterer think, 'I have obtained the fruit of a stream-enterer'?" Subhūti replied, "Bhagavan, that is not so. Why is that? Bhagavan, it is because he has not entered into anything whatsoever, therefore he is called a stream-enterer." And from the Ārya-rāṣṭrapālaparipṛcchā-sūtra: "Because they do not know the way of being empty, peaceful, and non-arising, beings wander. To them, those endowed with compassion apply skillful methods and hundreds of reasonings," which states that realizing emptiness is necessary for liberation from cyclic existence. Thus, it contradicts many sūtras of definitive meaning, and it falls outside the system of the noble Nāgārjuna, Candrakīrti, and so forth, because the Mūlamadhyamakakārikā says: "Through the exhaustion of karma and afflictions, there is liberation. Karma and afflictions arise from conceptualization. Those arise from proliferations. Proliferations are stopped by emptiness." And because the glorious Candrakīrti establishes with three reasonings and many scriptures that śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have the realization that all phenomena lack inherent existence. From his Madhyamakāvatārabhāṣya: "From this system, it is clearly ascertained that 'śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also have the knowledge that all phenomena lack inherent existence.' If it were not so, because they would lack the complete knowledge that entities lack inherent existence, like worldly beings free from attachment, they would also be outshone by the intellectual analysis of bodhisattvas who have generated the mind. And like non-Buddhists, they would not abandon all latent tendencies operating in the three realms either. Because they apprehend the essence of form and so forth, it would become perverse, so they would not realize the selflessness of the person, because they apprehend the aggregates, which are the basis for imputing the self. As the Ratnāvalī states: 'As long as there is grasping at the aggregates, so long is there grasping at "I" regarding them. When there is grasping at "I", there is karma again, and from that there is birth again. With the three paths lacking beginning, middle, or end, this wheel of cyclic existence, like the wheel of a firebrand, having mutual causes, turns. Because of not seeing it in its nature, as both, or as the three times themselves, the grasping at "I" will be exhausted, and from that, karma and birth as well.' Thus it is stated." And to prove that, he cites the Dhyānānusmṛti-sūtra and so forth. Furthermore, the difference between the great and lesser vehicles is not posited by way of view, but is posited by way of the differences of the bodhisattva's aspiration prayers, extensive deeds, and complete dedication, because the Ratnāvalī states: "Since the bodhisattva's aspiration prayers, deeds, and complete dedications are not explained in that śrāvaka vehicle, how could one become a bodhisattva through it? The meaning of abiding in bodhisattva deeds was not spoken in the sūtras. It was spoken in the Mahāyāna, therefore the wise hold it." Nevertheless, how there are differences in realizing or not realizing emptiness through infinite modes of reasoning, and in meditating or not meditating on emptiness perfectly, and so forth, should be known by studying the Madhyamaka texts in detail. ^3-77

###### **Incidental** ^3-3-2-1-2-3-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-78]]

Incidental Topic. Second, incidentally demonstrating that all activities from teaching and listening to the Dharma up to attaining the fruit by meditating are empty of true existence, like an illusion: The meaning taught by the three verses starting with "Teaching the Dharma..." is that the teacher teaching the Dharma, the student properly listening, the Dharma to be taught, and having properly taken to mind the meaning heard, meditating on the path and thereby attaining the four fruits of a Hearer, such as Stream Enterer; and similarly, attaining the path of seeing, path of meditation, and path of no-more-learning, etc., of a pratyekabuddha, that is, a solitary realizer; and similarly, actualizing the awakening of a Buddha, a Bhagavan, the protector, refuge, and ally of the three worlds; and whatever is the ultimate fruit, the non-abiding nirvana, attained by the power of meditating on the Mahayana path with the union of method and wisdom by someone endowed with the excellent, supreme wisdom skilled in accomplishing their own and others' purpose and with clear intelligence regarding the methods of the path - all these, when examined and analyzed by means of the reasoning of dependent origination and so forth, do not have even the slightest true establishment and were taught by the Tathagata, the perfectly complete Buddha, to be empty of true existence, like an illusion. From the Extensive Mother [the Prajñāpāramitā in 100,000 lines]: "Sons of the gods, listening to the Dharma from me is to be seen as like an illusion and like an emanation." Up to: "If there were any other dharma superior to nirvana, I would see that too as like an illusion." And from the Eight Thousand Line [Prajñāpāramitā]: Then, those sons of the gods thought this: "What is the nature of those who seek to listen to the Dharma from the Noble Subhūti?" And then, by the Buddha's power, venerable Subhūti knew with his mind the thoughts in the minds of those sons of the gods, and said to those sons of the gods: "Sons of the gods, listening to the Dharma from me is to be seen as like an illusion and like an emanation." Up to: "Sons of the gods, those sentient beings are like an illusion. Sons of the gods, those sentient beings are like a dream." Up to: "The perfectly complete Buddha himself is like an illusion and like a dream." Up to: "If even nirvana is like an illusion and like a dream, what need is there to speak of other dharmas?" If one asks what kind of person can respectfully train in the meaning explained in that way: These four persons are not frightened of the profound meaning of the abiding nature that has just been explained. If one asks who these four are: A "Child of the Victor skilled in the truth"—merely a pratyekabuddha on the paths of accumulation and preparation; an "irreversible one"—one on the path of preparation; an arhat, a noble one who has dispelled and exhausted the stains of the mind such as the afflictive obscurations and has abandoned doubt regarding the profound meaning of the abiding nature; those three, and fourthly, one who, although a beginner, has rendered special service to the Victors of the past and so forth, and is now fully embraced by a spiritual friend capable of teaching the meaning of the perfection of wisdom unmistakably. Here, because "Child of the Victor skilled in the truth" must be understood as an actual pratyekabuddha, the support is taught as being an actual pratyekabuddha; and texts such as the Eight Thousand Line teach it in that way. The reason why a pratyekabuddha is called a "Child of the Victor" or a "bodhisattva" is explained in the Profound Explanation of the Perfection of Wisdom [by Haribhadra]: "Because they attain awakening for their own sake, they are a bodhisattva, and because they teach the Dharma without words, they are called a mahāsattva." I wonder if it would be suitable to take what is explicitly taught here as principally a bodhisattva and secondarily a pratyekabuddha; but this should be examined. ^3-78

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-79]]

Having thus taught the support, if one asks what the manner of a bodhisattva's training in the path-knowledge of a pratyekabuddha is like: A bodhisattva who is skilled in accomplishing the purpose of those of the three lineages and in practicing the paths of the three vehicles, and who, having realized all dharmas as empty of true existence and free from conceptual proliferation, understands the logic of how they are ultimately established as the single vehicle, and who thus practices the perfection of wisdom that realizes non-apprehension and is embraced by special methods such as great compassion, does not themselves train in the stage that attains the arhatship of a Hearer, nor do they train in the stage that attains the awakening of a solitary realizer who realizes conditions, but rather, in order to attain the exalted wisdom of the omniscient knowledge of all aspects that without omission contains the purposes of those of the three lineages, they train by following the Dharma through which the Buddhas have gone and will go. ^3-79

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-80]]

Training in whatever path by way of taking to mind the knowledge of all aspects and not apprehending it as truly existent, and not training through mental engagement in the lower vehicles and clinging to true existence—having realized that both of these are not truly established, not training in any path with mental engagement in the lower vehicles and clinging to true existence is to train in the genuine path, the path-knowledge of a bodhisattva. So it is said. ^3-80

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-81]]

Since that path-knowledge of a pratyekabuddha also does not arise without causes and conditions, its cause, the aids to penetration: The meaning taught by the one verse starting with "[When] form increases..." is: Regarding what does a bodhisattva not train and instead discard? They do not train in order to fully apprehend by clinging to ultimate increasing or completely diminishing—that is, decreasing—and to true existence regarding all dharmas on the side of total affliction, such as form, and all dharmas of complete purification, such as the placements of mindfulness. This is because, since all these dharmas are naturally pure, ultimate increasing and decreasing, arising and ceasing, and so forth are not apprehended. They do not train through application in order to completely apprehend through manifest clinging to, or to generate within their own mindstream, any of the various aspects of dharmas from form up to the knowledge of all aspects, but rather they train by way of not apprehending the aids to penetration and so forth; and for the sake of others, by knowing that even the knowledge of all aspects itself lacks a nature, they conventionally fully apprehend it and attain it; and whatever definite emergence—that is, attainment—in that ultimate state of a Buddha there is in dependence upon having practiced the path through the union of method and wisdom, this is the manner in which bodhisattvas who delight in the qualities of the Mahayana train in the path. ^3-81

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-82]]

Here, it is said that the statement "form increases [or] completely diminishes" corresponds to "no increasing or decreasing" from the Ornament; and the statement "not for the sake of apprehending" corresponds to "patience in order not to apprehend". ^3-82

###### **Bodhisattva Path of Seeing** ^3-3-2-1-2-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-83]]

The Bodhisattva's Path of Seeing. Fourth, the bodhisattva's path of seeing: From the Ornament: "Truth and patience regarding truth," up to, "Moments of the path-knowledge itself." Regarding this, there are three [topics]: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. ^3-83

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-4-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-84]]

Definition. First: The definition of the Mahayana path of seeing is a Mahayana clear realization of truth that is embraced by the wisdom directly realizing emptiness within the mindstream of a person possessing it in their own mindstream. ^3-84

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-4-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-85]]

Divisions. Second, if that is divided, there are two: the exalted wisdom of meditative equipoise and the exalted wisdom of subsequent attainment. If divided by object and aspect, there are the sixteen moments of knowledge and patience. ^3-85

###### **Boundaries** ^3-3-2-1-2-4-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-86]]

Boundaries. Third: It exists solely on the path of seeing. Teaching such a Mahayana path of seeing by way of its benefits has two parts: the actual [meaning] and the supplementary meaning. ^3-86

###### **Actual** ^3-3-2-1-2-4-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-87]]

Actual [meaning]. First: It is taught by the two verses starting with "Form is not wisdom..." The meaning: If one asks what that perfection of wisdom which is the nature of the path of seeing is like: Form is not one with the perfection of wisdom which is the subject that realizes its mode of existence, because they are different. Also, the perfection of wisdom which is its subject does not exist in form as a different entity or as abiding differently ultimately, because it is not apprehended as such. Similarly, the aggregate of consciousness, and these aggregates of discrimination, feeling, volition, and compositional factors are not one with wisdom, nor does the perfection of wisdom which is the subject exist in them in the manner of a different entity, because ultimately they are not established as one, different, or as object and subject. This dharmadhātu, which is the object of observation of the Mahayana path of seeing's exalted wisdom of meditative equipoise, is a non-affirming negation devoid of all conventional signs and elaborations of dualistic appearance before the perception of that non-conceptual exalted wisdom of meditative equipoise; such suchness is, for example, similar to how the spatial element is designated upon the mere aspect of negating obstruction and contact, because there is not even a particle of dualistic phenomena such as one and different. ^3-87

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-88]]

As it is also said regarding the statement in the Mother Sūtra, "The perfection of wisdom is to be sought from the chapter of Subhūti": "Kauśika, the perfection of wisdom is not to be sought from form; it is not to be sought elsewhere than form. This is because those dharmas have a single characteristic, which is to say, they have no characteristic," and so forth. This verse specifically highlights and teaches the aspect of the truth of suffering from among the four truths, and the general teaching of the aspects of the four truths is: Among the sixteen objects of observation of the Mahayana path of seeing, such as impermanence, the nature or essence of the truths of suffering is the dharmadhātu empty of being established as a self of phenomena and persons, which is boundless because it is devoid of a beginning and an end and is not divided into directions or categories; and whatever is the nature of the truth of origin—the causes that generate the aggregates and so forth of sentient beings—is also boundless because it is equal in being the dharmadhātu empty of true existence; and just as the spatial element is posited upon the mere negation of obstruction and contact, the nature of the truth of cessation, the dharmadhātu in which obscurations are exhausted, is also boundless. ^3-88

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-89]]

The truth of the path, the wisdom that knows both the worldly conventional truth and the ultimate truth, is also boundless because it is empty of self, since all dharmas are of a single taste in being empty of true existence. ^3-89

###### **Related Points** ^3-3-2-1-2-4-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-90]]

Supplementary meaning. Second, the supplementary meaning: If one asks why all functional things are said to be empty of true existence: Because all dharmas are nothing but mere nominal designations, those who go to the perfection of excellent view must meditate by breaking apart the discriminative awareness that clings to true existence; in order to teach this, it is stated: "Because of perceiving all dharmas as a self, one is left behind here in this place of cyclic existence on the near shore"—thus it was fully proclaimed and spoken by the Guide, the perfectly and fully awakened Buddha; and by breaking apart the discriminative awareness of grasping at a self through individually analyzing wisdom, the uninterrupted path of the path of seeing that directly realizes the lack of true existence acts as the actual antidote to all those [afflictions] to be abandoned by seeing, without exception, and the path of liberation completely abandons them, thereby going to the perfection of excellent abandonment. ^3-90

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-91]]

Therefore, those bodhisattvas on the path of seeing who attain such a view devoid of all discriminative awarenesses that grasp at true existence abide in the word of the Teacher that teaches prophecies by Buddhas saying, "In this life they are irreversible and remember their future births, and having gone to the perfection of the grounds and paths, they will become perfectly awakened as a Buddha named such-and-such," and so forth. If one says, "Because meditating on the path and so forth exist, establishment by nature exists": Even if the Sugata, the Teacher who has become a valid cognition, were to reside for eons equal to the sand grains of the river Ganges and fully proclaim the sound, "Sentient beings exist," how could sentient beings ultimately exist or be born by nature? They would not, because from the very beginning they are unborn by nature and completely pure without defilements entering their nature. Therefore, directly realizing this meaning of not being born by nature is engaging in the supreme perfection of wisdom, which has become the essence of the path of seeing. ^3-91

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-92]]

To teach by way of an example that meditating on the path of seeing will attain the prophecy by the Tathāgata: The Victorious One, the Lion of the Śākyas himself, who was victorious over the enemies of the four māras, spoke thus: "At whatever time I, the Teacher Śākyamuni, came to speak in accordance with this supreme perfection of wisdom which is the essence of the path of seeing, at that time the former supreme being Dīpaṃkara prophesied me, saying, 'You, brahmin boy, will become a Buddha in future times.'" Therefore, having understood that it is implicitly taught that the trainees of future generations will also abide in this perfection of wisdom, be prophesied by Buddhas, and become completely and perfectly awakened, one should rely on exertion in practicing the Mahayana path like the continuous flow of a river. This completes the commentary on the second chapter, the chapter of Śakra, from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, the Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate. ^3-92

###### **Path of Meditation** ^3-3-2-1-2-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-93]]

Path of meditation. Fifth, to teach the function of the path of meditation, it is stated in the Ornament: "Completely pacified and to all..." up to "...awakening and the support itself becoming an object of worship." This has three parts: definition, divisions, and boundaries. ^3-93

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-5-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-94]]

Definition. First: The benefit attained by the power of meditating on the Mahayana path of meditation, which is the method for attaining it, is the definition of the function of the Mahayana path of meditation. ^3-94

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-5-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-95]]

Divisions. Second, if that is divided, there are six: the function of having mastered the mind and completely pacifying pride and so forth; the function of apprehending all sentient beings as spiritual friends and respecting and bowing to them; the function of being victorious over afflictions such as the three poisons; the function of not being subdued by the harm of humans and non-humans; the function of accomplishing perfectly complete awakening; and the function of the place where the support, the bodhisattva on the path of meditation, resides also becoming an object of homage and worship by all beings. ^3-95

###### **Boundaries** ^3-3-2-1-2-5-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-96]]

Boundaries. Third, it exists from the first ground up to the Buddha ground. Moreover, because seeing the result gives rise to seeking it, the function [of the path of meditation] is explained immediately after the path of seeing, without [first] explaining the path of meditation itself: this is taught by the single stanza beginning, "The perfection of wisdom." The meaning is: Whatever son or daughter of good family has faith and respect for this perfection of wisdom—which is the nature of the path of meditation, and in which the protectors of beings, the completely perfect Buddha Bhagavāns, and all the Bodhisattva heirs of the Victors engage and abide during all occasions of meditative equipoise and subsequent attainment—memorizes its text, and comprehends all its meanings through the wisdom that is the nature of hearing, reflecting, and meditating, they cannot be harmed by outer poisons, weapons, fire, and water. Not only that, but they cannot be harmed by afflictions such as the poison-like manifest clinging to true existence, the weapon-like hatred, the fire-like attachment, and the water-like ignorance. Even if māras such as Kāmadeva who cause obstacles to the path, and the hosts of māras or the dark relatives allied with the māras seek an opportunity, they will not find an opportunity, because of the power of dharmatā, and because the Buddhas and bodhisattvas hold them dear like a beloved child and always protect, shelter, and conceal them. Here, by saying "has respect," the function of bowing to all is taught, and it is stated in the Eight Thousand Stanza Perfection of Wisdom: "Bodhisattva great beings are completely tamed and their arrogance is eliminated," and so forth, teaching the function of the path of meditation. ^3-96

###### **Path of Meditation of Belief** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-97]]

Path of meditation of devoted interest. Sixth, the explanation of the path of meditation of devoted interest has three parts: definition, divisions, and boundaries. ^3-97

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-98]]

Definition. First: The Mahayana subsequent clear realization is the definition of the Mahayana path of meditation. ^3-98

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-99]]

Divisions. Second, if that is divided, there are two: the conceptual contaminated path of meditation and the non-conceptual uncontaminated path of meditation. ^3-99

###### **Contaminated Conceptual Path of Meditation** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-100]]

Conceptual contaminated path of meditation. First, there are three: the path of meditation of devoted interest, the path of meditation of dedication, and the path of meditation of rejoicing. ^3-100

###### **Path of Meditation of Belief** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-101]]

Path of meditation of devoted interest. To teach the first of those, the path of meditation of devoted interest, it is stated in the Ornament: "Devoted interest for one's own sake and..." up to "...is asserted as twenty-seven aspects." ^3-101

###### **Definition** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-102]]

Definition. The Mahayana subsequent clear realization that believes in the Mother as the source of the three meanings is the definition of the path of meditation of devoted interest. ^3-102

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-103]]

Divisions. If divided, there are three: the path of meditation of devoted interest for one's own sake, the path of meditation of devoted interest for the sake of others, and the path of meditation of devoted interest for the sake of both. Because each of those has three—small, middling, and great—there are nine, and because each of those is also divided into three—the small of the small and so forth—there are twenty-seven. ^3-103

###### **Boundaries** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-1-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-104]]

Boundaries. The boundaries are from the first to the tenth ground. As for the explanation of three—the path of meditation of devoted interest for one's own sake, for the sake of both, and for the sake of others—although there is no difference in acting for the sake of others by way of intention, they are divided into three from the perspective of how they are practiced in application. ^3-104

###### **Path of Meditation of Belief for One's Own Purpose** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-1-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-105]]

Path of meditation of devoted interest for one's own sake. To teach the first of those, the path of meditation of devoted interest for one's own sake, it is taught with three verses starting with "If some... the Sugata...". The meaning is that these paths of meditation are taught by way of their benefits, and that too is just as it is stated in the Suvarṇaprabhāsa: "The Buddha never passes into nirvana; / The Dharma too will not decline..." and so forth, and "If there is no bone or blood in the body, / How could there be relics?" and so forth. In the definitive meaning, the Buddha does not pass into nirvana and the true Dharma does not decline; until samsara is emptied, the enlightened activities of body, speech, and mind arise continuously, effortlessly, and spontaneously. And although there are no relics or the like in the body of wisdom, one should understand how, because of skill in means for taming disciples and the inconceivable power of aspiration, he emanates inconceivable arrays of marvelous emanations pervading space, such as demonstrating the manner of passing into nirvana in accordance with the devoted perspective of the disciples and leaving relics in the world. Therefore, if "some" make a reliquary stupa that has the essence of the relics of the completely nirvana-ed "Sugata," the materials of that stupa also being made of the seven precious things—gold, silver, lapis lazuli, crystal, emerald, red pearl, and heart of stone—and makes offerings to it; and furthermore, if it is not just one, but one were to make such stupas of the Sugata equal in number to the grains of sand of the "Ganges river," filling countless and boundless buddha fields—thousands of tens of millions and so forth—without exception; taking this as a complete hypothesis—that is, making an example in the manner of taking an extreme hypothesis—if all those sentient beings of however many types reside in those many boundless tens of millions of fields, doing nothing else at all such as commerce or agriculture, were to make offerings without interruption three times "each day and night" with particularly exalted offering substances—"divine flowers and exceedingly fragrant incense and marvelous ointments"—and if they were to make offerings in such a manner for an "eon" or more than an eon, there would certainly be immeasurable merit in that. Yet, compared to that, "if any being," generating devoted interest for the sake of their own excellent benefit, were to write this text of the "perfection of wisdom"—the Mother of the Sugatas which teaches completely both means and wisdom, from which cause the exalted qualities such as the ten powers and four fearlessnesses of the Guides, the Lords of Victors, will "arise"—in a volume and "hold it" in their hands, and make offerings to it with flowers and ointments and pay respect for just a short time, that previous merit of making such reliquary stupas of the Sugata and making offerings to them "would not rival even a fraction" or a number such as a hundred thousand ten million billionths of that latter merit of having written this sutra of the perfection of wisdom in a volume and made offerings to it. ^3-105

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-106]]

The reason for this is because the Sugata and the reliquary stupas of his physical remains arise in dependence on this Mother. ^3-106

###### **Path of Meditation of Belief for the Purpose of Both** ^3-3-2-1-2-6-2-1-1-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-107]]

Path of meditation of devoted interest for the sake of both. To teach the second, the path of meditation of devoted interest for the sake of both, it is taught with four verses starting with "The Victors' wisdom...". The meaning is: This perfection of wisdom, the Mother of the Victors, is a "great knowledge mantra" or "an unsurpassable knowledge mantra" because it pacifies the afflictions in the mindstreams of all, oneself and others. Furthermore, in general, a "knowledge mantra" refers to something like that which is able to pacify harms from humans and non-humans and so forth merely by rishis who have accomplished truthful speech reciting, "May it be pacified!", and although there are also ordinary ones, this perfection of wisdom is, as already explained, a great and unsurpassable knowledge mantra, and because in dependence upon it one becomes equal to the unequaled Buddhas, or because one will attain buddhahood, it is stated to be the "mantra equal to the unequaled". This is because it pacifies all that belongs to the phenomena of physical suffering such as sickness and mental sorrow such as separation from the pleasant for an "immeasurable" multitude of realms of "sentient beings", and causes them to attain buddhahood. "Whichever Buddhas have passed in the past and whichever" presently live and remain "as protectors of the worlds in the ten directions," they too having trained in this knowledge mantra equal to the unequaled have "become unsurpassable kings of physicians" who dispel the sins, obscurations, sufferings, sicknesses, and so forth of all sentient beings. ^3-107

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-108]]

If that is how the Tathagatas are generated, how do bodhisattvas accomplish benefit? "Whichever bodhisattvas" actually accomplish benefit and happiness by applying themselves to sentient beings and "practice" the conduct of the victors' children, such as giving, "with a mind of compassion," all of them having trained in this great knowledge mantra, the perfection of wisdom, all those "wise ones" will in future times "touch," that is, awaken to, "supreme, unsurpassable awakening". Whichever contaminated happiness is conditioned and included in samsara, and whichever unconditioned, undefiled happiness there is in the mindstreams of the noble ones who have attained liberation having abandoned afflictions—one should know that "all" those happinesses of samsara and nirvana "arise from this" perfection of wisdom. ^3-108

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-109]]

What is the example for its being the basis of all mundane and supramundane qualities? "Just as when seeds" of barley and so forth are planted, they "abide in the earth" without deteriorating and shoots and so forth will arise, and having found their own respective assembly of causes and conditions such as water, fertilizer, warmth, and moisture, "the forms of many aspects" of fruits "will grow," similarly, "however many" immeasurable qualities there are of the paths of the five perfections such as giving and of the result, unsurpassable, perfectly complete awakening, "all" of them "arise from this" perfection of wisdom. ^3-109

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-110]]

This is because if they are thoroughly embraced by the wisdom that realizes the agent, action, and object as lacking true existence, giving and so forth become perfections and cause the ultimate result, the Truth Body and Form Bodies, to be accomplished. "For example, whichever path" a wheel-turning king "always goes on, on that very" path "other kings also go" and pay homage, and the seven precious elements of sovereignty such as the precious wheel, accomplished by the power of his merit, "and all the four branches of his army go" without separation; "similarly," from whichever continuum of a person and whichever state of the grounds and paths "this perfection of wisdom," the Mother of the Victors, is present or "goes, from that very" place "all" the paths included in the collections of merit and wisdom such as love, compassion, and bodhicitta "and all the phenomena of the qualities of the Buddha," the result, "will also go," that is, will be attained, and thus supreme beings seeking unsurpassable awakening should have faith in and aspire to this perfection of wisdom, the Mother.
