When this sutra is explained, some will have doubt and hesitation about it, and will think: "In this Mother sutra, the Guide has not thoroughly expressed and not taught even my name, and has not thoroughly expressed my family, the places where I abide, or my clan," and, because they have not been clearly prophesied, they will think, "Therefore, what is the use of listening to this? It is not the speech of the omniscient one." By apprehending the reason for turning away from the Mother, with that erroneous mind they will not listen to that profound dharma and will abandon it; this is the action of a mara. ^3-169

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-170]]

Regarding the falling away of adhering to the reason, that is, the falling away of apprehending excessively, the meaning of the occurrence of the two lines starting with "Thus not knowing..." is: Thus, the perfection of wisdom is the root of the Mahayana path. Those ignorant, bewildered persons who are blessed by maras, having forsaken this precious sutra of the perfection of wisdom which is like the root of a Buddha, will seek out the śrāvaka piṭaka which is like branches and leaves, because they are obscured by bewilderment; this too is the action of a mara. ^3-170

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-171]]

Regarding the falling away from apprehending the supreme vehicle in all aspects, the meaning of the occurrence of the two lines starting with "Even finding an elephant..." is: How is it? Just as a person seeking an elephant, even though they find the actual elephant, leaves it and seeks the elephant's footprints, having heard the perfection of wisdom that teaches means and wisdom completely, one discards practicing it earnestly. Seeking the meaning of sutras of provisional meaning other than that should be known as being like what is taught by the example, so this too is the action of a mara. ^3-171

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-172]]

Regarding the falling away from experiencing the joy of the supreme fruit, the excellent taste, the meaning of the occurrence of one verse starting with "For example, someone [finding food with] a hundred tastes..." is: For example, just as someone having found food possessing a hundred tastes does not experience its taste, but having found supreme food seeks out inferior food such as sixty-day rice, whichever beginner bodhisattva, having found this profound sutra of the perfection of wisdom, does not accomplish the enlightenment of a Buddha, but seeks unsurpassable enlightenment relying upon texts that teach the path that brings about the attainment of the level of śrāvaka and pratyekabuddha arhats is like that above example; this too is the action of a mara. ^3-172

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-173]]

Regarding tasting the flavor of gain, honor, and verses [of praise], and looking for that, the meaning of the occurrence of the two lines starting with "Desiring respect..." is: One will desire fame for oneself and desire to taste the flavor of respect and honor by others, and one will desire to obtain gain such as food and wealth; with a mind possessing the desire to look at forms and various arrangements of objects, one associates and becomes familiar with householders and so forth; and by being distracted by various activities, one falls away from listening to the perfection of wisdom and so forth; this is the action of a mara. ^3-173

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-174]]

Regarding seeking skillful means from the piṭaka taught by maras, which is a non-path, the meaning of the two lines starting with "Having discarded the dharma..." is: Having discarded practicing the dharma of the Mahayana, one engages in various worldly activities that are not dharma; and having forsaken the excellent path that brings about the attainment of Buddhahood, one deviates and goes onto the bad path taught by maras; this is the action of a mara. ^3-174

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-175]]

Regarding the listener having great aspiration but the holder failing through laziness, causing the listener to also lose faith and turn away from listening to the dharma, the meaning of the occurrence of one verse starting with "Whoever at that time..." is: At that time when the explainer of the perfection of wisdom sutra has become distracted by gain, honor, and so forth, whichever listeners with great pursuit go to listen to this holy dharma, having aspiration and faith in the Mother, those listeners see that the speaker of the dharma possesses adherence and attachment to bad activities such as rejoicing in gain, honor, and fame; and from this, lacking joy in listening to the dharma and lacking faith, they become unhappy, thinking "Now I will not request dharma," and they turn back; this too is an action of a mara that acts as an obstacle to listening to the perfection of wisdom and so forth. ^3-175

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-176]]

Regarding summarizing their meaning, the meaning of the occurrence of the four verses starting with "At that time..." is: At that time of listening to the sutra of the perfection of wisdom and so forth, these actions of a mara that act as obstacles to the application, such as not possessing the gathering for explaining and listening, will occur. If one asks what those are: At that time of listening to and reflecting upon this dharma and so forth, those sinful maras who are hostile to the dharma will become tormented by suffering and pain; and they, having disturbed the mind-streams of many bhikshus who hold this sutra, by means of various types of methods of making obstacles, many types of dharma that act as obstacles causing one not to hold and reflect upon the meaning of this perfection of wisdom and so forth, which are not explained here, as well as many others, will also occur; one should know all such obstacles to be from maras. Thus also, from the Gaganagañja Sutra: "Son of noble family, engaging in whatever non-virtuous dharmas and discarding whatever virtuous dharmas should all be known as the actions of a mara." Regarding establishing the reason for there being many obstacles by means of an example: Just as whoever has found invaluable things such as a jewel king, they are rare because they are very difficult to find; and at all times of having found and holding them, harms by enemies, thieves, and so forth are also always abundant; similarly, the supreme dharma of the Mother of the Conquerors, the perfection of wisdom, which is invaluable like a precious jewel, is also very rare, and because the dharma is profound, harms are also always abundant. In order to make obstacles for whichever sentient being of small intellect, a beginner newly entered into the Mahayana, who has not previously found such a very rare and profound dharma as this precious sutra of the perfection of wisdom—that is, who has not brought it into their experience—maras will become joyful and will initiate obstacles. ^3-176

###### **Eliminating Antidotes** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-4-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-177]]

The antidotes that eliminate. Second, regarding the antidotes that eliminate, the meaning of the occurrence of the five lines starting with "The Buddhas of the ten directions..." is: Even though there are many obstacles like that, they will not find an opportunity. If one asks why: Because the Buddhas of the ten directions, whose power that mara exerting himself like that in obstacles is unable to rival even by a fraction, perform the three—guarding, protecting, and hiding—from those obstacles, and thereby apply one to fully holding the perfection of wisdom. From the commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, this is the commentary on the eleventh chapter, the chapter on Maras. ^3-177

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-178]]

If one asks why all the Buddhas keep them in mind and protect them, establishing that very thing by means of an example is: For example, if a mother becomes ill, all of her many sons will be unhappy, and they will perform services for that mother such as seeking a doctor who removes the illness; similarly, all those Bhagavan Buddhas of the world systems in the ten directions also exert themselves in order to remove the actions of maras and keep in mind this generation of bodhicitta and holy wisdom realizing emptiness which have become their own respective generating Mothers. ^3-178

###### **Characteristics of the Application** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-5-0

###### **Brief Explanation** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-5-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-179]]

The defining characteristics of the application. Brief teaching. Fifth, regarding teaching the defining characteristics of the application, from the Ornament: "By what it is characterized, that is the defining characteristic," having briefly taught with one verse starting with that, ^3-179

###### **Extensive Explanation** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-5-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-180]]

Extensive explanation. Regarding the extensive explanation, it is stated: "The occurrence of Tathāgatas..." up to "...is asserted as four defining characteristics." Regarding that, between the two, the defining characteristics and the divisions, ^3-180

###### **Characteristics** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-5-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-181]]

Defining characteristics. First, a bodhisattva's yoga embraced by the union of calm abiding and special insight, which has become a method that acts to attain the perfection of wisdom which is the result to be accomplished by it, is the defining characteristic of the yoga of the path perfection of wisdom. This is the general defining characteristic of the application; as for the definiendum, in general terms it is the yoga of the path perfection of wisdom itself; and here, the "application" in "the defining characteristics of the application" is also the path perfection of wisdom itself, and its defining characteristics are called the defining characteristics of the application. ^3-181

###### **Divisions** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-5-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-182]]

Divisions. Second, regarding dividing that, there are ninety-one, because there are forty-eight defining characteristics of knowledge that define the application of the three knowledges, sixteen distinguishing defining characteristics that define the application which is particularly superior to the application of sravakas and pratyekabuddhas, eleven defining characteristics of action that define the application possessing the special action of accomplishing the welfare of others, and sixteen defining characteristics of entity. Among the three even within the first, regarding teaching the first, the sixteen defining characteristics of knowledge of the knowledge of bases, such as the defining characteristic of knowledge of knowing the occurrence of Tathagatas, the meaning of the occurrence of the two verses starting with "Whichever past..." is: If one calls this perfection of wisdom the Mother of the Conquerors, if one asks what the manner of its being a mother is: just as in the world, because of giving birth to a son and establishing him in his activities, one is posited as a mother, similarly, whatever Buddhas are past because they have already appeared previously, whatever present Bhagavan Buddhas, protectors of the world, there are residing in the fields of the ten directions, and whatever Buddhas will occur in future times, because they all arise from this very perfection of wisdom, it has become the Mother of all the Conquerors of the three times. By this, the defining characteristic of knowledge of knowing the occurrence of Tathagatas is taught. That the Buddhas, having seen the reality of the world—that is, all dharmas subsumed by aggregates, constituents, and sense sources—just as it is, teach it to other disciples, also arose in dependence upon this very perfection of wisdom, the generating Mother of the Conquerors. The limitless infinite behaviors of the minds of others in the realms of sentient beings—withdrawn inward, distracted outward, associated with attachment, free from attachment, and so forth—this Mother entirely teaches to her sons, the noble Buddhas; and since the suchness of the five aggregates of the world, the suchness of sravaka arhats, the suchness of pratyekabuddhas, and the suchness of bodhisattvas, the sons of the Conquerors, are not established as different entities, they are exactly the same; since they are all not other than the dharmadhatu of the same type devoid of truly established entities, they are the same suchness; one should know that such reality, the suchness of the perfection of wisdom, the Tathagatas knew, realized just as it is, and taught to others. By these words of the sutra, one should know that mostly the defining characteristics of knowledge, such as knowing the non-destruction of the world and knowing the behavior of the minds of sentient beings, are explicitly taught in the literal words. ^3-182

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-183]]

Second, regarding teaching the defining characteristics of knowledge of the knowledge of paths, such as emptiness and signlessness, the meaning of the three verses starting with "World of the wise..." is: Whether they are the Bhagavan Buddhas who are wise in the meaning of reality who will occur in the future, or whether they are currently living, maintaining, and abiding in this world system, or have passed into nirvana, this flawless dharmata which is pure from the very beginning, the natural emptiness of dharmas, which cannot be transformed by anyone and is never changing, remains like space as the nature of a non-affirming negation posited merely from the aspect of having negated the object of negation, true establishment; because "bodhisattvas," that is, bodhisattvas, having unmistakably realized this reality, suchness free from proliferations, and practiced it in the union of method and wisdom, one day when their realization reaches culmination, because they have gone or realized the suchness of all dharmas just as it is, the Buddhas have come to be addressed by the name "Tathagata." ^3-183

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-184]]

If one asks, "Having realized all dharmas to be empty by nature, what is the manner in which they accomplish the welfare of sentient beings?", this relying upon and abiding in the perfection of wisdom, which is like a divine grove of joy where infinite uncontaminated joy arises, is the domain of the guides, the Buddhas endowed with the ten powers and four fearlessnesses; for although they completely rescue infinite sentient beings from the many sufferings of samsara such as birth, aging, sickness, and death, and from the three bad migrations, for those Buddhas the apprehension of them as truly existent sentient beings will never arise at any time, but rather, while remaining in meditative equipoise on dharmata, the welfare of others occurs effortlessly and spontaneously. ^3-184

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-185]]

As for the manner of vanquishing opponents and teaching the dharma of profound emptiness, it is, for example, like the king of beasts, the lion, who, relying upon and abiding in the valleys of snow mountains, without fearing any animal, terrifies many wild beasts such as tigers, leopards, bears, and brown bears, as well as minor animals, and lets out a roar; similarly, the lions among men, the Buddhas, also relying on the meaning of profound emptiness, the perfection of wisdom, terrify the many tirthikas who have fallen into the abyss of the view of a self, and roar the wondrous and vast roar of the ultimate dharma in the world. Furthermore, the bodhisattvas themselves attain Buddhahood by perfecting the ways of the three doors of liberation, and although they rescue infinite sentient beings from the sufferings of the bad migrations, because the apprehension of suffering and sentient beings as ultimately established does not arise, complete non-affliction, non-purification, non-entity, non-nature, and non-support and so forth are taught. ^3-185

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-186]]

Third, regarding teaching the defining characteristics of knowledge of omniscience, such as the Tathagata dwelling blissfully in the seen dharma, the meaning of the three verses starting with "For example, abiding in the sky..." is: For example, just as the rays of the sun "abiding in the sky" possess the two functions of drying up whatever moisture there is on this earth and, having dispelled darkness, "thoroughly illuminating forms"; similarly, the sun of speech, the Dharma king, the Bhagavan Buddha, also relying upon the sky-like perfection of wisdom, with the sharp light rays of the profound and vast dharma dries up all the rivers of faults such as craving for existence, and correctly "thoroughly illuminates" all the dharmas that cause all qualities to increase. If one asks, "How does he teach dharmata?" When searching for the reality of the five aggregates with rational knowledge that analyzes the ultimate, because forms are without apprehension, one "does not see" them; likewise, one "does not see feelings," one "does not see the nature of discrimination either," one "does not see compositional factors," and in the perspective of the noble one's pristine wisdom of meditative equipoise that is single-pointedly resting in equipoise on dharmata, seeing dharmata, "not seeing anything whatsoever called consciousness, mind, and intellect"—this is the seeing of the reality of the five aggregates, dharmata, suchness, so the Tathagata taught. Furthermore, if one asks what kind of example this is like: Although worldly people think in their minds "we see the sky" and sentient beings "thoroughly express" this in words, examine this point for yourself: "how can the sky be seen?" Because the sky is merely the elimination of obstructive tangible contact, if the object of negation, obstructive covering, existed, it would be suitable to be apprehended; but not seeing that is expressed as "seeing the sky." Similarly, saying "seeing the dharmata of forms and so forth" means that when searching with rational knowledge, not finding or "not seeing" forms and so forth is what the Tathagata taught as seeing their reality, suchness; and this manner of seeing or perceiving dharmata with the non-conceptual pristine wisdom of meditative equipoise "cannot be expressed by another example." From the Great Commentary, Illumination of the Thought: "It is said that what is not seen is the five aggregates, and what is seen is dharma; that is the meaning of reality, just as it is said, 'Whoever does not see dependent origination, they see dharma.'" And: "Because that pristine wisdom which knows reality just as it is directly sees the reality of the aggregates and so forth; and because the aggregates and so forth not being established in the perspective of seeing is their reality; and because by way of not seeing the aggregates and so forth one must see their reality. For, from the Auto-commentary: 'Because, without touching conditioned entities, only the nature is directly realized, and thus reality is understood, one is called a Buddha.' Thus it is said that the Buddha's pristine wisdom knowing the ultimate, without touching the subjects, understands only dharmata; this is synonymous with the statement that by way of not seeing the aggregates and so forth, their reality is seen. Furthermore, the meaning of the statement, 'Non-seeing is the supreme seeing': not seeing anything at all is not considered seeing, but rather, as explained before, not seeing proliferations is posited as seeing freedom from proliferations; therefore, seeing and not seeing do not refer to a single basis." So it is said. Although Buddhaśrī applied "not seeing forms" and so forth to the defining characteristics of superiority, the Master applying it to the defining characteristics of knowledge accords extremely well with the Twenty-five Thousand and the Eight Thousand, so said Gungtang Jamyang. This is the commentary on the twelfth chapter, "Teaching the World," from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, called "The Jewelled Lamp Clarifying the Reality of Profound Dependent Origination, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate." ^3-186

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-187]]

Second, regarding explaining the defining characteristics of superiority characterized by the inconceivable and so forth, the meaning of the fourteen verses starting with "Whoever sees in that way..." is: whichever bodhisattva "sees" the dharmata of all dharmas through that "seeing" of the "inconceivable and unequaled" and so forth—meaning that in the perspective of seeing dharmata through the pristine wisdom of meditative equipoise of the Mahayana path of seeing, such as the forbearance of the dharma of suffering and so forth, not seeing forms and so forth is seeing their dharmata—furthermore, regarding how the functions of the knowledge of dharma and so forth operate: Just as a "king" remains equanimous, without making effort in activities, and the great ministers perform all the king's activities; similarly, whatever deeds and enlightened activities of the Buddha and the dharmas of qualities of abandonment and realization of solitary realizers and sravakas there are, all of those are performed by the supreme non-conceptual pristine wisdom, the perfection of wisdom. These demonstrate the defining characteristics of the inconceivable and so forth, and to clearly demonstrate this reality, it was said: "How does the great king," without "going to cities" or "to other kingdoms," abide blissfully "in his own kingdom," inside his palace, "gathering" together "all accumulations such as taxes"? Similarly, the pristine wisdom in the continuum of a bodhisattva realizing profound dharmata, such as the forbearance of the dharma of suffering, does not waver and does not go anywhere else, but by gathering all the qualities of the Buddha level in one's own continuum, is a special dharma of wonderful activity. This demonstrates the defining characteristics of gathering the qualities of abandonment and realization of the three kinds of noble ones and so forth. This is the commentary on the thirteenth chapter, "The Inconceivable," from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, called "The Jewelled Lamp Clarifying the Reality of Profound Dependent Origination, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate." ^3-187

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-188]]

Furthermore, if one asks when one obtains the special qualities of a Buddha: Any person who has faith, aspiration, and stable irreversible faith in the Sugatas and bodhisattvas, and out of intention seeks the supreme perfection of wisdom, which is the path to becoming a bodhisattva and a Buddha, and out of application earnestly practices it with joyful effort, then since they possess both method and wisdom, having completely transcended the two straying paths of the sravakas and solitary realizers, they will not be overwhelmed by maras, tirthikas, and so forth, and will swiftly obtain the supreme enlightenment of the Victorious One. Through these, not only do the sravakas and solitary realizers not obtain non-abiding nirvana because they only have one-sided method or wisdom, but they also fall into the extremes of existence and peace; whereas because bodhisattvas practice method and wisdom inseparably—the two truths in the basis, and method and wisdom in combination on the path—they will not fall into the extremes of existence and peace, but will obtain the result, the union of the two kayas; therefore they are supremely elevated above the sravakas and solitary realizers. ^3-188

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-189]]

Therefore, since one cannot accomplish unsurpassed perfect enlightenment with one-sided method or wisdom, establishing through an example the way bodhisattvas must train in a path complete with both method and wisdom is as follows: For example, if a "boat" of "people embarking on the ocean" breaks and falls apart, at that time those people who "do not hold onto a corpse, grass, or a piece of wood" and so forth, remaining as they are, "will be destroyed in the middle of the water," meaning they will die, and will not reach the shore of the ocean; whereas "those who hold onto wood" and so forth, in reliance upon that, "will blissfully reach the other shore of the ocean." "Like that example," any person who possesses faith seeking perfect enlightenment and obtains joyous faith, but who, after finding faith in the perfection of wisdom—the supreme path that brings about the attainment of unsurpassed enlightenment and the mother that generates the Victorious Ones—"does not engage in" practicing it, will not obtain the result of perfect enlightenment; thus, they will constantly wander in the great ocean of samsara, churning with the waves of various kinds of sorrow and suffering such as birth, aging, sickness, death, and mental unhappiness. When that person is embraced by the supreme perfection of wisdom realizing lack of true existence, they will be skilled in the meaning of the abiding nature of entities, and being free from apprehending signs, they will point out ultimate truth without error. ^3-189

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-190]]

If those qualities of method, such as their faith seeking perfect enlightenment and compassion, are entirely embraced by wisdom realizing non-apprehension, the two accumulations of merit and pristine wisdom will be completely perfected, and since they do not fall into the domain of sravakas and solitary realizers, they will swiftly touch, meaning obtain, the supremely wonderful enlightenment of the Sugatas. ^3-190

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-191]]

Furthermore, as another example, if "some ignorant person carries water in an unfired clay vessel"—meaning unbaked by fire—because that vase is fragile and weak, "it will quickly be destroyed." "If one carries water in a fired vase," it will not break or be destroyed along the way without the danger of destruction, and "it will blissfully reach one's home"; "similarly," if any bodhisattva "has much faith" and aspiration seeking perfect enlightenment, "but lacks skill in means" to accomplish it "and the perfection of wisdom" of the path, they will fall into the lower path, degenerate from the Mahayana, and "quickly be ruined." If "faith and wisdom"—meaning both the aspect of method such as faith, and the perfection of wisdom—entirely embrace that person's continuum, they will completely transcend the two levels of sravakas and solitary realizers and "obtain supreme enlightenment," perfect enlightenment. ^3-191

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-192]]

To demonstrate yet another example, it was said: For example, if a "boat" has "not been well prepared" with repairs for leaks and patches and so forth, "it will be destroyed in the ocean together with its goods and merchants," and will be ruined; whereas if that boat is well examined and endowed with good repairs and so forth, it will not be destroyed or damaged in the middle of the water, and the merchants together with their wealth "will blissfully reach the shore of the ocean"; similarly, if any bodhisattva's continuum is "entirely endowed with faith and compassion" and so forth, but "does not rely on the perfection of wisdom" of the path, they "will quickly degenerate from perfect enlightenment"; whereas if that very person endowed with wonderful "faith and so forth is endowed with the supreme perfection of wisdom," they will not be harmed by mental engagements of the lower vehicle, and without degenerating from the Mahayana path, "will touch the enlightenment of the Victorious Ones." ^3-192

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-193]]

Showing that practicing method and wisdom united without separation is of great importance: For example, if "some person reaches the age of one hundred and twenty," reaching the end of their lifespan, and that person who is "aged and suffering from illness and so forth" is "unable to rise from their seat"; and "even if they are able to stand up," they are "unable to go elsewhere," such as a krośa away, "by their own strength," because they are old and decrepit. "If that person is supported on their right and left sides by two people possessing strength and they go," there is no danger of falling along the way, and "they will easily go to the place they wish to go"; "similarly, if any bodhisattva's wisdom" realizing emptiness "is weak," "even if they have fully entered the Mahayana path, they will degenerate" before obtaining the result of unsurpassed enlightenment; whereas if that very bodhisattva's entire path in their continuum is "entirely embraced by" the aspect of method such as faith and "the supreme wisdom" realizing lack of true existence, they will not degenerate from the supreme path and "will quickly touch the enlightenment of the Tathagatas." Therefore, intelligent bodhisattvas must train in the path complete with method and wisdom and seize the royal seat of supreme enlightenment. This concludes the commentary on the fourteenth chapter, the chapter on examples, from the Lamp of Jewels Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Origination, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, a commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection. ^3-193

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-194]]

To demonstrate that practicing the distinguished Mahayana preparation and bringing it to completion depends on properly relying on a Mahayana spiritual friend, three verses beginning with "bodhisattva" were spoken: Thus, any "bodhisattva abiding on the level of initial action"—meaning abiding on the first level, the Supremely Joyous, and so forth—who, out of a completely pure special resolve desiring to obtain unsurpassed perfect enlightenment for the sake of others, "enters into the preparation" of practicing the distinguished Mahayana preparation by means of seeking "the supreme enlightenment of a Buddha"—those good disciples endowed with faith and respect for the guru, who accomplish whatever is taught according to their word, "must constantly rely on learned gurus" whose view and conduct are supremely excellent and who are able to point out the meaning of the sutras of the perfection of wisdom without error. ^3-194

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-195]]

If one asks why one must rely in that way: because "all qualities"—such as the qualities of being learned in the Mahayana path together with its result, and generating those paths in one's continuum—"arise from that" proper reliance on a spiritual friend; and because those spiritual friends "teach" this perfection of wisdom of the Mahayana path. Therefore, "the Victor", the completely perfect Buddha who possesses the supreme of all qualities, "spoke thus", saying that "the dharmas of a Buddha"—the qualities of abandonment and realization such as the powers, the four fearlessnesses, and the eighteen unshared dharmas—"rely on the spiritual friend" of the Mahayana, because there is no basis for those qualities to arise without relying on a spiritual friend. Therefore, out of great affection he instructed us, saying, "One must properly rely on a spiritual friend through both thought and action." This shows the distinguishing characteristic of being entirely embraced by the method of a spiritual friend. If one asks what kind of instructions and subsequent teachings the spiritual friends teach: The summary of the Mahayana path that you must practice is the "six perfections". Instructing, "Properly train in the great waves of practice of the perfections—'giving' (giving away one's body, wealth, and all roots of virtue to others), completely pure 'ethical discipline, and patience, and similarly joyous effort, and concentration, and wisdom'—and entirely 'dedicate' the roots of virtue 'to unsurpassed enlightenment' without being tainted by the stain of mental engagements of the lower vehicle," is the instruction on the aspect of method. Furthermore, the instruction, "Do not adhere to and apprehend that ultimate great enlightenment as supreme by strongly adhering to it as truly existent in the uncontaminated aggregates such as the aggregate of ethical discipline," is the instruction on the aspect of wisdom. "That should be taught to that person of initial action" with aspiration "for enlightenment". This shows the distinguishing characteristic of having no savoring through strong adherence. ^3-195

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-196]]

Thirdly, to demonstrate the distinguishing characteristic of action endowed with the distinguished functions of benefiting, giving happiness, protecting, and so forth, one verse beginning with "acting thus" was spoken. Its meaning is: By the power of having meditated on the distinguished Mahayana preparation, the great being bodhisattvas who "act thus" as previously explained—that is, who excellently point out the instruction on how to practice the Mahayana path in that way—are equal to an ocean of qualities of abandonment and realization, moons of speech endowed with the light rays of the nectar of the profound and vast Dharma. Because they teach the Dharma that is the cause of benefit and happiness, they have become a "refuge" that dispels the suffering of transmigrators; they have become a "defender" that establishes them in the happiness of liberation; and they have become a "place" that dispels negative actions and obscurations, the causes of suffering. "Similarly," they have become a "support" for proceeding to great enlightenment; they have become an "intellect" that distinguishes good and bad; by establishing those sinking in the river of existence on the dry land of the true path of the holy Dharma, they are an "island"; by teaching the meaning of non-arising, they are like a "captain" who desires the goal or accomplishes the goal of entirely drawing them out of the ocean of cyclic existence; they have become a great "lamp" of Dharma that dispels the darkness of ignorance and clearly illuminates the path of enlightenment; and when they speak on the meaning of emptiness, the supreme profound Dharma, they cannot be disturbed by opponents such as demons and extremists, so they are "undisturbed" and without fear of anything, for having found certainty in the profound Dharma through valid cognition, their eloquence in explaining it is completely perfected to be fully victorious over all opponents. Furthermore, "refuge of transmigrators" demonstrates the characteristic of action of the knowledge of bases, and "defender" and so forth demonstrate the characteristics of action of the knowledge of paths and so forth. In the Aṣṭasāhasrikā as well: "Subhūti, whichever great bodhisattva enters for the sake of benefiting the world, enters for the sake of giving happiness to the world, and enters for the sake of compassion for the world, and having manifestly awakened to unsurpassed completely perfect enlightenment, will become a protector of the world." With this and so forth, the first nine functions of benefiting, giving happiness, protecting, and so forth are briefly demonstrated together, and then it is taught extensively for each with: "Subhūti, if one asks how a great bodhisattva, having manifestly awakened to unsurpassed completely perfect enlightenment, becomes a protector of the world, Subhūti..." and so forth. ^3-196

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-197]]

Fourthly, to demonstrate the distinguishing characteristic of essence, six verses beginning with "those possessing great renown" were spoken. Their meaning is: "Those possessing great renown"—who have entered the Mahayana path and whose name "bodhisattva" is of great renown in the fields of the Victors of the ten directions—"don" a great "armor" of extremely difficult deeds, generating the resolve thinking, "I alone shall establish all sentient beings, equal to space, in non-abiding nirvana." Furthermore, they do not don the armor by way of apprehending the essence of dharmas—such as the five aggregates on the side of thorough affliction, the eighteen elements, and the twelve sense sources—as truly existent; they are free from the perception of strongly adhering to the paths of the three vehicles on the side of the remedy, complete purification; and because they abide in the perfection of wisdom without entirely apprehending or grasping any dharma as truly established, they do not degenerate from the perfection of their own purpose, being irreversible from perfect enlightenment, and they do not waver to anything other than the perfection of the purpose of others, the Mahayana path. Since no demons or anyone else can afflict their continuums, they possess the nature of not degenerating from the perfection of the two purposes and being undisturbed. ^3-197

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-198]]

Thus, those bodhisattvas are endowed with such profound and hard-to-fathom qualities, and having generated in their continuums the non-conceptual exalted wisdom obtained by the power of meditating on the preparation—the exalted wisdom free from the elaborations of dualistic appearance—they are free from doubt, hesitation, and double-mindedness regarding the Dharma of the Mahayana, and having found certainty in their own path through valid cognition, they possess the meaning of conviction. By the power of that, having heard the profound perfection of wisdom, their minds do not shrink back from its meaning; and because they have deeply found certainty in their own path and integrated it into experience, they do not rely on others—meaning they do not go wherever led by others—and should be known as irreversible from the Mahayana. ^3-198

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-199]]

This perfection of wisdom, the Dharma of the guide Buddhas, is supremely profound. The abiding nature of dharmas, suchness, is difficult to see as it is by a mind with dualistic appearance, and it is not realized directly by any ordinary beings whose continuums are tainted by true grasping; and it is ultimately unobtainable, and will not become obtainable either. ^3-199

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-200]]

Because this profound dharmatā is thus profound and hard to fathom, when the compassionate Buddha, the Bhagavan who accomplishes the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings, attained great unsurpassed enlightenment and manifestly completely awakened, he thought, 'Who among the assemblies of sentient beings will understand such a profound Dharma that I have realized? It is difficult for those of inferior intellect to realize!' Thinking thus, he showed little concern for teaching the Dharma. As it is said in the Lalitavistara Sutra: 'I have found a Dharma like nectar: profound, peaceful, free from elaborations, luminous, and unconditioned. To whomever I teach it, they will not understand, so I shall remain silent and hide in the forest.' Having spoken thus, he acted in the manner of not teaching the Dharma for a while, and then, based on the supplication made by Mahābrahmā, he turned the wheel of Dharma. ^3-200

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-201]]

If one asks what the reason is that this profound Dharma is difficult to realize: It is like this. Sentient beings, by the power of being habituated to true grasping since beginningless time, to whom whatever appears appears as true and is grasped as truly established, delight in sensory pleasures, which are bases for generating attachment and the rest, and desire objects such as forms and sounds. Abiding in apprehending those as true, they are unskilled in the meaning of suchness, the profound abiding nature, and have become foolish and ignorant, not knowing the profound and vast path as it is, like abiding in the midst of darkness. The dharmas of the path to be obtained abide as lacking true existence, so they do not have even the slightest true establishment, and the non-conceptual exalted wisdom of meditative equipoise does not apprehend them as truly established either, because it unmistakably realizes the meaning of the abiding nature. ^3-201

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-202]]

Thus, those supramundane dharmas are discordant with worldly people and particularly exalted, therefore disputes arise with worldly people; this is because worldly people assert them as truly established, whereas the Buddha teaches them as lacking true establishment. Worldly people, not seeing the abiding mode of dharmas, dispute, whereas the Tathāgata, even though dharmas do not exist ultimately, accepts conventional illusion-like dharmas in accordance with worldly conventions. In the sutra, it is said: "I do not dispute with worldly people; whatever is asserted to exist in the world, I also assert to exist." This is the commentary on the fifteenth chapter, the Chapter on Gods, from the commentary on the Verse Summary of the Precious Qualities Collection called The Jewelled Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising. As for illustrating with an example that the dharmadhātu is indivisible in essence: Just as the element of space is not established as the essence of form and is not divided into directions and categories, so the eastern direction, the southern direction, and likewise the western direction and the northern direction—the four directions being boundless—are infinite, and likewise above and below and the four intermediate directions; in short, however many limitless extents of the ten directions there are, it exists in them as well, because there is nothing not pervaded by space; and in those spaces there is no essence that has become different, nor is there any difference whatsoever of disparate shapes and so forth. In the same way as that example, whatever is the suchness of the dharmas of the past time, and whatever is the suchness of the unarisen, that is, the future, and whatever is the suchness of the present, and whatever is the suchness of persons such as arhats, and whatever is the suchness of all dharmas of the basis, objects of knowledge, and whatever is the suchness of the result, the Victors, and whatever is the suchness of the dharmas of the path—in all of these there is no difference or distinction of disparate essences whatsoever, because they are of one taste in the dharmadhātu, which is merely the negation of the object to be negated, true establishment. This is what is said. ^3-202

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-203]]

Furthermore, "don an armor of difficult deeds" and so forth indicates the essence of the preparation of difficult practice; "free from doubt, hesitation, and double-mindedness" and so forth indicates the preparation of being certain to attain enlightenment; "not realized by anyone" and so forth indicates the preparation of non-apprehension; and the preparation of negating strong adherence and so forth; and "sentient beings" and so forth indicate the distinguishing characteristic of essence of the preparation discordant with all the world, and so forth. Having thus explained the general presentation of the preparation of complete realization, following that, regarding the explanation of the stages of generation in the continuum from the aids to liberation up to the preparation: first, as for the aids to liberation, it is said in the Ornament: "Perfectly giving without characteristics and so forth" up to "are asserted as the aids to liberation." For this there are three: definition, divisions, and boundaries. First, the definition of the Mahayana aids to liberation explicitly taught in this context is: a clear realization of Dharma in the continuum of a bodhisattva who is skilled in accomplishing the omniscient exalted wisdom in their own continuum. Second, if that is divided, there are three that have become that: small, medium, and great. Third, as for the boundaries, it exists only on the Mahayana path of accumulation. ^3-203

###### **Factors Conducive to Liberation** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-6-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-204]]

Aids to liberation. Sixth, as for explaining those very aids to liberation: The meaning of what is stated in the three verses starting with "Sugata enlightenment" is: whatever bodhisattva desires to obtain this ultimate object to be obtained, the enlightenment of a Sugata, which is the dharmatā equal to space, free from diverse dharmas of disparate essences—they must, by being endowed with exceptional methods such as the aspiration seeking perfect enlightenment for the sake of others, also exert themselves in the preparation of the supreme perfection of wisdom that realizes existence and peace as equal in being empty of true establishment, but if this Mother of the Guides, the perfection of wisdom that is the Mother of the Victors, does not exist for them, they will never obtain that great enlightenment. This is because this perfection of wisdom realizing the abiding mode of all dharmas is the supreme cause for obtaining it. For example, if a bird has an extremely large body of one hundred and fifty yojanas, but its wings are ruined and non-existent, so it does not have the strength to be able to fly in the sky, and if that bird thinks, "I will abide here in Jambudvīpa, coming from the abode of the gods of the Thirty-Three," and jumps, at that occasion, there is no other outcome than that the bird will undergo great ruin, such as its body being injured. ^3-204

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-205]]

Similar to that example, even if they accomplish these five perfections, giving and so forth, the path for obtaining the enlightenment of the Victors, by exerting themselves for a long time, for many tens of millions and hundreds of thousands of eons, and even if they continually rely in this world on vast, limitless aspiration prayers, such as ten hundred thousand countless aspiration prayers of a bodhisattva, they will not be able to obtain great enlightenment without a complete set of method and wisdom; for if they become separated from exceptional method and wisdom, such as the immeasurable skillful methods of dedicating roots of virtue to the omniscient exalted wisdom and so forth, and the wisdom that realizes all dharmas as equal in being empty of true establishment, they will fall to the level of a śrāvaka, just as the bird with a large body but no feathers or wings jumps and falls downward, experiencing suffering. Therefore, one who desires to obtain great enlightenment, having become skilled in the five objects such as faith and so forth, must train in the path complete with method and wisdom. This is what is said. ^3-205

###### **Factors Conducive to Penetration** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-7-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-206]]

Aids to penetration. Seventh, as for the aids to penetration: it is said in the Ornament: from "Faith focused on the Buddhas and so forth" up to "should be known by the maturing factors and so forth." There are three: definition, divisions, and boundaries. First, the clear realization of meaning of the Mahayana explicitly taught in this context, which is mainly distinguished by the factor of method, is the definition of the Mahayana path of preparation explicitly taught in this context. If divided, there are four Mahayana paths of preparation taught here: heat and so forth. As for the boundaries, it exists only on the Mahayana path of preparation. To teach such aids to penetration, the meaning of the one verse starting with "Buddha vehicle" and so forth is: If one asks how a bodhisattva who desires to definitely emerge into, that is, reach, great enlightenment by relying on this perfection of wisdom—the path of penetration to the unsurpassed "Buddha vehicle", the great path that is the single trajectory of all the Victors—should engage in the path of the Mahayana: Since they must train in the six perfections and so forth after generating love and great compassion focused on all sentient beings, first, meditating on equanimity with a mind not divided into factions by attachment and aversion towards all infinite beings—an "equal mind"; and abiding in the recognition of older men and women as parents, the recognition of peers as brothers and sisters, the recognition of younger ones as sons and daughters, the recognition of others as friends and companions, and the recognition of relatives and blood relations and so forth; and with a mind of ultimate benefit such as desiring to establish them in enlightenment and a mind of ultimate benefit such as desiring happiness in the short term and desiring happiness in the short term, with a loving mind one should exert oneself, that is, strive, for the welfare of sentient beings; and without the stubbornness of anger and harm, and without deceit, with an upright continuum, one should teach the Dharma through speech and speak agreeable, gentle words, and one should also perform physical actions of love that benefit others; thus, discarding one's own welfare, one engages in accomplishing all the benefit and happiness of others without exception. This explicitly teaches for the most part the focal objects of the small, medium, and great Mahayana paths of preparation of heat, and implicitly implies the aspects of peak and so forth. From the commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection called The Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, this is the commentary on the sixteenth chapter, Suchness. ^3-206

###### **Sangha of Irreversible Bodhisattvas** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-8-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-207]]

The saṃgha of irreversible bodhisattvas. Eighth, to teach the saṃgha of irreversible bodhisattvas: it is said in the Ornament: from "Starting from the branches of penetration..." up to "...here today the assembly of irreversibles." For this there are three: definition, divisions, and boundaries. First, the bodhisattva who has attained any of the forty-four signs, such as the reversal of manifest clinging to the true existence of forms and so forth, is the definition of the saṃgha of bodhisattvas who have attained the signs of irreversibility. Second, if divided, there are three: the saṃgha of bodhisattvas of sharp faculties who attain the signs from the path of preparation, those of medium faculties who attain them from the path of seeing, and the saṃgha of bodhisattvas of dull faculties who attain the signs from the path of meditation. Third, the boundaries: it exists from the path of preparation of heat up to the end of the continuum. Regarding that, requesting the general definition or signs of irreversibility: the meaning taught by the one verse starting with "To the protector of the world" and so forth: To the Buddha, the Bhagavan, the protector of the three worlds below the earth, upon the earth, and above the earth, the Elder Subhūti thoroughly asked a question: "Please teach me what the signs and marks are like of the bodhisattvas of great power possessing immeasurable ocean-like qualities being 'without afflictions', that is, not deteriorating from having abandoned afflictions. How will it be known that the bodhisattvas of great power in accomplishing the two purposes will not turn back again from perfect enlightenment, that is, that they are irreversible bodhisattvas? Thus, please may the Victor prophesy to me just a fraction of their immeasurable signs and qualities." This was requested. ^3-207

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-208]]

In explaining the actual signs of irreversibility, there are the signs of irreversibility of those on the path of preparation, those on the path of seeing, and those on the path of meditation. First, the meaning taught by the three verses starting with "cognition of difference" and so forth is: The answer spoken to what was thus requested by Subhūti is: A bodhisattva on the path of preparation who realizes profound dharmatā as it is, is free from the cognition apprehending the dharmadhātu of all phenomena—such as both saṃsāra and nirvāṇa—as different in essence, and by realizing them as of a single taste in being empty of true existence, mostly reverses manifest apprehension of true existence with respect to phenomena like forms and so forth; this is the sign of irreversibility. And, they perfectly speak words endowed with scripture and reasoning that unmistakably teach the meaning of the profound abiding nature, emptiness; and, having known the qualities of the Three Jewels and attained the faith of conviction, they do not go for refuge to, nor designate as teachers, outside ascetics and other brahmins; and, being learned in and having conviction in the phenomena of cause and effect and dependent arising, they abandon the manifest ten non-virtues and so forth, therefore at all times they have abandoned the three lower realms and the eight non-leisures illustrated by them; and, those wise ones themselves constantly abide in the bodily paths of the ten virtues, and manifestly strive to establish other trainees in them; and, without a mind looking for material things such as gain, honor, and wealth, they perfectly teach others the Dharma of the six perfections and the four means of gathering and so forth; and, having found certainty in the profound Dharma, they uniquely rejoice in profound dharmatā and direct their minds one-pointedly without doubt; and, because all the actions of their three doors have engaged solely in benefiting others, they constantly speak gentle words and are endowed with the three actions of loving body, speech, and mind; and, being habituated to mindfulness, introspection, and carefulness, when going to other places, walking nearby, sleeping, and sitting squatting, in all occasions of the four postures, having abandoned carelessness, they are well endowed with mindfulness and introspection, that is, thus even when going on a path, they go while looking with their eyes only a yoke's length ahead, and are free from mental confusion such as losing their way or trampling on black snakes by turning back; and, with completely pure ethics and not engaging in actions deprecated by the noble ones, they make all outer and inner behaviors pure and have abandoned wrong livelihood, thus by wearing pure clothes and so forth, their three doors of body, speech, and mind are free from the stains of faults, completely pure, beautiful, and agreeable; and, types of worms do not arise on their bodies; and, they do not desire gain and so forth through means such as taking up ascetic qualities; and, just as when following a supreme bull in a herd, no harm or injury occurs on the path and one meets with excellence, they become a supreme bull among holy beings who go straight on the middle path free from the two extremes of permanence and annihilation; and, not only do they constantly not harm others, but they dispel others' suffering and accomplish their benefit and happiness and so forth; because of desiring to practice the Dharma of the Mahayana, through the power of affection even if they had to stay in the fires of hell for the sake of others, they would not be afraid and would desire to stay there; and, since they have deep conviction in the view realizing the abiding nature, they cannot be led astray to other paths by other teachers, and having recognized as māras the māras who show pseudo-paths that are wrong paths, diverting them from the Mahayana path, since they are not harmed by the actions of māras, they have passed beyond the domain of māras and, having found certainty by their own power regarding method and wisdom, they do not depend on others; and, although they enter into or practice the concentrations of the four meditative absorptions, they do not abide in those actual absorptions of concentration through tasting their flavor and so forth, and all the activities of their three doors become conduct pleasing to the Buddhas—these and so forth should be known as the signs of irreversibility from the Mahayana. Thus it is said. Furthermore, regarding those signs, from the Eight Thousand Stanza: "Then the venerable Subhūti said this to the Bhagavan..." ^3-208

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-209]]

By saying "Bhagavan, what are the aspects, what are the signs, what are the marks of an irreversible great bodhisattva?" and so forth, having taught the reversal of the cognition of form and so forth; and by saying "entering into the Dharma of the nature of suchness and abiding in suchness" and so forth, those such as having no doubt and so forth are taught. Second, in explaining the signs of irreversibility of those on the path of seeing, it is taught by three verses starting with "[not] desiring fame". Furthermore, although it appears to accord with the statement by Jñānapāda that the one quarter-verse starting with "concentration" teaches the sign of having completely exhausted the auxiliaries as the sign of irreversibility for one on the path of seeing, here it is explained in accordance with the Master's [Haribhadra's] statement that it is taught by the lines downwards from "not desiring fame": A bodhisattva abiding on the path of seeing knows entities to be empty of true existence like an illusion, and by reversing the imputed apprehension of true existence that apprehends form and so forth as truly existent, they themselves are "not desiring" good "fame"; and, because special love and compassion have arisen in their minds, they are free from being mentally disturbed by anger or enwrapped by it; and, because they are skilled in the means of taming the mind, even if they become householders for the sake of others, they are constantly unattached to all desired entities and give all their possessions to others; and, having realized selflessness and come under the control of compassion, with the character of a holy being, having abandoned wrong livelihood and wrong margins of action, for the sake of their own "livelihood" they do not seek enjoyments through fearsome ripened results such as taking life, flattery, hinting, and so forth; and, for their own sake they do not perform fierce mantras and sorcery actions that harm others; and, by the power of attachment they are "not" performing mantras to subjugate women or bring them under their own control; and, for the sake of fame and so forth, they do not prophesy regarding an embryo endowed with karma of the desire realm, saying "this will be a boy" or "this will be a girl" and so forth, and thus they are endowed with the sign of irreversibility of being completely pure of wrong livelihood and wrong margins of action; and, by means of being completely secluded from the elaborations of hustle and bustle that distract the three of body, speech, and mind, they manifestly strive in practicing the supreme perfection of wisdom; and, because their mindstreams are tamed by love and compassion, wherever they abide, they are free from mutual fighting and dispute and so forth, and their loving minds desiring to establish others in happiness are extremely stable, not stolen away by conditions; and, in order to accomplish the benefit of others, they desire to attain for themselves the state of a Buddha who knows all aspects of knowables, and they constantly aspire for the holy Dharma of the Buddha's teachings and are endowed with minds delving into it or striving in its practice; and, free from birth under the power of karma and afflictions, having completely abandoned birth in barbarous border regions where the fourfold retinue does not travel, and in remote lands where the Dharma does not spread, they obtain only bodies endowed with the Dharma; and, having found conviction of belief in the realizations or grounds they have themselves attained, without doubt or hesitation they are unwavering and unwaning, constantly taught to be like Mount Meru; and, for the sake of the Dharma they even cast away their own lives, and manifestly strive in the yoga of practicing the Mahayana—these wondrous qualities should be known as the signs of irreversibility from perfect awakening for bodhisattvas abiding in the dharmas of seeing; furthermore, the signs taught here should also be known through reasonings such as from the Pramāṇavārttika: "Effects arise from a collection of causes"; and those signs are also taught in the Eight Thousand Stanza: from "Furthermore, Subhūti..." up to "...if they desire, they enter into the first meditative absorption" and so forth. This is the commentary on the seventeenth chapter, the Chapter on the Aspects of Irreversibility, from the commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection (Ratnaguṇasañcayagāthā) titled Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising. ^3-209

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-210]]

Following that, although it is necessary to teach the defining characteristics of an irreversible one on the path of meditation, in order to ascertain the specific quality—the sign of irreversibility—it must be preceded by ascertaining the basis of the quality, the path of meditation; thus, there are two parts: the explanation of the path of meditation and the explanation of the signs of irreversibility of those on the path of meditation. Regarding the first, there are two: the explanation of the profound feature of the path of meditation and the explanation of the basis of the quality, the path of meditation. As for teaching the first, the meaning of the three verses spoken starting with "Form and feeling..." is taught here in accordance with the Ornament of Clear Realization: "The path of meditation is profound..." and so forth: Dharmas such as form seem to exist for the innate, unanalyzing and unexamining mind, but when the rational consciousness that analyzes the ultimate clears away the individual parts of physical things and the individual momentary parts of non-physical things, their own essence left behind is not found, thereby freeing from the extreme of permanence; and because all actions and agents are suitable within illusion-like appearances while being empty, it frees from the extreme of annihilation, and since appearance and emptiness mutually assist each other, emptiness is profound. Similarly, "form" is also "profound" because it is ultimately unproduced; and likewise, by analogy, "feeling", "discrimination", "volition", and "consciousness" are also profound, because they are empty of their own essence and merely imputed by conceptualization. Because they are isolated from an inherent nature, they ultimately lack "marks" such as being suitable as form and so forth, and because "prayers" of manifest attachment to the result are completely "pacified", the meaning of the abiding nature is hard to fathom. For example, when seeking to reach the bottom of the "ocean" with an "arrow", one does not find a final bottom such that one can say "it is like this"; in the same way, when examined and analyzed by the "wisdom" that analyzes suchness, the bottom of the aggregates of "form and so forth" is "not found". Because the object, emptiness, is profound, the subject that realizes it, the Mahayana path of meditation, should also be known to be profound. ^3-210

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-211]]

Asking what superior result there is for that profound path of meditation: "Whichever bodhisattva"—since the abiding nature of the dharmas to be realized, emptiness, is extremely "profound" as explained before, and in the perspective of whoever "abides" on the Mahayana path of meditation of the "supreme vehicle", with "no attachment" through manifest grasping by means of the non-conceptual exalted wisdom of meditative equipoise that is the subject of the ultimate, realizes this profound dharma where no conventional elaborations such as aggregates, elements, and sense sources exist—thinking "what could be superior to or more supreme than their perfect accomplishment of immeasurable merit, for it is immeasurable?", when asked this: ^3-211

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-212]]

To teach the answer to that: "For example, a person engaging in the dharma of desire" for inferior objects, "having made an appointment with a woman", during the time before meeting her, thinking "will she come or not?" and so forth, "however many moments occur in various conceptualizations of the mind for that person in that single day", that is how many "aeons" of virtue from accumulating the collections of merit and wisdom "the bodhisattvas" who "perform yoga for a single day" on the path of meditation "will attain." Having thus taught the profound features... ^3-212

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-213]]

Regarding the second, the explanation of the basis of the quality, the path of meditation: Although there are nine antidotes to the nine conceptualizations of grasping at true existence, here they are condensed into three. To teach the first, the small path of meditation, the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "The bodhisattva..." is: Whichever "bodhisattva," "for many thousands of tens of millions of aeons," with the mental engagement of seeking the enlightenment of the inferior vehicle, "gives gifts" to śrāvaka arhats and pratyekabuddhas and so forth or "maintains completely pure ethical discipline," still, for whichever other "child of the victorious ones" who possesses the meditation of the nature of the small path of meditation, and having arisen from that in the post-meditative state "explains" or states "merely a single" true nature whose subject matter is the "supreme perfection of wisdom," that former giving and ethical discipline "cannot match even a fraction of that" excellent collection. ^3-213

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-214]]

To teach the medium path of meditation, one verse starting with "The bodhisattva..." is spoken: Whichever "bodhisattva" meditates on the "supreme perfection of wisdom" in the meditative equipoise of the path of meditation, and arising from that in the post-meditative state "explains" or expresses the dharma of profound emptiness that is concordant with the dharma they meditated upon, unstained by the conception of desiring gain, respect, and fame, and "completely dedicates" that virtue of explaining the dharma as a cause for supreme enlightenment for the sake of beings, "there is no merit equal to that" virtue "in the three worlds" below the earth, on the earth, and above the earth. ^3-214

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-215]]

To teach the great path of meditation, the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "That merit also..." is: Although there is conventionally immeasurable merit in explaining the profound dharma concordant with what one meditates on in that way, there is not even the slightest ultimately established thing; thus, "that merit" arising from explaining the dharma is also "thoroughly known" as "hollow" because it is empty of being established by its own defining characteristics, and likewise "empty" because it is empty of inherent nature and empty of the true existence that is the object of negation, and "insubstantial" because it is not established from its own side and arises from the accumulation of causes, conditions, and dependent origination, and "coreless" because its nature is empty of true existence like an illusion. Having thus completely understood, if one "practices the profound practice" of the "perfection of wisdom of the sugatas," which has the nature of the great path of meditation, at the time of engaging in such practice, one "completely grasps" or generates "immeasurable, incalculable merit." To anticipate the doubt that thinks, as raised in the dispute from the Ornament of Clear Realization: "For an inexpressible entity, decrease and increase are not suitable," and so forth, because all dharmas are equal in having no inherent nature within the dharmadhātu, the exhaustion of objects of abandonment and the arising of antidotes are untenable, so therefore the levels of supreme and inferior realizations of the children of the victorious ones are also illogical, the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "Explained by the buddhas..." is: "Whatever dharmas have been spoken" or taught by the "buddhas, the transcendent conquerors," explaining the meaning in brief, and teaching the meaning of the words extensively by combining examples and meaning, and teaching it with extreme elaboration through infinite reasonings of refutation and proof, "all these" are known as ultimately lacking any object of expression or means of expression to be apprehended, and being conventionally expressed as mere names; and having understood this, even if one were to "express" the profound abiding nature, emptiness, through countless many reasonings of refutation and proof "for many tens of millions of billions of aeons," still, since by that "the dharmadhātu of dharmas" such as the path of meditation "does not decrease" or exhaust, and does not even slightly "increase" or expand, they dispute, thinking, "the exhaustion of objects of abandonment and the arising of antidotes are also untenable." In answer, if one establishes that ultimately there is no increase or decrease, since that is exactly what we assert, it would be establishing what is already established, and... ^3-215

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-216]]

To teach that conventionally, because the dharmas of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa are not permanent, increase and decrease are also suitable like an illusion, the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "Of the victorious ones..." is: Although those dharmas that are whatever path of the Mahāyāna, such as the six perfections which are the sole path to be traveled by the "victorious ones," are indeed "completely proclaimed" and expressed as mere names as being established by way of their own entities; nevertheless, for a bodhisattva who conventionally "completely dedicates" all roots of virtue to unsurpassable enlightenment and lacks the conceit of adhering to the result to be attained as truly existent and the pride of grasping it as supreme, conventionally, as mere illusions, the cause and effect of dependent origination are infallible, and because they have practiced the path that unites method and wisdom, they "do not decline" from the Mahāyāna, and not only is there nothing untenable about them "touching" the supreme "enlightenment of a buddha," it is highly logical and possesses valid proofs. This concludes the commentary on the eighteenth chapter, on emptiness, from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection titled The Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Clarifying the Suchness of Profound Dependent Origination. ^3-216

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-217]]

If they say: Even conventionally, it is illogical to attain enlightenment by realization increasing higher and higher; because each individual mind occurring earlier or later lacks the capacity, it does not attain enlightenment; and it is not by all the earlier and later minds gathering simultaneously either, because it is impossible for all earlier and later ones to occur at the same time; nor is it in dependence upon the earlier ones, because without the earlier ones the later ones do not arise - anticipating this dispute from within and teaching the answer to it by way of an example, the meaning taught by the five verses starting with "Wick and oil..." is: "For example," when a "wick and oil" and a blazing "flame of a lamp" meet, that "first moment of the flame alone" does not burn the wick "completely," yet without that first moment of the flame, that wick also does not burn; and the "last" meeting of the flame "alone" also does not burn that wick, yet without the last flame, that wick also does not burn; however, one should understand that from the continuum of all the earlier and later moments of the flame, the wick comes to be completely burned. ^3-217

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-218]]

Applying that very example that was taught to the meaning of the context, the way of attaining enlightenment: "Likewise," by the "first moment of the mind" of generating bodhicitta "alone," one does not "touch" supreme "enlightenment," yet "without that first mind," one is also unable to "touch that enlightenment"; and by whichever mind is the "last moment" of that mind's continuum "alone," one does not attain the "great enlightenment" where all objects of abandonment are pacified, yet "without that last moment," one is also unable to attain that enlightenment. However, by the true nature of dependent origination, having arisen gradually through the continuum of the earlier and later generations of mind, enlightenment is attained. Having thus generally taught that the collection of earlier and later moments of the path to enlightenment is the cause of enlightenment, now to specifically teach that the first moment of the path to enlightenment is also the principal cause, like a seed, it is said: Thus, "from a seed" a sprout arises, and from that a stalk, flowers, and fruits arise, and at the time its own fruit is established, that "seed is also destroyed," yet it is not the case that the tree that is its fruit would also become non-existent. Just as it is not like that, "likewise" here in the context of the path, the "first mind" initially generated is also a "cause for enlightenment"; at the time enlightenment is attained, it is also destroyed, but it is not the case that the fruit of enlightenment does not exist; rather, one becomes "manifestly and completely enlightened." ^3-218

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-219]]

To teach that the second moment is also a cause: thus, the way a result arises in dependence upon a cause is that having "found the seed itself" that possesses the capacity to generate its own result, its result, "various grains" such as "rice" and so forth, "arises," and when the result of that seed ripens, "at that" time that seed does "not exist," yet its own continuum is "also not non-existent." "Likewise, this enlightenment of the victorious ones is also" something that will "arise" from the complete perfection of the aspects of the path such as the generation of bodhicitta, and since it too is devoid of an entity's nature if one searches for the designated object of a conventional designation, one should logically understand it as arising like an illusion as an unanalyzed, unexamined convention. ^3-219

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-220]]

Having extensively taught the way of attaining enlightenment, to summarize the meaning: "For example," as a "pot is filled by the accumulation" of many "drops of water," meaning that it is completely filled gradually, little by little, from the first drop to the last, "likewise," the "first generation of mind" during the state of the path of accumulation "is also" a cause of supreme enlightenment; however, it is not by that alone, but by the later ones, such as the generation of bodhicitta with pure special resolve, traversing the ten grounds gradually starting from the stage of action through belief, and in dependence upon the qualities of the grounds and paths increasing higher and higher, that one becomes a buddha by way of the completion of all white qualities. So it is said. Having thus finished extensively teaching the basis of the attributes, the path of meditation, along with the incidental digressions, ^3-220

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-221]]

Second, to teach the actual signs of irreversibility because of abiding on the path of meditation, which is the specific attribute that is the main subject of the current context: the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "Emptiness, signlessness..." is: Thus, "engaging" in the meaning of having realized the profound dharmas of realizing existence and peace as empty of true existence, the realization of the three doors of liberation—such as "emptiness" due to all dharmas not being established by their own entities, "signlessness" due to causes not being truly established, and "wishlessness" by way of not adhering to results as truly established—a bodhisattva endowed with special methods such as great compassion does "not touch" the one-sided peace of nirvāṇa; and although they take rebirth in saṃsāra for the sake of others, because they directly realize existence as empty of true existence and do "not engage in the signs" of manifest adherence, they do not abide in either extreme of existence or peace. This is, "for example," "like" an "expert boatman" carrying goods, going back and forth across the ocean, who neither abides on the two extremes of the far shore or the near shore, nor abides in the ocean. So it is said. ^3-221

###### **Application of the Equality of Existence and Peace** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-9-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-222]]

The training of the equality of existence and peace. Ninth, to teach the training of the equality of existence and peace: From the Ornament: "Because dharmas are like dreams," up to, "The reply is exactly as explained." So it is said. Regarding this, there are three [topics]: the definition, the divisions, and the boundary of the grounds. First: the yoga of the pure grounds that has exhaustively destroyed the opportunity for manifest true apprehension to arise during the state of subsequent attainment, by way of being embraced by the wisdom that directly realizes existence and peace as empty of true existence—that is the definition of the training of the equality of existence and peace. Second, if that is divided, there are the three wisdoms of the three pure grounds. Third, the boundary of the grounds: it exists only on the three pure grounds. Among the three trainings that accomplish the three bodies, to explain the training of the equality of existence and peace, which accomplishes the dharmakāya: the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "A bodhisattva engaging thus..." is: "Bodhisattvas engaging" in the path of familiarizing with and directly realizing the equality of existence and peace as empty of true existence "thus", do not have the manifest adherence of thinking, "'I' will be 'prophesied' to unsurpassable enlightenment 'by a victorious one'" endowed with the ten powers, nor the conceited mind of true adherence thinking, "I will touch supreme enlightenment," and if one analyzes and searches with the rational consciousness that investigates the ultimate, one understands that among these dharmas subsumed by both existence and peace, there is "nothing whatsoever" to be removed or abandoned, etc.; having understood this, lacking any unendurable mental "fear" towards the object of abandonment, saṃsāra, and the object of attainment, enlightenment, existing like illusions, and possessing confidence, "engaging thus is engaging" in the profound "wisdom of the Sugatas," the path of realizing existence and peace as empty of true existence. So it is said. ^3-222

###### **Application of Purifying the Field** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-10-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-223]]

The training of pure lands. Tenth, the training of pure lands: From the Ornament: "Likewise the worlds of sentient beings," up to, "The buddhafields are pure." So it is said. Regarding this, there are three [topics]: the definition, the divisions, and the boundary of the grounds. First: the yoga of the pure grounds in which roots of virtue, such as aspiration prayers that accomplish the special field where oneself will become a buddha, become potent in one's own continuum—that is the definition of the training of pure lands.
