Second, if that is divided, there are two: the training of purifying the environment of the buddhafields, and the training of purifying the inhabitants. Third, the boundary of the grounds: it exists on the three pure grounds. To explain that very training of pure lands, which accomplishes the saṃbhogakāya: the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "In the wilderness of the world..." is: Although seeing that in this "world"-system of the ten directions there are faults of the environment—such as fearful paths of solitary "wildernesses" that are far from towns, devoid of water and food, and filled with fierce wild animals—and seeing the inhabitants, the sentient beings, in "wildernesses" of famine, destitute and impoverished regarding food, clothing, and resources, as well as possessing inner afflictions and various outer diseases such as wind, bile, and phlegm, one knows that all of them are empty of true existence and "like illusions," and is thus "without fear"; and "then" puts on the great armor of the thought to purify "buddhafields" "thus": "Alas! These sentient beings, having not accumulated merit due to bad karma, are tormented by immeasurable diseases and suffering; how pitiful! For the sake of all these and other sentient beings, I will accomplish a special buddhafield where I will become a buddha, lacking any suffering or faults of an impure environment, with such perfect excellence of environment and inhabitants. And for that purpose, I will always strive to accumulate the collections of merit and wisdom, such as the six perfections, until the limit of future eons. By way of thoroughly knowing them to lack inherent existence, I will not generate even an atom of a completely weary mind toward training on such a path, just 'like' the sister, the 'goddess Gaṅgā'"—putting on the armor of such a thought, one practices through application. So it is said. From the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection entitled The Jewelled Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate Ones that Illuminates the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, this is the commentary on the nineteenth chapter, the Goddess Gaṅgā. ^3-223

###### **Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-224]]

The training of skillful means. Eleventh, the training of skillful means: From the Ornament: "This object and practitioner," up to, "Skillful means has ten aspects." So it is said. Regarding this, there are three [topics]: the definition, the divisions, and the boundary of the grounds. First: the yoga of the pure grounds in which enlightened activity is spontaneously accomplished by way of pacifying coarse effort—that is the definition of the training of skillful means. Second, if that is divided, there are ten: the training of skillful means of overcoming the four māras, that of not abiding in the ultimate but abiding in the conventional, that of others' benefit being propelled by the force of previous aspiration prayers, that which is uncommon, that of non-attachment, that of non-apprehension, that of signlessness, that of wishlessness, that of the sign of non-retrogression, and the training of skillful means that is immeasurable. Third, the boundary of the grounds: it exists only on the three pure grounds. To explain that very training of skillful means, which accomplishes the nirmāṇakāya: to teach the training of skillful means in general by way of teaching the object of skill: the meaning of the one verse spoken starting with "A bodhisattva..." is: "Bodhisattvas," the great beings, engage and abide in the Mother of the Victorious Ones, the perfection of wisdom, through hearing, thinking, and meditating, and how do they do so? Having directly known that all these dharmas, such as the aggregate of form, are empty of being inherently established from the beginning and lack true arising, they rest evenly and one-pointedly in dharmatā, suchness; but by the power of compassion, "in the meantime", having actualized the ultimate limit taught here, the benefit for others is uninterrupted; arising from meditative equipoise, during the state of subsequent attainment when not in meditative equipoise, they accomplish the immeasurable benefit of the realms of sentient beings to be tamed. Because they continuously engage in great compassion for them, they do not abide in the expanse of peace; and even during that interval of engaging in accomplishing the immeasurable benefit of sentient beings, they do not completely degenerate from the qualities of a buddha, such as the ten powers and four fearlessnesses, but through the skillful means that unites wisdom and compassion, they "will attain the qualities of a buddha." So it is said. ^3-224

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-225]]

Among the ten actual trainings of skillful means: ^3-225

###### **Application of Skill in Means Conquering the Four Maras** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-226]]

The training of skillful means of overcoming the four māras. To explain the training of skillful means of overcoming the four māras: the meaning of the three verses spoken starting with "For example, a person..." is: among the explanations of the manner of training in the skillful means that unites compassion and wisdom by way of multiple examples, the first is: "For example, a person" who is skilled in the means of taming enemies, protecting his own side, and so forth, who possesses all qualities such as great courage, and who is endowed with the strength and capability to accomplish great deeds, has exerted himself in deeds that cannot be overcome by others. What is he like? Like this: he has become knowledgeable in the deeds or methods of arts such as running fast and jumping; he has perfected archery, such as shooting arrows, and many worldly fields of craft; and knowing how to emanate or produce various illusions, one who wishes to thoroughly accomplish the benefit of beings, such as protecting his relatives, has completely gathered and led his relatives, including his father, mother, and wife, and travelled on a road through an unpopulated wilderness where there are many who cause injury, such as enemies, robbers, and thieves who would harm him. And when those enemies and so forth have come there, without forsaking his relatives, by means of great courage, that person emanates many people who are extremely brave and possess the valor capable of attacking the enemies. Having ensured that harm from enemies and so forth does not arise, he goes safely in the direction he wishes to go, and also returns safely to his own home. Just like that example, at the time of practising the path and accomplishing the benefit of beings, a "bodhisattva" who is "skilled in means" continually generates great compassion for the realms of sentient beings and accomplishes immeasurable temporary and ultimate benefit and bliss. Having completely passed beyond the hindrances to the path—the "four māras", such as the māra of the son of the gods, and the two [vehicles] of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas—and abiding in the supreme samādhi that causes the attainment of buddhahood, he does "not touch enlightenment" by resting evenly in such a way that he does not rise again from the expanse of peace prematurely. Thus, it directly teaches that noble bodhisattvas do not actualize the ultimate limit taught here without having [completed] the three—perfecting, ripening, and purifying—and thereby implicitly indicates that they do actualize it after having [completed] the three—perfecting, ripening, and purifying. ^3-226

###### **Non-Abiding Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-227]]

The training of skillful means of non-abiding. To explain the second, the training of skillful means of non-abiding: two verses are spoken starting with "The wind supported by space..." It is like this: when the world is formed, first the maṇḍala of "wind" forms, "supported by space"; the mass of "water" is supported by that wind; the golden ground is supported by the water; this "great earth" is supported by that; and the beings of the essence, the four continents, the eight subcontinents, and so forth, remain supported by that earth. Such is the cause or basis for experiencing the result of the accumulation of the common karma of sentient beings. Having investigated, thinking, "Although the other maṇḍalas of the four elements are established in dependence on one another, upon what else is space supported and remaining?", you should reflect upon this meaning! And because it is not established as a physical entity, you will understand the manner in which it is not supported by anything at all. In "that manner", a "bodhisattva" who remains one-pointedly in "emptiness" "like space", even at that time, does not set his mind on the bliss of peace, but rather, having the support of objectless great love and compassion—without the apprehension of perceiving those who are tormented by suffering as truly existent sentient beings—and having attained immeasurable previous generation of the mind and aspirations, displays by the power of those "aspirations" immeasurable arrays of emanations that accomplish multiple "various" deeds that increase the excellent collections of the benefit and bliss of "beings". "By means of" the "skill in means" as explained, he does "not touch" the one-sided peace of "nirvāṇa", nor does he truly "abide in emptiness". ^3-227

###### **Application of Skill in Means Propelled by the Force of Aspirational Prayers for the Benefit of Others** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-228]]

The training of skillful means implicitly indicated by aspirations for the benefit of others. To explain the third, the training of skillful means implicitly indicated by aspirations for the benefit of others: the meaning of the single verse spoken starting with "Whenever..." is: "Whenever" they train on the path of the Mahāyāna, thus "bodhisattvas" who are skilled in the meaning of the profound abiding nature, emptiness, and "who", having dispelled the darkness of the ignorance of other disciples, "illuminate" the excellent path of the Mahāyāna, directly realize that all phenomena are empty by nature, and practice by way of resting evenly in "this" supreme "samādhi of the heroic stride" in which the elaborations of dualistic appearance are entirely "pacified". At the time of cultivating the path, they "do not cultivate" even the slightest mark of conventional elaborations; remaining in the meaning of the "signless" object of negation (truly existent marks), they practice the pacification of the conceptualization that adheres to nirvāṇa as truly something to be attained, and the complete pacification of the conceptualization that grasps saṃsāra as truly something to be abandoned. The "non-abiding" in either extreme of existence or peace "arises from the power of cultivating" the path of the special training of "skillful means". ^3-228

###### **Uncommon Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-229]]

The uncommon training of skillful means. To explain the fourth, the uncommon training of skillful means: the meaning of the single verse spoken starting with "flying in the air..." is: "For example", for a "bird flying in the air", there is no place or basis upon which to be supported, so it does "not remain" in that "space", "yet" by the power of its two wings it "does not fall to the ground". "Like that", a "bodhisattva practices" or abides "at the doors of the three liberations"—"emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness"—yet due to skillful means he "does not touch" the extreme of "nirvāṇa"; and due to the wisdom that realizes the abiding nature, he "does not practice the path" of grasping at marks "in existence". That he does not abide in the two extremes "is by the power of skillful means." ^3-229

###### **Non-Attached Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-230]]

The training of skillful means of non-attachment. ^3-230

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-231]]

The fifth, the training of skillful means of non-attachment: Although slightly different from Buddhaśrī, according to the system of the Master (Haribhadra), it is explained by two verses starting with "For example, trained in archery..." Furthermore, "for example", a man who has properly "trained" and become skilled in the "archery" of shooting arrows shoots an "arrow" into the sky together with a wooden board; and again, by shooting other arrows after that, attaching them completely in continuous succession behind each previous arrow, by being held by those shot unceasingly, that earlier arrow finds no opportunity to "fall to the ground". But when that man desires it to fall to the ground and does not shoot an arrow, that arrow will fall to the ground. ^3-231

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-232]]

"Like" that "example", that "great being", a "bodhisattva practicing" the "supreme perfection of wisdom", during the time of training on the path of the Mahāyāna, possesses the wisdom that realizes emptiness and means such as "great compassion", aspirations, and complete dedication; and those of vast intellect who consider and completely investigate by means of the powers of faith and so forth and clairvoyances and "miraculous abilities", "as long as" the "roots of virtue" that culminate in the three—completion, ripening, and purification—are "not completed", for "that long" they "do not" manifest or attain that "supreme emptiness" or the "authentic limit in the meantime". ^3-232

###### **Non-Apprehending Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-6-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-233]]

The training of skillful means of non-apprehension. To explain the sixth, the training of skillful means of non-apprehension: the meaning of the two verses spoken starting with "For example, a monk..." "For example", a "monk" without afflictions, who possesses supreme powers of "miraculous abilities", remains in "space" and simultaneously manifests "various" arrays of miracles to tame beings, "displaying" multiple actions such as "walking, pacing, lying down, and sitting"; "yet" his mind has no weariness from those "various" behaviors, "nor does his body have" any "fatigue". "Like that example", a "bodhisattva" who is "skilled in means" remains in the door of liberation, "emptiness", and having "reached the far shore" of the "miraculous ability" of the primordial wisdom that knows the conventional and the ultimate, does not remain with true adherence to any object at all; "yet" he displays infinite types of deeds of benefit and bliss to "beings" in accordance with their respective fortunes, and although he exerts himself for the welfare of beings for "immeasurable millions of eons", there is no weariness in his mind "nor is there any exhaustion or fatigue of the body"; this is "by the power" of extraordinary means and wisdom. ^3-233

###### **Signless Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-7-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-234]]

The training of skillful means of signlessness. The seventh, the training of skillful means of signlessness: the meaning explained by the two verses starting with "For example, some men..." "For example", if "some men" stand on the edge of a great abyss, holding two umbrellas in both hands, and jump into the space of the abyss, casting their bodies into the great abyss, by the power of the wind lifting those two umbrellas, they do not "fall to the ground" immediately, and not only are their bodies not injured, but they safely arrive in the meantime and go to wherever they wish. "Like that", a "bodhisattva" who is skilled in the meaning of the abiding nature and abides in love and compassion completely "holds the umbrellas" of the two—means and wisdom—and remains; by the power of wisdom, he realizes all phenomena as empty by nature, signless, and wishless; and by the power of special means, he "does not touch" the extreme of peace, nirvāṇa, and also "sees all" knowable "phenomena" as explained. ^3-234

###### **Wishless Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-8-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-235]]

The training of skillful means of wishlessness. Eighth, to explain the training of skillful means of wishlessness: two verses are spoken starting with "For example, a jewel..." Furthermore, "for example", a man of great strength and noble character, desiring jewels in order to make his relatives happy, goes to an island of jewels, and having arrived and found many jewels, when he returns to his own home again, it is not the case that the merchant himself alone lives happily with what he has found and gives nothing at all to his other relatives and friends, leaving them unhappy. "Like that example", a "bodhisattva" goes to the "island of jewels" of the primordial wisdom realizing profound emptiness, which is the source of all qualities, and when he attains immeasurable phenomena of qualities such as the joy and bliss of concentration, the five faculties such as faith, and the five powers, he does not delight in merely himself touching the nirvāṇa where all suffering without exception is pacified, and without accomplishing the benefit and bliss of other sentient beings, leave them as possessors of minds tormented by suffering; for by great compassion he establishes all beings in ripening and liberation. ^3-235

###### **Application of Skill in Means of the Signs of Irreversibility** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-9-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-236]]

The training of skillful means of the mark of non-reversal. Ninth, to explain the training of skillful means of the mark of non-reversal: the meaning of the two verses spoken starting with "For example, seeking..." "For example", in order for "merchants seeking jewels" to know the conditions of the road and the value of goods for buying and selling, they travel to whatever "large cities", "towns, and villages" lie between their home country and the island of jewels, yet they do not stay there long; "nor do they remain permanently on the island of jewels"; having set out on the path, they know and understand the conditions of the path, and "without remaining in their own homes either", being skilled in how to travel the path and exerting themselves, they arrive at their desired destination. ^3-236

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-237]]

"Like that example", a "bodhisattva" who constantly exerts himself in accomplishing the welfare of others, who is clear-minded, that is, skilled in the path of means and wisdom, having accomplished the welfare of sentient beings to be tamed by them, does "not remain" himself in the path or fruit of the lower vehicles; nor does he remain in the "buddha's primordial wisdom", which is like the island of jewels, without having performed the three—completion, ripening, and purification; "nor does he remain" with true adherence in the unconditioned dharmadhātu, which is like one's own home, "yet" having understood the modes of the paths of the three vehicles, it should be known that he acts for the welfare of immeasurable sentient beings, such as the trainees of the three classes. Such is the mark of non-reversal, the uncommon distinguishing characteristic of a bodhisattva. ^3-237

###### **Immeasurable Application of Skill in Means** ^3-3-2-2-1-1-11-10-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-238]]

The training of skillful means of the immeasurables. Tenth, to explain the training of skillful means of the immeasurables: the meaning of the three verses spoken starting with "Whenever towards beings..." "Whenever" training in the deeds of a bodhisattva, having connected with all "beings", sentient beings, through generating great love that wishes them to meet with happiness, a "bodhisattva" who "practices" the "supreme concentration", the nature of being naturally empty, without the mark of the cause being truly established, and without the wish of the result being truly established, that he would attain the unconditioned expanse, nirvāṇa, for his own sake alone, or that he could be designated as one belonging to conditioned saṃsāra, is impossible. In order to clearly teach this very thing by way of an example: ^3-238

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-239]]

"For example", "the body of an emanated man" is not such that it does not appear to anyone; and although by its own essence "it is empty", it can "also be designated by a name", and in the world "there are also those who express its manner". "Like that", a "bodhisattva practicing" in the doors of the three liberations does not abide in the two extremes of existence or peace, "yet" he appears in the world and can "also be designated by a name". ^3-239

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-240]]

Teaching the limitless skillful means by way of a summary: "If someone" completely "asks" a bodhisattva what the "deeds to be practiced by the sons of the victorious ones are like" "and" how the elements, intentions, and sharp or dull faculties, and so forth, of the trainees are, if the "bodhisattva" does "not know" the elements, inclinations, and so forth, of the trainees, and does "not completely teach" the profound dharmas of emptiness and signlessness to be taught, and does "not explain", meaning is unable to explain, "the dharmas", marks, aspects, and so forth "of the stage of non-reversal" by which one can ascertain that bodhisattva as non-reversing, "it should be known that" he has "not been prophesied" as non-reversing; whereas knowing those and being skilled in teaching the dharma appropriately to the minds of the trainees should be known as being one who has attained the mark of non-reversal. Having thus finished explaining the eleven phenomena that characterize the application of the complete realization of all aspects, the conclusion of the chapter: this is the commentary on the fourth chapter from The Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate: A Jewel Lamp Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, a Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection. ^3-240

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-241]]

Having finished explaining the application of the complete realization of all aspects, the cause for mastering the four applications of practice: ^3-241

###### **Resultant Peak Application** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-242]]

The result, the peak application. Second, to explain the result to be mastered, the peak application: it is stated in the Ornament of Clear Realization (Abhisamayālaṃkāra), "The realization of that and the increase of aspects..." up to "...the clear realization at the peak." That yoga of a bodhisattva held by wisdom that has become superior to the Mahāyāna path of accumulation in summarizing and cultivating the three kinds of exalted knowledge is the definition of the peak application. That and the achievement of engagement are synonymous. There are eight phenomena that characterize the peak application: because there are the four peak applications of the four paths of preparation (heat and so forth), the peak application of the path of seeing, the peak application of the path of meditation, the uninterrupted peak application, and the mistaken achievement to be cleared away, making eight. Its boundaries are from the heat of the Mahāyāna path of preparation up to the end of the continuum. ^3-242

###### **Peak Application of Heat** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-243]]

The peak application of heat. To explain the first among those, the peak application of heat: it is stated in the Ornament of Clear Realization, "Even in dreams, all phenomena..." up to "...twelve aspects are asserted in speech." Here, concerning the three: definition, divisions, and boundaries; first, the definition of the peak application of heat is: the first Mahāyāna aid to penetration that has attained any of the twelve marks of attaining the peak application. Second, if divided, there are three: small, medium, and great heat. Third, its boundaries: it exists only on the Mahāyāna path of preparation of heat. To explain the peak application of heat, which is characterized by the twelve marks, such as experiencing revulsion towards saṃsāra and viewing phenomena as like a dream "even in dreams," and pacifying the afflictions of beings by words of truth and so forth, there are two parts: explaining the first eight marks, and related topics. ^3-243

###### **Divisions of the Signs** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-244]]

The thorough division of marks. First: the meaning taught by the three verses starting with "The arhats and..." By the power of having become accustomed to the Mahāyāna path of the unity of means and wisdom for a long time, let alone in actuality, "even in dreams he does not desire" the stage of a śrāvaka "arhat and" the "pratyekabuddha", that is, the primordial wisdom of a solitary realizer of the lower vehicles, nor the qualities and happiness of saṃsāra in the three realms. And seeing "in dreams" and so forth "buddhas" surrounded by an ocean of retinues, whichever bodhisattva sees those buddhas "also teaching the dharma to beings", "it should be known that" this is the "mark of having been prophesied by the buddhas" as not reversing to the lower paths away from the Mahāyāna enlightenment, or of having attained the peak application. Thus, the first two lines teach the realization of means, and the next two lines teach the mark of seeing buddhas and the second mark of them teaching. ^3-244

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-245]]

Furthermore, when that bodhisattva sees many sentient beings abiding in the three lower realms "in dreams," out of compassion, in "that instant" he makes aspirations "to cut off the continuation" of the lower realms, and by its power he sees their sufferings pacified; "and" also, when awake, if he sees a town and so forth burning with fire, and without fear he says, "If it is true that I have been prophesied as non-reversing, by this truth and words of truth may this fire be pacified!" and thus by the blessing of expressing the power of truth the ability to "pacify the mass of fire" is accomplished, "it should be known that" he is one who has been "prophesied by the victorious ones of the past" as non-reversing from complete enlightenment. This verse teaches the two marks of abandoning the lower realms and of pacifying fire and so forth by truth. "Likewise, in the human realm," when one sees sentient beings harmed by "the demons of spirits" and tormented by "many different kinds of diseases," that bodhisattva endowed with love who wishes to permanently benefit and temporarily save them from suffering, by the blessing of pronouncing words of truth and so forth, "those will be pacified"; "yet" he does "not become conceited" by that ability, thinking "My power is extremely great," and does "not generate pride" holding himself supreme; "it should be known that" this is a mark of having been prophesied as non-reversing or of having attained the peak application. This teaches the mark of the peak application of pacifying the demons of spirits and so forth merely by uttering words of truth. When the first eight marks are explained in the Eight Thousand Stanza Perfection of Wisdom: it is taught extensively from, "Subhūti, if a bodhisattva, a great being, even in dreams perfectly views all phenomena as like a dream and does not actualize them, Subhūti, this too is of a non-reversing great bodhisattva," up to "...the twentieth chapter, on skillful means." The commentary on the twentieth chapter, on skillful means, from The Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate: A Jewel Lamp Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, a Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection. ^3-245

###### **Related Topics** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-246]]

Related topics. Second, regarding related topics: expanding from the two marks of pacifying a burning town and pacifying the harm of spirits by uttering words of truth, in order to teach how, instead of this being a mark of non-reversal, it is the entry of the actions of Māra, the meaning taught by the eight verses starting with "If he...": when by uttering words of truth, a fire burning and the demons of spirits and so forth are pacified by the power of Māra, "if" that "bodhisattva" becomes "conceited," thinking, "Through my words of truth, various blessings have been accomplished or obtained, and since I am endowed with such qualities and powers, I am a bodhisattva who has attained the prophecy," or "if" he becomes conceited, thinking, "Other bodhisattvas will prophesy me and proclaim my fame," and he despises other bodhisattvas, then that one who abides in pride and conceit, because he is endowed with conceit that hopes for fame and with pride, has a small mind and an unripened continuum, and "should be known as" a suitable candidate to be deceived by "Māra"; relying on that, one should know it as the entry of the actions of Māra. ^3-246

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-247]]

Teaching the actions of Māra that arise from a basis of names. The demon of generating conceit and pride arises from a basis of names as well. Thus, if "sinful Māra approaches before" that bodhisattva in the guise of a buddha, a fully ordained monk, and so forth, he will say this: "Such are 'your and your parents' names', and the names 'up through the lineage of your ancestors for seven generations' are this," thus speaking their names; and in accordance with what he previously thought in his mind, he says, "Whenever you 'become a buddha, your name will be this'." And he clearly states just how that person will currently possess qualities such as the ascetic qualities of being an "alms-beggar" and one who wears "rags from a dust heap" and so forth, possessing the vows of a novice or fully ordained monk, and possessing the yoga of calm abiding and special insight; and he says, "'Also in your former times,' the manner of your qualities, such as ascetic qualities, was 'similar to this' present one." Having heard those words spoken at that time, "whichever bodhisattva" generates overt "pride" and "conceit" based on that basis or cause of merely naming the expression of his own qualities, "it should be known that" his "conceit" has been thoroughly "incited by Māra" and he has a "small mind" or is inferior. ^3-247

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-248]]

How Māra enters even when one is practicing isolation. In general, there are two kinds of isolation: outer isolation from crowds, and inner isolation from distraction. Among these, for a bodhisattva, the primary isolation is to be isolated from inferior mental engagements that distract one from the Mahāyāna; therefore, without that, even if one fully relies upon and abides in places "thoroughly isolated" from crowds—such as a "wilderness" situated far from "villages and great cities," "mountain valleys," and "forests" isolated from the coming and going and clamour of people—a bodhisattva who generates conceit, thinking, "'I abide in isolation highly praised by the Sugata'," and praises himself while "despising other bodhisattvas who abide in villages," "it should be known that" his mental continuum has been completely corrupted or generated "by Māra," and he has a "small mind" and is not good. ^3-248

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-249]]

Teaching the specific identification of a bodhisattva's isolation, the example of its pointlessness if it does not occur, and how it is unsuitable to despise based on appearances if it does occur. If one "always" abides in "villages" such as Videha and so forth, "countries" such as Campaka and so forth, and "cities" such as Pāṭaliputra and so forth, and even while abiding there, apart from striving to ripen the unripened disciples among sentient beings, to liberate those who are ripened, and to accomplish unsurpassed awakening, that bodhisattva "never," i.e., at no time, "generates the desire for" a śrāvaka arhat and "pratyekabuddha"; this is taught to be "the genuine isolation of the bodhisattvas," the "sons of the Sugatas." If one's own mind is isolated from inferior vehicle mental engagements, no matter where one abides, such as in towns or villages, one abides in supreme isolation. Not abiding in such isolation, "whichever bodhisattva" abides in a place extending for "five hundred yojanas," a "mountain valley thoroughly isolated" from the bustling of people, "wherever" it is, a place unpopulated by humans and filled with poisonous snakes and so forth, "even for many tens of millions of years," that bodhisattva "who does not know this" inner isolation generates, i.e., "acquires, overweening pride," thinking, "My abiding in isolation is supreme!" and abides similarly to inferior vehicle mental engagement; that is not abiding in the isolation of a bodhisattva, because it is corrupted by inferior mental engagement. Under the influence of such pride, towards "other bodhisattvas" who strive for the welfare of transmigrators and who, having attained the "four dhyānas," the "five powers," the "five faculties" such as faith, the "three doors of liberation," the "heroic stride samādhi," and so forth, roam in villages and cities for the sake of others, establishing sentient beings in ripening and liberation, not knowing their intent and conduct, one who "despises" them, thinking, "This one is not practicing in wilderness and isolation," is "the object of Māra's activity," that is, they have come to abide in an object where Māra has found an opportunity; so "the Victors have taught." ^3-249

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-250]]

Well then, if abiding alone is not isolation, what is isolation? "Whichever bodhisattva," whether his body abides in a "village or else in a wilderness," if his mind is free from the thought desiring to attain either of the two vehicles of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and becomes singularly ascertained in the path of supreme awakening, this manner is the completely pure isolation of bodhisattvas who have engaged in the welfare of transmigrators. Not knowing this, one such as the previous bodhisattva who assesses the measure, i.e., "gauges the level," of that bodhisattva who abides in such isolation, and appraises them, thinking, "This one is not abiding in isolation," will themselves degenerate from the qualities of the grounds and paths. Therefore, the Capable One, the Buddha Bhagavat, has stated: "Apart from those like me, individuals should not assess the measure of individuals, lest they degenerate," and "A bodhisattva should examine their own continuum but should not look for faults in others." Having understood according to what is taught in the sūtras, generating the perception of all sentient beings as the Teacher and paying respect is the primary training for bodhisattvas. This is the commentary on the twenty-first chapter, the Chapter on Māra, from the Commentary on the Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom, entitled A Jeweled Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising. ^3-250

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-251]]

Second, since the non-occurrence of such things also depends upon relying on a spiritual friend, the meaning of what is stated in two verses starting with "Therefore" and so forth, in order to advise the necessity of relying on a spiritual friend and to explain the signs of an irreversible one who is like that: Because many deeds of Māra occur that cause hindrances when practicing the path of the Mahāyāna, "therefore," having understood that, one must turn them back, and so one should rely on outer and inner tutors. Moreover, the one who relies has a strong intention "seeking" "supreme awakening"—unsurpassed awakening—for the sake of others, and is skilled in the means to accomplish it; as for the manner in which to rely, by "definitely destroying" "pride" that holds oneself as supreme and so forth, for example, just as "in the world" "a host of sick people" "rely upon" another, "a skilled physician," "to cure their illness," one should properly rely upon a qualified spiritual friend, unfalteringly through both intention and application, by relying on the perceptions of oneself as a sick person, the Dharma speaker as a physician, the Dharma as medicine, earnest practice as curing the illness, the Tathāgata as a supreme being, and the Dharma system as remaining for a long time. ^3-251

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-252]]

If one asks, "Who is that spiritual friend to be relied upon?": the outer tutors, the Buddhas Bhagavats and the bodhisattvas who have engaged in practicing the path of supreme awakening, together with the inner tutor, the path of the six perfections, should be relied upon as spiritual friends. Moreover, because those Buddhas and bodhisattvas are the ones who teach the path of the Mahāyāna, they are called outer tutors, and because this path of the six perfections is what is to be practiced and accomplished, it is called the inner tutor. Because those two hold one back from falling into the inferior paths and so forth, by the two types of causes of relying upon those two tutors, one will quickly realize the unsurpassable awakening of a Buddha, "because it is the nature of dependent arising that its own result arises from complete perfection." Also, from the Aṣṭasāhasrikā: from "Then again, Bhagavat..." down to "At the very beginning, one should rely upon and pay respect to a spiritual friend," and so forth, this is explained. In order to explain the signs of training in the perfection of wisdom, which is the inner tutor, the meaning of what is stated in one verse starting with "Past Jinas" and so forth: The manner in which the six perfections act as inner tutors is as follows. The completely perfect Buddhas, the "Jinas," who have "past," having already appeared in the past, and the "un-arisen," future Buddhas, and those who are "abiding," currently teaching the Dharma in the "ten directions"—the great path traveled by all the Jinas of the three times is solely these six perfections and none other than this, because without relying on the six perfections it is impossible to attain Buddhahood, and by a path other than this one cannot attain Buddhahood. These six perfections dispel the darkness of the afflictive obscurations of those who have engaged in the path or method of supreme awakening, and become the "illuminator" that illuminates the path of realizing selflessness; because they clear away the darkness of cognitive obscurations, they are a "lamp"; because they make things apparent through the path of seeing, they become "light"; and because they bring the paths of the supreme vehicle, such as the path of meditation, to full completion, they become the supreme "teacher"—thus the Jinas have explained. Likewise, the Aṣṭasāhasrikā states: "These very six perfections are the teacher. The six perfections are the path. The six perfections are the light. The six perfections are the lamp. The six perfections are the illumination. The six perfections are the protector, the refuge, the resting place," and so forth. In brief, for attaining Buddhahood, the two causes—the spiritual friend who teaches the six perfections and one's own accomplishment or practice—are indispensable, and... ^3-252

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-253]]

If those are complete, even though one attains the signs, manifest clinging does not arise; and in order to explain that very realization of having no manifest clinging, such as the manner in which all faults and qualities arise merely through being imputed by conception, the meaning of what is stated in four verses starting with "Just as wisdom" and so forth: Moreover, just as the Aṣṭasāhasrikā states, "The perfection of wisdom has the characteristic of emptiness," "just as" the essence or characteristic of the perfection of wisdom is inherently empty because it is devoid of a nature of being truly established as one or many, "likewise," all these phenomena, "form and so forth," in their mode of abiding or characteristic, are known to be inherently empty, similar to that perfection of wisdom; and if one thoroughly knows all phenomena from form up to omniscience to be empty of their own essence and without the mark of true establishment, then "at that time" when one practices the signs of attaining the peak application as taught above, or practices "here" in that way, one is "practicing the perfection of wisdom of the Sugatas." ^3-253

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-254]]

If one asks, "If all phenomena are inherently empty, how do sentient beings become completely afflicted or completely purified?", in order to teach the answer to that: ordinary sentient beings excessively superimpose pleasantness and unpleasantness and "completely conceptualize" through improper mental engagement, and by the force of that, they generate craving desire for the food of the sensory qualities such as form, sound, smell, taste, and tangibles, and those possessing minds attached to saṃsāra, such as sensory qualities, constantly cycle from the peak of existence down to the Avīci hell, like flies swarming around the smell of filth, and therefore become completely afflicted; moreover, except for being established merely conventionally like an illusion, both the "self" and the virtuous phenomena of a self are not genuinely established and are inherently empty, because they are ultimately unproduced. Nevertheless, foolish "beings" who are unskilled in the meaning of the abiding nature apprehend what is ultimately non-existent as existent, for example, becoming like some people who think they have "tied a knot in space"; therefore, beings whose mind-streams are bound by erroneous conceptions are difficult to liberate—thus the side of complete affliction is tenable; and as for how they suffer in saṃsāra by the power of grasping at true existence, which holds things to be established in that way even though they are not ultimately established: "for example," when a manifest concept arises from an erroneous mind due to one's own perception of suspicion that "poison has flared up" or "poison has entered the stomach," even though in reality poison has not entered the stomach, by the power of that conception suffering arises and one faints from fear, just as cowards tremble with fear merely upon being told by an enemy or the like, "I will kill you." ^3-254

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-255]]

Just like that example, foolish ordinary beings who have not attained the noble path apprehend a self as inherently existent even though it is not ultimately established, and by focusing on the eye, nose, and so forth, they accept them as belonging to a self; from habituation to that, there arises the untrue complete conceptualization that perceives the self and what belongs to a self as true, and by the power of that, they accumulate karma, take birth again and again in existence, and continuously experience various sufferings such as perishing—that is, death—decline, and mental unhappiness. Just as it was taught that grasping at a self and what belongs to a self as existent even though they do not exist in the mode of abiding leads to complete affliction by the power of all-obscuring ignorance, it is stated that a bodhisattva who directly realizes the meaning of selflessness—that is, the non-apprehension of self and what belongs to a self in accordance with the unmistaken mode of abiding—knows the abiding nature and habituates to it, and thereby will attain the state of liberation, which is complete purification; moreover, that is conventional, but ultimately, a bodhisattva who genuinely knows that in this regard no person whatsoever becomes afflicted or completely pure will subsequently realize the perfection of wisdom, which realizes existence and peace as equal in being empty of true existence, thus it is said. The explanation of this is taught in the Aṣṭasāhasrikā by Subhūti asking, "Lord, what is the characteristic of the perfection of wisdom?" and so forth. ^3-255

###### **Application of Peak** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-256]]

Peak application. Second, the peak application: the Ornament states, from "If the beings of Jambudvīpa..." up to "...has the nature of sixteen increases." In this there are three parts: First, the characteristic of the peak stage of the peak application is the second Mahāyāna aid to penetration, which attains the sixteen increases of merit that surpass making offerings to Buddhas equal to the sentient beings of a trichiliocosm. Second, if divided: there are three peak stages of the peak application: small, medium, and great. The boundary is solely on the Mahāyāna path of preparation of peak. Among the sixteen increases of merit in the peak stage of the peak application, the first: the peak application where the increase of merit surpasses sentient beings of a trichiliocosm, such as those in Jambudvīpa, making offerings to Buddhas: the meaning of the two verses stated starting with "If in Jambudvīpa": Taking a hypothetical extreme as an example, "if" all the sentient beings without exception in a trichiliocosm, illustrated by the sentient beings "in Jambudvīpa," were to attain a human body, and all of them were to generate the mind for unsurpassed, perfectly complete supreme awakening, and for "many tens of millions of thousands of years" they were to make extensive offerings to Buddhas and bodhisattvas and give immeasurable generosity to sentient beings, and they were to fully dedicate all those roots of virtue as causes for supreme awakening for the sake of beings, even though they would have immeasurable merit; "if" any bodhisattva manifests endeavor in the supreme perfection of wisdom, which has the nature of the clear realization of the peak, let alone for a long time, if they meditate on the path in accordance with that perfection of wisdom and teach it to others and so forth for even just "a single day," the mass of generosity just explained above would "not dare to compete" with even a fraction of that merit, that is, it would be unable to compete; "therefore," one should without distraction and constantly engage in this meditation on the perfection of wisdom, or peak application. Thus it is said. ^3-256

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-257]]

The increase of merit of the union of method and wisdom is explained by one verse starting with "The perfection of wisdom". Those yogis who course in the supreme perfection of wisdom that has the nature of the peak stage of the peak application generate great compassion for all sentient beings tormented by suffering and accomplish the benefit of beings, yet they do not have the perception of sentient beings as truly existent; when they possess the excellent path in which such non-apprehending compassion and wisdom are united, those bodhisattvas who are skilled in method become unsurpassed objects of generosity, or fields of merit, for all beings, and constantly fully consume meaningful alms and so forth desired from the realms. ^3-257

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-258]]

The way one must endeavor in that path of the union of method and wisdom along with an example: the meaning of the three verses stated starting with "A bodhisattva". "Any bodhisattva" abiding in the peak application and so forth, in order to liberate from the bondage of karma and afflictions and from the suffering of saṃsāra the sentient beings subsumed by the five continuums—the transmigrators of gods, humans, and the three lower realms—who have been connected as fathers, mothers, and so forth since long ago in this place of saṃsāra, out of a desire to teach the realm of sentient beings that very non-abiding nirvāṇa which is the further shore of the ocean of saṃsāra by relying on the "broad path," that is, the extensive path of the Mahāyāna, should endeavor in the perfection of wisdom that realizes emptiness, together with compassion, "day and night" without distraction. ^3-258

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-259]]

Explaining the sign of bringing the perfection of wisdom to mind by way of clarifying that very meaning with an example: "For example, if a person" who "had not found it previously," that is, a "supreme jewel" that is difficult to find, were to find it at another time, their mind would be intensely joyful, but if as soon as they found it they fell under the sway of careless distraction and that jewel were "lost," since it was lost they would manifest a desire in their mind thinking, "When will I find that jewel?" and they would "always be miserable." Like that "example," a "bodhisattva" who has entered the path of "supreme awakening," having found this "perfection of wisdom" which is like a supreme jewel that is very difficult to find and of great significance, should "practice yoga" in it and "never abandon" it; "for example," just as finding again later that lost jewel, joyfully taking it, wrapping it in cloth and so forth with intense endeavor, and quickly going to one's own home for fear of losing it or having it stolen by others, and cherishing it, pacifies the sorrow of being parted from the jewel and the suffering of poverty, similarly if a bodhisattva constantly endeavors in meditation on the perfection of wisdom, they will quickly attain unsurpassed awakening. Thus it is said. This is the commentary on the twenty-second chapter, the Chapter on the Spiritual Friend, from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, the Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate Ones that Clarifies the Reality of Profound Dependent Arising. ^3-259

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-260]]

To explain, by combining three increases of merit, the way that path of the union of method and wisdom outshines discordant factors, the patience towards non-arising phenomena, and so forth: the meaning of the two verses stated starting with "The sun and clouds". "For example", when "the sun" parted from "clouds", extremely bright and possessing a net of light rays, rises, vanquishing all the dense black darkness, it "outshines" fireflies that have only a tiny amount of light, the light of all living beings who are able to clear away only the darkness in their vicinity with the light of their own bodies, the host of stars that illuminate a little, the light of the moon, and all the rest. Similarly, the "bodhisattva" who courses in the supreme perfection of wisdom endowed with all supreme aspects, who is like the sun parted from the clouds of obscurations, by naturally "coursing in emptiness and the signless", is a wise one who, by radiating the light rays of speech that teaches the selflessness of phenomena and persons, completely destroys the dense darkness of views such as the view of the transitory collection, and outshines all transmigrators who are like fireflies, śrāvaka arhats who are like other living beings, pratyekabuddhas who are like stars, and many other bodhisattvas lacking skill in method who are like the moon, and is thereby specially exalted. By that statement "coursing in emptiness and the signless", it teaches the increase of merit of patience towards non-arising phenomena and the two which are the non-apprehending of awakening and that which awakens, and it teaches the increase of merit that is specially exalted above arhats, pratyekabuddhas, and so forth. ^3-260

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-261]]

To explain the way of ripening other trainees and the increase of merit of all classes of gods drawing near: two verses are stated starting with "For example, a king's". The meaning is: "For example," a "king's" son who gives away wealth in order to benefit the subjects, who desires to protect all beings from harm and to accomplish the goal of their benefit and happiness, who is skilled in method and of good character, becomes the chief of all the king's sons, is revered by all the subject beings, and becomes worthy of their going to his presence, each hoping to accomplish their own goals; if that prince brings benefit and joy to many sentient beings even now when he has not yet become king, "what need is there to mention" that he will accomplish the benefit of beings and bring them joy when he is consecrated as king, obtains the methods of sovereignty, and becomes a king abiding in sovereignty? Like that example, a wise "bodhisattva" who courses in the deeds of a prince with wisdom and compassion, gives the nectar of Dharma to hosts of fortunate beings and brings joy to gods and humans by establishing them on the path and so forth, so that the six classes of desire realm gods and so forth will come to their presence to listen to the Dharma and show reverence; if this bodhisattva endeavors to accomplish the benefit and happiness of many sentient beings even now during the phase of the path of training, what need is there to mention that they will bring benefit to all transmigrators when they become a Buddha, a Dharma King, and abide in the exalted wisdom of omniscient knowledge? Because it goes without saying. This is the commentary on the twenty-third chapter, the Chapter on Śakra, from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, the Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate Ones that Clarifies the Reality of Profound Dependent Arising. ^3-261

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-262]]

To explain the two increases of merit of outshining all māras and revering other bodhisattvas as similar to the Teacher and abiding in equal training with them: the meaning of the six verses stated starting with "At that time". "At that time" when coursing on the path of the union of method and wisdom that cannot be overpowered by discordant factors, the "evil māras" abiding in the trichiliocosm think, "This bodhisattva has passed beyond my domain and, having awakened, will cause many sentient beings to pass beyond as well, so my domain will become empty," and their minds become endowed with "agony", and thinking "I am unable to cause interruptions," they are stricken with "sorrow", and due to that, they experience physical suffering and mental displeasure, and become "faint-hearted" and weak; and unable to bear being tormented by suffering in that way, they think, "What can be done so that this bodhisattva's mind will shrink back and turn away from the path of the Great Vehicle?", and they cause interruptions, and in order to display great terror to that bodhisattva, they display various miracles, such as burning the directions with fire and casting meteors. ^3-262

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-263]]

Who is the bodhisattva against whom the māras do not find an opportunity, even if they act in that way? "At whatever time" those "bodhisattvas" who are "skilled in wisdom and method", endowed with an intensely strong, unyielding intention for the path of the Great Vehicle, "constantly day and night look upon" and consider the meaning of the supreme "perfection of wisdom"; "at that time" when acting in that way, "the bodies and minds" of those bodhisattvas course "like birds" going in the sky, without fear of the precipices of existence and peace, because they abide on the path of the union of method and wisdom. Therefore, "how could" the evil "māras" and other partisans of the dark "find an opportunity" against those bodhisattvas? They will not. ^3-263

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-264]]

The way an opportunity is found if a fault enters one's behavior: "At whatever time" "bodhisattvas" become mutually enraged and engage in physical "fighting" by striking each other and so forth, or "disputing" by verbally exposing each other's faults or litigating before a king, and become mutually discordant, endowed with minds enraged with hatred; "at that time" "the evil māras" become "supremely joyous" and delighted. The reason for this is because they think: "Due to the fault of engaging in fighting and disputing, 'both' of those bodhisattvas 'will become distant from the Victor's exalted wisdom' and it will become an interruption to the path of the Great Vehicle." In that way, "both" of them "will become distant from the Buddha's exalted wisdom", and with their minds becoming corrupted, they become similar to harm-inflicting rākṣasas and piśācas. "Both" of them also cause their own vow to accomplish the benefit and happiness of sentient beings to degenerate through their "disputing." How could they attain awakening when they are endowed with hatred, which degenerates compassion, love, bodhicitta, and so forth, and are devoid of meditating on its antidote, patience? It is like a sprout not emerging from a seed burnt by fire. "At that time" when bodhisattvas engage in mutual disputes, "the māras together with their retinues" or partisans "will become intensely joyous." Therefore, it is taught extensively in the sutras that the disadvantages of a single instant of anger arising in a bodhisattva are greater than a great fault of attachment occurring for a long time, as it destroys love and compassion and annihilates roots of virtue and so forth. As quoted in the Sūtrasamuccaya from the Candrapradīpa Sūtra: "Whoever harbors a mind of mutual harm, they are not saved by ethical discipline and learning, they are not saved by concentration and dwelling in the wilderness, nor are they saved by generosity and making offerings to the Buddha." And from the Mañjuśrīvikrīḍita Sūtra: "Mañjuśrī, that which is called anger, anger, because it thoroughly destroys the virtue accumulated over a hundred eons, it is called anger." Also from the Sūtra of Maitreya's Great Lion's Roar: "One should avoid a place with angry disputes by a hundred yojanas, and one should not generate a mind of hatred. Generating a mind of malice and hatred towards another bodhisattva is a far heavier fault than scolding, striking, or stabbing with weapons all the sentient beings of the great trichiliocosm, and it will injure and corrupt the bodhisattva. Just as, for example, iron cannot be cut by anything other than iron, such as clods of earth, the roots of virtue of a bodhisattva are exhausted by hating another bodhisattva, but cannot be exhausted by anything else. Therefore, they should mutually show respect and reverence to one another, and should generate the recognition of the Teacher even towards a bodhisattva who has newly generated the mind [of awakening]." In particular, becoming angry at a bodhisattva who has received prophecy is an extremely great fault: if any bodhisattva who has not received the prophecy of irreversibility from the Great Vehicle becomes mentally enraged at a bodhisattva who has received the prophecy of irreversibility or has obtained the signs, and initiates physical or verbal disputes, and does not feel regret or confess it and so forth, and does not let go of the angry mind, they must don the armor of engaging in the path and train on the path after calming themselves, for a number of eons equal in number to however many instants of anger arose in them, endowed with faults like a barren field where crops of virtue do not grow. Furthermore, from the Sūtra of Maitreya's Lion's Roar quoted in the Sūtrasamuccaya: "If any bodhisattva scolds, reprimands, or strikes with a stick or fist all sentient beings belonging to the great trichiliocosm, merely by that, the bodhisattva will not incur a wound or become injured; but if any bodhisattva generates a malicious mind, or even a mind of malice and hatred, toward one bodhisattva, merely by that, the bodhisattva will incur a wound and become injured. Why is that? If they do not completely abandon omniscience, for as many times as the bodhisattva generated a malicious mind, or a mind of malice and hatred, toward the other bodhisattva, they must repeatedly don the armor for that many eons." Therefore, individuals who desire to practice the path of the Great Vehicle should not allow an opportunity for anger even at the cost of their lives, and should make love, compassion, and bodhicitta the core of their practice. ^3-264

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-265]]

The way one must rely on patience, since the faults of anger are as explained above: Since "the Buddhas touched" or attained "great awakening through the perfection of patience," "this angry mind," which is its discordant class, "is not good." Generating the mindfulness of thinking, "It destroys the roots of virtue accumulated over many eons and causes love and compassion to degenerate," one individually confesses that sin with intense regret and vows not to commit such faults again in the future. Thinking, "I must not give this corrupt mind an opportunity to arise in my continuum," one does not delight in fighting, disputes, and so forth; this is to "arrive at this path of the Great Vehicle, the dharma of the Buddha." Thus it is said. From the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate: A Jeweled Lamp Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, this is the commentary on the twenty-fourth chapter, the Chapter on Pride. ^3-265

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-266]]

To teach the increase that is foremost among the inner ones, such as the two increases, by reason of becoming one possessing the lineage of the Buddha and attaining the fruition of Buddhahood itself, the meaning of the two verses stated starting with "Whoever, when training" is as follows: "When whoever," meaning a bodhisattva, "trains" on the path of the Great Vehicle, they do not accept any of the dharmas to be trained in—the three trainings, the six perfections, and so forth—ultimately, and they do not apprehend the training self and the dharmas to be trained in by it as truly existent. "Whoever trains like that," knowing "both these"—the antidote, the training of the bodhisattva, and its discordant class, "non-training," such as anger—as equally empty of true existence, "without conceptualizing" them as truly existent, "that is the unmistaken training in this perfection," the dharma that brings about the attainment of the state of a Buddha. ^3-266

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-267]]

Any bodhisattva who "knows this training" without transgressing the stages of the two truths in that way, "this one will never," meaning at no time, degenerate from the trainings of wisdom and concentration, "and will not become of corrupted ethical discipline"—thus they become of the lineage of the Buddha. And it teaches the reason for attaining Buddhahood itself, stating, "Such a bodhisattva 'trains in this,'" such a path of the supreme vehicle, "in order to find" or attain "the dharmas of the Buddha," the ten powers, the four fearlessnesses, and so forth. This is because that bodhisattva will exceptionally increase in the training of concentration and the training of ethical discipline, and will be skilled in the training of wisdom. The reason for this is because they train by way of knowing them to be devoid of true existence, without even the slightest apprehension of their three spheres as truly existent. Thus it is said. ^3-267

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-268]]

Regarding the teaching on the increase of the non-arising of discordant classes such as miserliness, the meaning of the two verses stated starting with "The wise ones, makers of light": "When the wise ones," bodhisattvas who are skilled in the meaning of the two truths in that way, "the makers of light" who illuminate the dharma of profound emptiness, "train thus" in the perfection of wisdom, they do not generate even a single non-virtuous mind of afflictive conceptualizations, such as true grasping, miserliness, and so forth, which contradict the path of omniscience. Since their continuums are pervaded by the light of wisdom realizing profound emptiness, there is no refuge where the darkness of bad conceptualizations can abide. It is like how "even a slight amount of darkness does not abide in the space before the sun traveling in the sky, being overpowered by its light rays." Thus it is said. ^3-268

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-269]]

To teach the increase of knowing the way to practice by subsuming all perfections within the perfection of wisdom, together with an example, the two verses starting with "The perfection of wisdom" were stated. The meaning is: For those who train unmistakenly in the perfection of wisdom in this way, "all perfections, giving and so forth, are subsumed within this perfection of wisdom" realizing the non-apprehension of the three spheres. "For example, regarding the view of the transitory collection," just as it is stated in the Sūtra Requested by Brahmājāla that "all those sixty-two bad views" based on the extremes of past and future, such as the view that the self and the world are eternal, "are subsumed" within it, "similarly" to that example, it is stated that "all these perfections, giving and so forth," are subsumed within the practice of the perfection of wisdom realizing emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects, which is embraced by not lacking any aspect of method. ^3-269

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-270]]

"For example, if the life faculty is stopped, all however many other faculties there are, such as the eye faculty and so forth, will also stop." Just so, for "whoever practices the perfection of wisdom," meaning the bodhisattvas who have become supremely skilled in the way of practicing the path of the Great Vehicle, "all" other "perfection" trainings "are subsumed within this" practice of the perfection of wisdom, and it is stated that without this perfection of wisdom, all others are absent. Thus it is said. ^3-270

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-271]]

To explain the two increases—the increase of attaining all excellences and the increase of nearing awakening—one verse starting with "Wise bodhisattvas" was stated. The meaning is: That "bodhisattva skilled" in method and wisdom "trains" in "whatever are the qualities of the grounds and paths of the hearers, and likewise, all the qualities of the solitary realizers," in order to care for the trainees to be tamed by them. And although they care for those trainees, those bodhisattvas "do not abide" in that path of the lesser vehicle "and do not generate the desire to attain their fruitions." Thinking, "This path is merely to be known or trained in by me for the sake of caring for other trainees, but is not to be actualized," "therefore," they train in "all" the paths of the three vehicles. These increases are explained in the Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines by the passage: "Subhūti, moreover, if all however many sentient beings there are in Jambudvīpa were to simultaneously obtain a human body, and having obtained a human body, were to generate the mind set on unexcelled, perfect awakening..." and so forth. From the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate: A Jeweled Lamp Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, this is the commentary on the twenty-fifth chapter, the Chapter on Training. ^3-271

###### **Peak Application of Patience** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-272]]

Peak training of patience. Third, regarding the peak training of patience: It is stated in the Ornament, from "Of the phenomena of the three omnisciences..." up to "...is plainly called stability." Among the three aspects of this—definition, divisions, and boundary—first: The definition of the peak training of patience is the third aid to penetration of the vehicle, which is the wisdom completely conforming to the three omnisciences and the attainment of stability not diverted from the welfare of others. Second, if that is divided, there are three peak trainings of patience: small, middling, and great. The boundary is solely on [the level of] patience. To explain this very stability or peak training of patience, which is the firm attainment of the realization of method and wisdom, one verse starting with "Irreversible awakening" was stated. Although Buddhaśrī applies [the text] from here onwards to the state of mind continually abiding, it is as the Master applied it to stability. Moreover, if someone else rejoices from the depths of their heart at the mind generation of that person—a bodhisattva who, having stably attained the realization of method and wisdom, possesses a stable, intense mind generation (the intention desiring to establish beings in supreme awakening without turning back from the Great Vehicle) and who has thoroughly engaged by means of the application of practicing the perfections and so forth—then, while it is possible to capture the measure by weighing on a scale the kings of mountains, the Mount Merus, present in this great trichiliocosm, that virtue of rejoicing in the bodhisattva's roots of virtue cannot be thus measured, because it is limitless. Gungtang Jamyang stated that this is the hidden meaning, because it teaches that rejoicing illustrates their firm realization of method and wisdom. ^3-272

###### **Peak Application of Highest Dharma** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-4-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-273]]

Peak training of supreme dharma. Fourth, regarding the peak training of supreme dharma: It is stated in the Ornament, from "Those in four continents, a thousand..." up to "...are proclaimed to be the meditative concentration." Among the three aspects of this, first: The definition of the peak training of supreme dharma is the fourth aid to penetration of the Great Vehicle, which attains the state of mind continually abiding in meditative concentration passed beyond extremes, from the perspective of having ripened the capacity to generate the path of seeing of the Great Vehicle, which is its fruition. Second, if that is divided, there are three supreme dharmas: small, middling, and great. Third, the boundary is solely on [the level of] supreme dharma of the path of preparation of the Great Vehicle. ^3-273

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-274]]

To explain the peak training of the state of mind continually abiding, the meaning of the one verse stated starting with "Seeking virtue" is: If one asks what the reason is that rejoicing in the roots of virtue of a bodhisattva in that way has such great benefits: By directly rejoicing solely in that virtue of the bodhisattvas, one indirectly comes to rejoice in the aggregate of merit of all however many sentient beings there are in the world who "seek virtue" for themselves and who desire to benefit other beings. Why is that? It is because whatever virtue bodhisattvas accumulate is for the sake of benefiting all sentient beings. Those irreversible bodhisattvas, by the power of that excellent path, will attain all the qualities of the omniscient victors, and in order to exhaust without remainder the suffering of sentient beings wandering in cyclic existence, they give the gift of dharma in all worlds and establish beings without remainder on the path of awakening. Thus it is said. ^3-274

###### **Peak Application of the Path of Seeing** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-275]]

Peak training of the path of seeing. Fifth, to teach the peak training of the path of seeing: It is stated in the Ornament, from "On engaging and turning back..." up to "...summarized, that is the path of seeing here." Among the three aspects of this, first: The definition of the peak training of the path of seeing is the clear realization of the truth of the Great Vehicle that abides in the class of being the direct antidote to the seeds of the conceptions to be abandoned on the path of seeing. Second, if that is divided, there are two: meditative equipoise and subsequent attainment; and if divided by way of what is to be abandoned, there are four peak trainings of the path of seeing that are the antidotes to the four conceptions to be abandoned on the path of seeing. Third, the boundary is solely on the path of seeing of the Great Vehicle. To explain the peak training of the path of seeing, there is the explanation of the conceptions to be abandoned and the explanation of the path of seeing that is the antidote. ^3-275

###### **Explanation of the Conceptualizations to be Abandoned** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-276]]

Explanation of the conceptions to be abandoned. Regarding the first, there are two: the actual and the related topics. ^3-276

###### **Actual** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-277]]

The actual. Regarding the first as well, there are two: the conception of an apprehended object that is a cognitive obscuration and the conception of an apprehender. ^3-277

###### **Conceptualization of the Apprehended which is a Cognitive Obscuration** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-278]]

Conception of an apprehended object that is a cognitive obscuration. Regarding the first of those as well, there are two: the conception of an apprehended object concerning engaging, and the conception of an apprehended object concerning turning back. ^3-278

###### **Conceptualization of the Apprehended regarding Engagement** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-279]]

Conception of an apprehended object concerning engaging. First, to teach the conception of an apprehended object concerning engaging, the meaning of the one verse stated starting with "A bodhisattva..." is: A bodhisattva who, abiding in the pristine wisdom that does not conceptualize truly, completely knows that all thoroughly afflicted and purified phenomena are empty of nature, ultimately without marks, and without the elaborations or wishes of true establishment, does not seek awakening by way of true establishment or grasping at the two extremes through the wisdom realizing the pairs of thorough affliction and purification, apprehended and apprehender, and object to be abandoned and antidote and so forth to be without true existence. That yogi abiding on the path of seeing free from all elaborations of dualistic appearance exerts themselves in the supreme perfection of wisdom that has become the essence of seeing and meditation. Thus it is said. ^3-279

###### **Conceptualization of the Apprehended regarding Disengagement** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-280]]

Conception of an apprehended object concerning turning back. Second, to teach the conception of an apprehended object concerning turning back, the meaning of the one verse stated starting with "That realm of space..." is: For example, because space is merely the absence of obstruction and contact, there is no entity of that realm of space itself, nor is there anything incompatible that cannot co-exist with that space, nor is there anything obstructing the occurrence of other physical forms. Because the realm of space and what is called space are equally non-entities, their entity is not attained by any physical thing at all. Similar to that example, a bodhisattva who is skilled in practicing the profound wisdom that realizes the nature of the path of seeing—the pairs of things to be adopted and discarded, and objects to be abandoned and antidotes, and so forth—as equality in being empty of true existence, also abides in non-conceptual pristine wisdom like space, possessing the greatness of practicing the complete pacification of all conceptions apprehending true existence, which are what turn one back to the inferior path. Thus it is said. ^3-280

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-281]]

Second, to teach the first conception of an apprehender, which has the basis of a person substantially existing, the meaning of the two verses stated starting with "In the midst of people..." is: For example, in the midst of a large crowd of people gathered to watch a spectacle, an illusory person—a person emanated by means of illusory mantras and substances—does not think: "I shall please this large crowd of people with my excellent form and diverse activities," but still performs actions that please and delight the spectators, thoroughly displaying diverse miracles such as going and staying. Even though the crowd of people sees that, the illusory emanation is not established as a human body, it does not have a mind, and even its name is not ultimately established. ^3-281

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-282]]

Likewise, bodhisattvas who practice the perfection of wisdom that does not conceptualize the nature of the path of seeing as truly existent never think, "Having ultimately realized great awakening, I will completely liberate beings from cyclic existence." Although they do not think that, for the sake of taming disciples, they demonstrate diverse births as gods, humans, and so forth, and possess many activities that accomplish the benefit and happiness of beings. Although they do not ultimately exist, they conventionally demonstrate them like illusions, and they practice by way of not conceptualizing those things as truly existent. Thus it is said. ^3-282

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-283]]

Second, to teach the second conception of an apprehender, which has the basis of a person existing imputedly, the meaning of the two verses stated starting with "For example, a Buddha..." is: Even though emanations, such as supreme emanation bodies, perform all the activities of a Buddha such as teaching the Dharma, with regard to those emanations performing the activities of a Buddha, pride and arrogance thinking "I am the performer of the Buddha's activities" do not arise even slightly, because they do not have any conceptualization whatsoever. Likewise, a bodhisattva who is skilled in practicing the wisdom that does not conceptualize apprehended and apprehender as truly existing, although realizing all phenomena to be of one taste in being empty of true existence, conventionally performs all activities, such as the three of perfecting, ripening, and purifying, and demonstrates diverse activities, like emanations and illusions. ^3-283

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-284]]

Explanation in order to teach another example. When a carpenter who is skilled in the action of constructing mechanical devices constructs mechanical devices from wood in forms resembling men and women, the men and women constructed in that way also perform all activities such as going and staying, but they do not conceptualize thinking, "I have done this," because they have no minds. Similar to that example, that bodhisattva who is skilled and has attained stability in practicing the wisdom that realizes that although all phenomena are not ultimately existent, conventionally appearances are like illusions, performs all activities that accomplish the purpose of beings while abiding in non-conceptual pristine wisdom free from manifest conceptions that strongly adhere to apprehended and apprehender, like a wish-fulfilling jewel and a wish-granting tree. Thus it is said. This concludes the commentary on the twenty-sixth chapter, [the chapter on] being like illusions, from the Lamp of Jewels Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate: A Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising. ^3-284

###### **Related Topics** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-1-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-285]]

Related topics.
