Regarding the second, the related topics: To teach how, by having thus abandoned the four conceptions to be abandoned, they are praised by the Buddhas and cannot be defeated by the four maras, the meaning of the three verses stated starting with "Thus skilled in practicing..." is: Many hosts of gods such as Brahma and Indra, joining their palms and bowing with physical respect, perform offerings and homage to those wise ones who, having abandoned the four conceptions that adhere to apprehended and apprehender as truly existent as explained above, practice realizing all phenomena as empty of true existence. Not only that, however many Buddhas reside in the world systems of the ten directions, they also completely proclaim garlands that continuously spread masses of praise for the wonderful qualities of those great beings, the bodhisattvas. ^3-285

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-286]]

Explanation in order to teach that, therefore, they have passed beyond the sphere of maras. If however many sentient beings there are in fields equal to the number of grains of sand of the great river Ganges all completely assumed forms and became maras, and having emanated that many maras from each single hair of each of those maras, they sought an opportunity [to harm] that bodhisattva who has attained non-conceptual pristine wisdom, all of them would not be able to cause an obstacle even to the extent of moving a single hair of that one who is skilled in means. ^3-286

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-287]]

Having thus taught passing beyond the mara of the devaputra, to teach passing beyond the four maras without exception, it is stated: Due to four kinds of causes, that bodhisattva who is skilled in means and possesses the strength of having directly realized emptiness is difficult to be defeated by the four maras, such as the mara of the devaputra, and cannot be moved from the path of the Great Vehicle. What are those four causes? Abiding in the wisdom that realizes phenomena to be empty by nature; great compassion that does not completely abandon accomplishing the purpose of sentient beings; the firm commitment that irreversibly accomplishes unsurpassable awakening for the sake of sentient beings, just as stated in the words of the promise and commitment that were made; and possessing the blessings of the Sugatas, having pleased the Victorious Ones by accumulating great collections of merit and pristine wisdom through making offerings to the Buddhas and so forth. Thus, if they possess those four kinds of causes, they cannot be harmed by the four maras and are able to defeat all maras. Thus it is said. ^3-287

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-288]]

To explain the remedies for those four conceptions, the three causes of the peak training of the path of seeing—teaching the path of awakening to others, imparting the words and meaning of the perfection of wisdom, and the cause for having no obstacles, which is exerting oneself in practice—and so forth, the meaning of the six verses stated starting with "The Mother of the Tathagatas..." is: When a bodhisattva perfectly teaches the path of seeing and so forth to others in order for them to attain great awakening, and when they explain, write in letters, and so forth this perfection of wisdom, the Mother that generates the Tathagatas, whatever bodhisattva feels heartfelt devotion with supreme joy and also gives it to others, and exerts themselves with sincere resolve in practicing or accomplishing this path themselves, that certain bodhisattva, whose continuum is gentle with love and compassion, should be known as having entered the very path of omniscience. As for the way of abiding without abiding because—although the great awakening subsumed by the peak training of the path of seeing exhausts stains—ultimately there is no exhaustion and no arising: bodhisattvas do not ultimately abide even in suchness, the ultimate nature of things, the dharma expanse; they abide without abiding by strongly adhering to anything whatsoever. For example, just as clouds in the sky do not rest steadily on anything whatsoever, they abide in suchness free from all elaborations without abiding by way of adhering to any phenomenon whatsoever as truly existent. ^3-288

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-289]]

Just as when a mantra-holder roaming in the sky without any support to abide on, such as a house or dwelling, desires to abide, by the power of the mantra, in a single instant when it is time to arise in the sky, they bless and make appear whatever place they desire, such as beautiful and delightful parks endowed with captivating flowers, trees, and so forth, without it having arisen from the ground and so forth. Like that example, by the blessings of the unsurpassable mantra of the non-conceptual perfection of wisdom, that bodhisattva who practices the conduct of the Victor's children, such as establishing sentient beings in ripening and liberation, who is skilled in the method of accomplishing the purpose of others, and whose intelligence is clear in realizing the meaning of the natural state, does not apprehend the phenomena of the path that cause realization as truly existent objects of engagement, nor do they apprehend the qualities of a Buddha to be attained or what are not the means for attaining them as truly existent objects to be turned away from; and by abandoning the cognitive obscurations and conceptions—such as apprehending persons like the one explaining the dharma as substantially existent, and apprehending the person desiring the dharma, who is viewed as the very object of the dharma explanation, as a truly existent imputed person—they do not apprehend them as such, and this realization of all phenomena as empty of true existence is the abiding of the bodhisattvas who conventionally desire to pacify the suffering of sentient beings and delight in the path of the Great Vehicle and the qualities of the result such as the ten powers. Therefore, the great difference in the way of abiding from that of hearers, solitary realizers, and so forth is: except for the very abiding of the Tathagatas, who are arhats having abandoned the two obscurations and abiding in the preeminent samadhis such as the eight liberations, the totalities, and the outshinings, this abiding of the bodhisattvas has become the supreme of all the abidings of others, the hearers and solitary realizers, however many there are, which are endowed with the undefiled joy and bliss of the samadhi that pacifies afflictions without exception and thoroughly pacifies all suffering; and because there is nothing higher than that, it is an unsurpassable abiding. ^3-289

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-290]]

Although they abide in the samadhi that pacifies all elaborations, they do not fall into the extreme of peace where working for the purpose of others is interrupted. For example, just as winged birds abide in the supportless sky but do not fall to the ground, and fish abide in water but do not die from their ears and noses being blocked, in that way, although bodhisattvas who have attained skill in means have gone beyond the level of ordinary beings through the powers of meditative concentration, and having given rise in their continuum to the direct realization of the truth on the path of seeing, abide one-pointedly in emptiness free from elaborations, they will not have their minds captivated by the bliss of peace and pass into a nirvana where the purpose of others is interrupted, because they possess the preeminent powers of compassion and previous prayers of aspiration. ^3-290

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-291]]

Therefore, the way one must train in the perfection of wisdom if one desires to attain the state of the three bodies is: Therefore, whoever desires to go to the supreme of all those qualities of all sentient beings, however many there are—that is, desires to attain the truth body; and desires to touch the pristine wisdom of the Buddha that is supremely wondrous and marvelous, which has become the supreme of all qualities; and, having awakened themselves, desires to give the best, supreme, and holy gift of dharma to sentient beings through inconceivable emanated displays, should rely on this supreme abiding of the Victorious Ones and their Children, which accomplishes the benefit and bliss of sentient beings without exception. Thus it is said. From the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, the Lamp of Jewels That Illuminates the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, this is the commentary on the twenty-seventh chapter, the essence chapter. ^3-291

###### **Nature of the Path of Seeing** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-5-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-292]]

The nature of the path of seeing: Having thus explained the secondary topics, second, the nature of the path of seeing. The meaning of what is stated in the four verses starting with "This training" is: This training of the perfection of wisdom, which has become the very nature of the great awakening of the path of seeing, has become the supreme of all the measureless trainings of the bodhisattvas that the Guides, the perfect Buddhas, have completely expressed, however many trainings they have taught; and because there is no other higher than this, it is unsurpassed; therefore, whichever wise one desires to go to the level of a Buddha, the other shore or culmination of all trainings, should train in this perfection of wisdom, the path of the Great Vehicle, the training of a Buddha. Explaining how this very path of seeing is the treasury of the twelve hundred qualities and so forth; the storehouse of the thirty-six aspects, grouping the six perfections within each of giving and so forth; and how one is born into the family of the Buddhas and how all the qualities of the ultimate ten grounds arise from this, along with a pair of examples, is: This path of seeing, such as the patience of knowing dharma regarding suffering, is the source of the twelve hundred qualities and so forth, such as seeing the faces of Buddhas, so it is the supreme great treasury of dharma; and it is the holy, wondrous storehouse of dharma with vast wealth of qualities, such as the thirty-six perfections of giving and so forth, dhāraṇīs, samādhis, and factors of awakening; and one is born into the family from which the Buddha himself arises, because one is certain to attain the state of a Buddha. Progressing sequentially through the ten grounds, such as the first ground, the Very Joyful, and reaching the culmination of all qualities is the storehouse from which all the temporary and ultimate bliss and happiness of transmigrators arises and abides; and although all the Protectors of the World of the ten directions, the Buddhas, the transcendent and accomplished conquerors, however many there are—those of the past who have already come, those who have not yet come, and those currently residing—are born from practicing this path of the perfection of wisdom, the natural perfection of wisdom, the dharmadhātu, never becomes exhausted, but abides free from decrease and increase. Therefore, explaining in order to show through examples that this is the cause of all qualities: For example, just as all trees, fruits, flowers, forests, and so forth, however many there are, genuinely arise from the earth and come forth upon the earth; yet, although it produces all the trees and so forth again and again, the earth is not exhausted by way of being interrupted, nor does it increase higher and higher, nor does it completely diminish by changing into another state of a thing, nor does it conceptualize, thinking, "I have produced the plants, forests, and so forth," and even though it produces fruits again and again, it is without weariness or frustration, because they arise from the collection of causes, conditions, and dependent arising, but there is no personal self who is a creator. ^3-292

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-293]]

Likewise, all the qualities of bliss, happiness, and excellence of all transmigrating sentient beings, however many there are—the Buddhas' children, the bodhisattvas; hearers and solitary realizers; gods such as Brahmā and Indra; and humans and so forth—all arise from meditating on the supreme perfection of wisdom, the path of seeing, because by practicing the path of the Great Vehicle, they awaken perfectly and completely, and from their teaching the dharma, all higher elevations and definite goodness arise. Thus, although all the bliss and goodness of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa arises in dependence upon the perfection of wisdom, that wisdom never becomes exhausted, nor does it increase, in the ultimate sense. Thus it is said. ^3-293

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-294]]

Because merely meditating on emptiness in meditative equipoise is not sufficient, but one must also become familiar with the forward and reverse progressions of the twelve links of dependent arising during the subsequent attainment, explaining this very method: The meaning of what is stated in the three verses starting with "Lower sentient beings" is: The Sugatas have taught that in the world, in relation to body and resources, all the classes of sentient beings, however many there are—the lower sentient beings, those of the lower realms; the middling, humans and so forth; and the supreme, gods—do not arise from a Principal, a Lord, permanent Time, and so forth, but arise from the ignorance of self-grasping; by the power of the ignorance of grasping at a personal self, which is the contradictory and discordant class to awareness and pristine wisdom, karma is accumulated, and when conditions assemble, such as being nourished by craving, grasping, and so forth, which are the cooperative conditions of karma, the projected results of birth, aging, sickness, death, and so forth are established, and this great mechanism of the suffering of saṃsāra, such as the completely afflicted twelve links of dependent arising, genuinely arises again and again; yet, even so, that mechanism of ignorance in the continuums of sentient beings is not exhausted by the arising of results, nor does it increase, but remains just as it is. ^3-294

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-295]]

The pristine wisdom of the Buddha, the path of meditation that accords with its mode, its point of entry, the path of seeing, the method for generating it, the path of preparation, and its root, the path of accumulation—however many there are, all of these arise from the supreme perfection of wisdom that realizes selflessness. Because one repeatedly familiarizes oneself with that very pristine wisdom that realizes selflessness and accumulates a vast collection of merit, ignorance ceases, and because one does not accumulate karma that projects saṃsāra, the mechanism of suffering does not arise. By being taught by a spiritual friend, conditions of method such as meditating on love and compassion assemble, and the mechanism of pristine wisdom, such as the ten grounds and five paths of the Great Vehicle, genuinely arises, and its root, the perfection of wisdom that realizes the abiding nature, is never exhausted, nor does it increase in the ultimate sense, but abides conventionally as dependent arising, like a magical illusion. Thus, it is said in the Gaganagañjaparipṛcchā Sūtra: "If one genuinely knows all dharmas / That arise in dependence upon causes, / That treasury is inexhaustible; / The treasury of dharma is inconceivable. / The protector of the world has genuinely taught / That these four dharmas are inexhaustible: / Sentient beings, space, bodhicitta, / And likewise, the qualities of a Buddha. / If they were substantial, / They would become completely exhausted. / Because they are non-existent, they are called 'inexhaustible'; / Therefore they are taught as inexhaustible." Therefore, it is said that whichever bodhisattva knows this profound meaning of the perfection of wisdom—that all dharmas of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa conventionally arise in dependence, and if one investigates and searches for them with reasoning, not even the slightest bit is found, so one completely knows that they are without arising by their own intrinsic nature, and without exhaustion, that is, without cessation—penetrates the profound precious treasury of the Buddha's speech and sees the dharmakāya. As it is said in the Suhṛllekha: "This dependent arising is the profound, / Precious treasury of the Victor's speech. / Whoever genuinely sees this / Sees the Buddha, the supreme knower of suchness." Thus, by knowing the meaning of the profound abiding nature, emptiness, familiarizing oneself with it, and abiding on the path of meditation, such as the meditative absorption of leap-over, just as, for example, the sun, being without conceptuality and radiating light, dispels darkness without exception, so too, having destroyed the thick darkness of the obscurations of ignorance along with its latent tendencies without exception, one will attain the supreme pristine wisdom of a spontaneously arisen Buddha, just as it was prophesied that the six hundred monks will become Tathāgatas named Avakīrṇakusuma. This is the commentary on the twenty-eighth chapter, the Chapter of Avakīrṇakusuma, from The Fulfiller of the Hopes of the Fortunate: A Jeweled Lamp Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, a Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection. ^3-295

###### **Peak Application of the Path of Meditation** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-6-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-296]]

The peak training of the path of meditation. To indicate the sixth, the peak training of the path of meditation, the Ornament states: "The nine meditative absorptions, along with the cessation... / ...the nine other aspects of discordant classes." Regarding this, there are three points. The first: the definition of the peak training of the path of meditation is that subsequent realization of the Great Vehicle which abides in the class of the actual antidote to the seeds of conceptualizations to be abandoned by the path of meditation. Second, if it is divided, there are the nine uninterrupted paths that are the actual antidotes to the nine cycles of conceptualizations to be abandoned by the path of meditation, and the nine cycles of paths of complete liberation of the path of meditation. Third, its limits are from the first ground up to the tenth ground. To explain such a peak training of the path of meditation, three verses beginning with "Possessing great power" are stated. Moreover, to indicate the two conceptualizations of grasping at an object out of the four conceptualizations of grasped and grasper that are to be abandoned: The bodhisattva on the path of meditation who is "possessing great power" of qualities, such as having abandoned the two conceptualizations of grasping at an object of the cognitive obscuration, abides by way of the four meditative absorptions of the actual dhyānas, and also familiarizes themselves with the leap-over samādhi; and at that time, they do not rely on them by way of clinging to the grasped object as true, nor do they abide by way of relishing the bliss of meditative absorption, because they directly realize the grasped object as being without truth. However, these four actual dhyānas and meditative absorptions along with their branches, such as the samādhi, joy, and bliss of the first, become the support for attaining the qualities of abandonment and realization of supreme, unsurpassed holy awakening, because all paths of seeing and meditation rely on the actual dhyānas. Although Buddhaśrī applies what is from here onwards to the uninterrupted peak training, according to the Master Haribhadra, the method of accomplishing the welfare of others by having abandoned the two conceptualizations of grasping at a grasper of the cognitive obscuration, familiarizing oneself with limitless samādhis, and taking various births is as follows: The bodhisattva who has attained the supreme perfection of wisdom that realizes the abiding nature, and who abides in the four dhyānas of the path of meditation, enters into and experiences the meditative absorptions of the four holy formless samādhis and the meditative absorption of cessation and so forth, yet does not cling to them as true. Because these formless dhyānas are favorable conditions for higher knowledges and miraculous powers and so forth, they become conditions that benefit the attainment of the supreme state of a holy awakened Buddha; therefore they train in them, but that bodhisattva does not train in order to abide in the bliss of peace after afflictions or defilements are exhausted, like the Śrāvakas and Pratyekabuddhas, because they do not have the mind that seeks peace for their own welfare. This method is the wonderful and marvelous quality of the bodhisattvas who gather or possess limitless qualities, such as having abandoned the one hundred and eight conceptualizations of grasped and grasper of the cognitive obscuration. And what is that? Having abided in samādhis such as the four dhyānas, they realize them to be without even the slightest mark of being truly established; and for those abiding on that path of meditation who have attained the eight dhyānas and formless absorptions, even after their bodies have perished, because the dhyāna branches of the meditative absorptions that are the causes are completely exhausted, by that power they do not take birth in the upper realms, but take birth even in the desire realm for the welfare of others, such as ripening sentient beings, just according to their own desire or intention. ^3-296

###### **Uninterrupted Peak Application** ^3-3-2-2-1-2-7-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-297]]

The uninterrupted peak training. To indicate the seventh, the uninterrupted peak training, the Ornament states: "The Śrāvakas in the trichiliocosm... / ...the uninterrupted samādhi." Regarding this, there are three points: the definition, the divisions, and the limits. The first: the definition of the uninterrupted peak training is that ultimate yoga of a bodhisattva—held by wisdom that is superior to the path of accumulation of the Great Vehicle which meditates upon the three knowledges in a condensed manner—that actually produces the omniscient knowledge that becomes its own result. It is synonymous with the knowledge at the end of the continuum. Second, if it is divided, there are the four momentary trainings. Third, its limit is exclusively at the end of the continuum. To explain the uninterrupted peak training wherein they abide in existence but are not tainted by the flaws of existence, like a lotus in water: For example, if a person from Jambudvīpa who had never before gone to a place in the realm of the gods or the divine city of Sudarśana and so forth which are perfect and supreme, later went there by miraculous powers and so forth, and having seen the pleasing objects such as the palaces and pleasure groves completely held by the gods there, was not attached to them and later came back to this Jambudvīpa, they would become nauseated by their own objects and houses and so forth, see them as unpleasing, and not develop attachment. In the same way as that example, to explain the wisdom that realizes truthlessness and the love, compassion, and so forth of a bodhisattva which become the supreme basis and support for the qualities of the path of meditation: Making effort in the yoga of the uninterrupted samādhi, having abided in samādhis such as the four dhyānas and the eight liberations, they see the supreme, excellent qualities of a Buddha, and therefore do not cling to the samādhis and the joy and bliss of the dhyānas and formless realms, and having abandoned birth by the power of that, they "again" abide "in the desire realm"—meaning not just the single lower realm, but rather they abide wherever they wish in the desire realm, and they have no attachment to any phenomena of the three realms; therefore, although they abide in saṃsāra, they are not tainted by the flaws of saṃsāra—just as a lotus born from water is not tainted by water, they do not abide in childish phenomena. ^3-297

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-298]]

The eighth, the mistaken practices that are the objects to be eliminated. To indicate this, the Ornament states: "The tenability of the object of focus, and its... / ...are asserted as the sixteen aspects." Regarding this, there are three points: the definition, the divisions, and the limits. The first: the definition of the mistaken practice that is the object to be eliminated taught here is that which is subsumed by either the seeds or the manifest forms, whichever is appropriate, of apprehending that the two truths cannot be combined into a single essence. Second, if it is divided: mistaken conceptualization regarding the tenability of the object of focus, mistaken conceptualization regarding definitely apprehending the nature of the object of focus, mistaken conceptualization regarding the knowledge of omniscience, mistaken conceptualization regarding the two truths, mistaken conceptualization regarding the training, mistaken conceptualization regarding the Three Jewels, mistaken conceptualization regarding skill in means, mistaken conceptualization regarding the realization of the Muni, mistaken conceptualization regarding the perverted, mistaken conceptualization regarding the path, mistaken conceptualization regarding the discordant class and the antidote, mistaken conceptualization regarding the definition of phenomena, and mistaken conceptualization regarding meditation—there are sixteen. Third, its limit is from before entering the path up to the seventh ground. According to Buddhaśrī, the mistaken practices that are the objects to be eliminated themselves are asserted to be taught from the earlier line, "These are the marvellous qualities, etc." However, according to our own system, following the assertion of the Master, the five verses starting with "The great beings..." are spoken. Moreover, the way in which they perfectly accomplish the two aims without being attached or clinging to any of the three spheres, as an antidote to those who mistakenly engage by thinking, "If all phenomena lack true existence, how could objects of focus, and adopting and discarding what is sought after, and so forth, be tenable?", is this: Although that bodhisattva attains the eight dhyānas and formless realms, they have no manifest clinging to them, and without taking birth in the formless realms and so forth, the purpose for taking birth in the desire realm and so forth is solely to accomplish the aims of others—for those "great beings" to completely mature sentient beings through teaching the Dharma and so forth, to purify the buddhafield where they themselves will become a buddha, and to completely perfect the perfections and so forth—there is no other purpose. Therefore, because there is no teaching of the Dharma, objects of giving, and so forth in the formless realm, thinking, "The practice of the perfections such as giving which generate the qualities of awakening and the vast accomplishment of the aims of others would degenerate," that bodhisattva does not seek out and take "birth in the formless realm." ^3-298

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-299]]

The manner of this is, for example, if a person were to find treasures made of jewels, but at that time there were enemies or they would be wasted, they would not develop the mind of being attached to them or desiring to take them, and they would not take them; but later, when there are no enemies, by the power of necessity at another time that person would fully take those great treasures for the sake of others, and having taken them, would go to their own home and, without being attached to them, would give them away to relatives and friends, and so forth. In the same way, bodhisattvas who are skilled in the nature of the two truths, who bestow temporary joy and ultimate bliss upon sentient beings, having attained the four samādhis subsumed by the main practices of the dhyānas which are pacified of the flaws of dullness and agitation, have no attachment or manifest clinging to possessing the bliss of the dhyānas, and letting go of the attained samādhis of the dhyānas that produce results, those who possess affection for all wandering sentient beings as if they were their only child proceed to take birth "again" in the desire realm for the sake of others, because the immeasurable aims of others will be accomplished. ^3-299

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-300]]

The way in which one engages even more mistakenly if one clings to adopting, discarding, and so forth as truly existent is this: If a bodhisattva abides in the eight liberations, the samādhis of the totalities and the outshining, and the four dhyānas, but has manifest clinging to them and generates the mind desiring to attain the vehicles of the śrāvaka arhats and the pratyekabuddhas for their own aim, such a one is uncomposed because it is discordant with the path of the Mahāyāna, and agitated because the reference point of grasping at true existence has not been destroyed, and their mind has become distracted externally from the path of the Mahāyāna, thus fully degenerating from the great aim, the qualities of a buddha, just like, for example, a boatman whose boat breaks in the water. ^3-300

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-301]]

Therefore, the need to train on the path in a manner like an illusion is this: Furthermore, other than that manner, whatever bodhisattva, out of joy and attachment to the five sense qualities—beautiful forms, sweet sounds, and likewise fragrant smells, pleasing tastes, and excellent tactile sensations—were to manifestly exert themselves in accomplishing them, yet is free from the mind that desires and seeks the vehicles of the śrāvaka arhats and pratyekabuddhas, and who rejoices in generating and increasing the mind of awakening whose essence is emptiness and compassion and in accomplishing solely the aims of others, that hero should be known as being always composed, because their accomplishment of unsurpassed awakening for the sake of others does not degenerate and their mind remains one-pointed. Thus, having finished teaching the eight phenomena that characterize the training of the peak, the concluding summary of the chapter is this: This is the commentary on the fifth chapter from the Commentary on the Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom (Ratnaguṇasañcayagāthā) entitled "The Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising." ^3-301

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-302]]

Regarding the explanation of the presentation of the four trainings of practice, having finished explaining the mastery, cause and effect, ^3-302

###### Explanation of the Cause and Effect of Stability ^3-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-303]]

The explanation of the stability, cause and effect: Second, regarding the explanation of the stability, cause and effect, although one attains mastery over the aspects of the three knowledges through the mastery, cause and effect above, that alone is not sufficient, and one must make it stable; therefore, there are two parts: the explanation of the cause of stability, the progressive training, and the explanation of the result, the momentary training. From between these two: ^3-303

###### **Gradual Application as the Cause of Stability** ^3-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-304]]

The cause of stability, the progressive training: The first is: In the Ornament, having briefly taught it as "The progressive [training] is the thirteen aspects," it teaches it extensively by way of its branches, stating, "From generosity up to wisdom..." down to "...is asserted as the progressive training." Regarding this, there are three [topics]: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: The definition of the progressive training is a bodhisattva yoga embraced by the wisdom that sequentially cultivates the aspects of the three knowledges in order to attain stability in the aspects of the three knowledges. Second, if divided, there are the six from generosity up to wisdom, the six [recollections] from the Buddha, Dharma, Saṅgha, ethical discipline, and generosity, up to the recollection of deities, and the progressive training on the non-existent nature—thirteen in all. Third, the boundaries are from the Mahāyāna path of accumulation up to right before the end of the continuum. As for this progressive training, although Buddhaśrī applies it from "The bodhisattva's generosity cuts off the hungry ghosts' wanderings..." up to "...also teaches the Dharma to wandering beings," our own tradition follows the Master [Haribhadra]. The meaning taught by the single verse starting with "Whatever sentient beings..." is this: In order that benefit and bliss arise for all other sentient beings, however many there may be, who wander in saṃsāra, those who are brave by having donned the armor of accomplishing unsurpassed awakening and who are perfectly pure from the stain of mental engagement in their own aims manifestly exert themselves with reverence in the six perfections beginning with generosity, the six recollections beginning with the recollection of the Buddha, and the perfection of joyous effort which trains on the path realizing that all phenomena lack a truly existent nature; and they perfectly practice by cultivating those thirteen phenomena with the joyous effort of continuous application. For example, just as a female servant of someone like a king—that is, a female water-drawer—is under the control of her master, without the power to do whatever she desires for herself, similarly, hero bodhisattvas proceed or engage entirely under the control of others, solely accomplishing the aims of all sentient beings. ^3-304

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-305]]

Gungtang Jamyang stated: "I think that this teaches that by recollecting the benefits of the six perfections, one manifestly exerts oneself in them; by recollecting the Three Jewels, ethical discipline, and deities, one places whatever one does under their control rather than engaging as one pleases; and by recollecting the lack of nature, the mind is perfectly pure." He [also] stated: "To explain the progressive training, there is nothing in the Aṣṭasāhasrikā other than this statement: 'In order to roar the lion's roar, one should know the perfection of wisdom.'" Thus, having finished teaching the thirteen phenomena that characterize the progressive training, the concluding summary of the chapter is this: This is the commentary on the sixth chapter from the Commentary on the Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom (Ratnaguṇasañcayagāthā) entitled "The Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising." ^3-305

###### **Momentary Application as the Result** ^3-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-306]]

The result, the momentary training: Second, the result, the momentary training: In the Ornament, having briefly taught it as "In a single moment, manifest complete awakening," it teaches it extensively by stating, "Also each one, such as generosity..." down to "...sees that veryness in a single moment." Regarding this, there are three [topics]: the definition, the divisions, and the boundaries. The first: The definition of the momentary training is a final bodhisattva yoga that has attained stability in the aspects of the three knowledges. That, the pristine wisdom at the end of the continuum, and the uninterrupted peak training are three with the same meaning. Second, if divided, there are four that are identical in nature but conceptually distinct isolates. Because there are four: the non-maturational momentary training, the maturational momentary training, and the non-dual momentary training. Third, the boundaries are exclusively at the end of the continuum. Although Buddhaśrī applies it to only the statement, "The bodhisattva, having completely perfected these phenomena...", our own tradition follows the Master; the meaning of what is stated in the two verses beginning with "Whether scolded..." to explain that very momentary training is this: For example, whether scolded by the master speaking badly with his speech, or even constantly beaten and struck with the body, that female servant does not speak words replying back to whatever that master commands, but thinking, "If things go wrong, I will be killed by him, because I have no independence and am under his control," with a greatly terrified mind, possessing the fear of being killed, she listens to and accomplishes whatever he says; just as she is overpowered by that master in the example, similarly, bodhisattvas who have engaged in practicing the path to supreme awakening, such as the four trainings, for the sake of attaining unsurpassed awakening for others, should abide by pleasing them, as if they had become servants to all wandering beings; thinking, "Through pleasing these wandering beings and accomplishing solely their benefit and bliss, I will attain unsurpassed awakening and the qualities of abandonment and realization will be completely perfected," they engage in accordance with the intentions of sentient beings and accomplish solely the aims of others. If they do not do so, getting angry at sentient beings and the like will destroy the roots of virtue in their own continuums through anger, pride, and so forth, just as a fire arising from grass and wood burns that very grass and wood; therefore, extracting those from the root, they constantly exert themselves in accomplishing the benefit and bliss of other sentient beings. As for how this teaches the momentary training: by illustrating it through teaching the ultimate culmination of familiarity with the exceptional resolve, it teaches the final completion of all the bodhisattva paths. When explaining that very thing in the Aṣṭasāhasrikā, it is taught by this very statement: "In order to not be disturbed, one should know the perfection of wisdom." Furthermore, because "not disturbed" and "unwavering" have the same meaning, Gungtang Jamyang stated: "It means that at the time of attaining the momentary training, it is impossible to be swayed or waver through mental engagements of the Hinayana, grasping at true existence, and the like." Thus, having finished explaining the four phenomena that characterize the momentary training, the concluding summary of the chapter is this: This is the commentary on the seventh chapter from the Commentary on the Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom (Ratnaguṇasañcayagāthā) entitled "The Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising." ^3-306

##### The Resultant Dharmakaya ^3-3-2-3-0

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-307]]

The result, the dharmakāya: Third, to teach the result, the dharmakāya: In the Ornament, it states from "The nature body of the Sage..." down to "...asserted as twenty-seven aspects." The definition of the result, the dharmakāya, is the final result attained through the force of meditating on the aspects of the three knowledges, which have become the means of attaining it. That and Buddha have the same meaning. If divided, there are four: because there are four, the nature body, the pristine wisdom truth body, the enjoyment body, and the emanation body; as it is said, "The nature, together with complete enjoyment, likewise the other, the emanation, and the dharmakāya together with its activities—these four aspects are perfectly expressed." The boundaries are exclusively at the level of Buddhahood. To teach the enlightened activity of a Buddha, the Ornament states: from "Likewise, as long as this cyclic existence [remains]..." down to "...asserted as twenty-seven aspects." Regarding this, there are five [topics]: the definition, the divisions, the boundaries, the manner of engagement, and the duration. The first: The definition of a Buddha's enlightened activity is the perfectly pure qualities arising from the pristine wisdom truth body, which is its own cause, acting as the dominant condition. Second, if divided, there are two: enlightened activity present in the object of action, and enlightened activity present in the agent; and if divided extensively from that, there are twenty-seven, from the enlightened activity of establishing beings in peaceful actions down to the enlightened activity of establishing them in nirvāṇa. Third: It exists from the stage of not having entered the path up to the level of Buddhahood. Fourth: Regarding the manner in which a Buddha's enlightened activity engages the continuums of disciples, there are infinite ways, because in accordance with the respective fortunes of the disciples, the extensive and uninterrupted enlightened activities of body, speech, and mind engage effortlessly and spontaneously like a wish-granting tree and a wish-fulfilling jewel. Fifth: As for the duration, a Buddha's enlightened activity is uninterrupted until cyclic existence is emptied, because the Ornament states: "Likewise, as long as this cyclic existence [remains], this activity is asserted as uninterrupted." To explain that very result, the dharmakāya, the meaning of what is stated in the one verse beginning with "The happiness of oneself..." is this: "Thus, having taken that very precious mind of awakening of equalizing and exchanging self and others as the basis of the path, and having practiced the conduct of the Conqueror's children—the six perfections, the four means of drawing disciples, and so forth—one gradually traverses the ten grounds and five paths; having actualized the four bodies, abiding without wavering from the dharmakāya as long as space remains, through non-apprehending great compassion, having cast away the mind that desires 'the happiness of oneself,' and with a mind that has no hope for a response or a ripened result, one manifestly exerts oneself day and night constantly, without distraction and without letting the time pass, in accomplishing the welfare and needs of other sentient beings; just as, for example, a mother tends to her only child with a mind free of weariness in all ways, like the Buddhas, the Bhagavāns who solely accomplish the aims of others, you should know to abide without weariness in the aims of sentient beings from the depths of your heart." As for how this teaches the dharmakāya: it teaches that, having actualized the four bodies, abiding without weariness until cyclic existence is emptied, one accomplishes the aims of wandering beings through the twenty-seven enlightened activities. When explaining that very thing in the Aṣṭasāhasrikā, it is stated from "Subhūti, by whatever the bodhisattva, the great being, thus subsequently knows and perfectly practices the perfection of wisdom..." down to "...the twenty-ninth chapter, on Subsequently Knowing." Thus, having finished explaining the four phenomena that characterize the dharmakāya, the concluding summary of the chapter is this: This is the twenty-ninth chapter on Subsequently Knowing and the commentary on the eighth chapter from the Commentary on the Verse Summary of the Perfection of Wisdom (Ratnaguṇasañcayagāthā) entitled "The Jewel Lamp Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate, Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising." ^3-307

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-308]]

From this point downwards, there is no correlation with the Ornament. Although a system of correlating it has existed from previously, the master Haribhadra refuted it. As for our own system, explaining in accordance with the master's assertion: in order to generate in one's own continuum the eight clear realizations as explained above, one must be preceded by listening to, reflecting on, and meditating on the Mother, the perfection of wisdom. Therefore, having earnestly sought a fully qualified spiritual friend of the Great Vehicle and having pleased them by practicing according to whatever they say, the manner of engaging in earnest practice with great patience, without the mind becoming weary from the suffering of ascetic hardships on the path, through intense and uninterrupted irreversible joyous effort, is as follows: The meaning taught by the four verses beginning with "Whatever bodhisattva..." is this: If a yogi—who, out of affection and great compassion, desires to accomplish the aim of others by remaining in this place of cyclic existence for an immeasurably long time and never giving up acting for the welfare of sentient beings; and who exerts themselves in activities for wandering beings, such as completely maturing sentient beings by remaining in cyclic existence for the sake of others, and in purifying their own Buddha-field, completely fulfilling their aspirations, and so forth—does not generate even an atom of a mind of weariness towards the long duration of time and the suffering of ascetic hardships and so forth, then they are endowed with the three types of the perfection of joyous effort, such as acting for the welfare of sentient beings, and will become undistracted in accomplishing the aims of others. ^3-308

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-309]]

If one asks, "What fault is there in generating a mind of weariness?" To teach that: If any bodhisattva unskilled in means counts the measure of tens of millions of eons and so forth, thinking, "Having exerted myself for this long, I will attain the awakening of a Buddha," and if they practice for awakening through the perception of such a long time and the perception of suffering in giving away their head, limbs, and so forth, then at that time of accomplishing the pure dharma of unsurpassable awakening for the sake of others, through the power of that very perception, it will become a long, suffering, and difficult task. And because of that erroneous perception, they will degenerate from the perfection of joyous effort and become lazy, experiencing discouragement, self-contempt, and so forth. ^3-309

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-310]]

If one asks, "Then how does one not generate a mind of weariness?" Wise bodhisattvas must generate courage without looking at the long duration of time and the difficulty of the tasks, like the noble Sadāprarudita. If, beginning from the very first generation of the mind set on supreme awakening until finally attaining the state of a perfectly complete Buddha of unsurpassable great awakening, they mentally consider all those periods of time as being as easy to pass as the measure of a single day and night, and generate a perception of joy, then bodhisattvas skilled in means will have destroyed the darkness of laziness through the luminosity of the wisdom that realizes all phenomena to be empty of true existence and illusion-like; you should know that the manner of exerting joyous effort is like that. Having generated courage and joy, thinking, "Although I have experienced intense and uninterrupted suffering in this place of cyclic existence since beginningless lifetimes, I have had no weariness or aversion, so why should I not bear the suffering of ascetic hardships now in accomplishing the excellent path of the supreme vehicle that will make me attain the great purpose, the state of a Buddha?"—through the power of that, they will overwhelm the long duration of time and all suffering, and their joyous effort will extensively increase. ^3-310

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-311]]

If someone else were to say to them these words, "Destroy this Mount Meru, the king of mountains, and after you have destroyed it you will attain supreme awakening," and having heard that, if they were to think, "How can I destroy this Mount Meru which is extremely vast, high, and firm?" and generate a mind of difficulty and weariness, and also generate or think about the measure of time such as eons—at the time of generating such a discouraged mind, that bodhisattva has become lazy, because their joyous mind has degenerated. Whatever bodhisattva thinks, "If unsurpassable awakening is attained with the measure of just this much joyous effort, why is this difficult at all?" and, without holding to a measure of time, generates courage and a joyous mind that is capable of crushing that Mount Meru into dust in a mere instant of time, that bodhisattva of great courage and skilled in means has exerted the supreme joyous effort; without taking a long time, they will attain the supreme awakening of a Lord Buddha, leading wandering beings out of cyclic existence. ^3-311

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-312]]

Even if one exerts joyous effort in that way, it is important to be without adherence. If one exerts oneself in accomplishing the welfare of others with body, speech, and mind from within a state of perception that apprehends characteristics of self and other, thinking, "Through the wondrous conduct of being skilled in means to tame whoever needs taming, such as teaching the profound and vast dharma, I shall completely mature wandering beings and accomplish the welfare of others, such as placing them on the grounds and paths!"—because one abides in the perception of a personal self, it becomes laziness relative to the supramundane objectless joyous effort. Because one is bound by the apprehension of characteristics, it has become the discordant class of pure joyous effort, and therefore one will become far from omniscience itself, just as, for example, a place and the ground are far apart. ^3-312

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-313]]

If one asks, "Then in what manner should one exert joyous effort?" When exerting joyous effort, one does not have a perception of the agent exerting it, of one's own body and mind, or of others as sentient beings, and the perception holding the three spheres of joyous effort to be truly existent is completely reversed; practicing the way of phenomena as empty of true existence and illusion-like, without falling into the two extremes of permanence and annihilation—this is the very perfection of joyous effort of bodhisattvas who desire to attain the state of the supreme qualities of abandonment and realization, great awakening, the pacification of all stains of the two obscurations without ever degenerating, so say the Conquerors who act for the benefit of wandering beings. ^3-313

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-314]]

The manner of having to train in the perfection of patience by being unconcerned about ascetic hardships and suffering, like the Conqueror's son Sadāprarudita relying on the noble Dharmodgata in order to seek the perfection of wisdom, is as follows: At the time of acting for the welfare of wandering beings, if one hears from other persons many harsh words speaking ill, such as exposing one's faults and deprecating one, the bodhisattva skilled in means will become joyful, thinking, "The practice of patience arises in dependence on the object of patience, and by its power great merit will increase, therefore this is the cause of my happiness." Not only that, having examined and analyzed with pure reasoning, when searching with rational cognition, thinking, "Who is the speaker speaking these harsh words? And who is the object spoken to that hears them? By what cause, such as anger? Expressing what? For what purpose do they speak?"—one does not find even the slightest thing. Because it is not established ultimately, having realized it to be an illusion-like dependent arising, one who is endowed with the supreme perfection of patience is wise or learned, because they are a supremely wise one who realizes that one should not become angry at phenomena that are like a dream and an illusion. ^3-314

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-315]]

If one asks, "What qualities and benefits are there in patience like that?" If any bodhisattva is endowed with the virtuous phenomena of the patience of being unconcerned about harm-doers and the patience of certainty regarding phenomena, having taken a thorough measure, if some person were to fill this great trichiliocosm with various precious things such as gold and silver and offer it to the knowers of the world, the Lord Buddhas, and to the śrāvaka arhats and pratyekabuddhas, that aggregate of generosity would not dare to compete even for a fraction of merit with that one endowed with the supreme practice of the perfection of patience. ^3-315

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-316]]

The ripened result of abiding in patience is that the body will become completely pure like refined gold, endowed with the thirty-two excellent marks such as the hands and feet being marked with wheels, and endowed with the eighty minor signs such as the strength and power of Nārāyaṇa and nails that are red like the color of copper, and so on limitlessly. The result of personal effort of the patience of certainty regarding phenomena is that one will proclaim the supremely excellent dharma, profound emptiness, to the sentient beings to be tamed; the dominant result is that in this world wandering beings without exception will pay respect to that bodhisattva endowed with patience; and the result of separation is that, being separated from the delusion of unknowing, one will become skilled in the points of what to adopt and what to reject. ^3-316

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-317]]

In order to teach the patience of voluntarily accepting suffering and the patience of individually analyzing phenomena, it was said: If some sentient beings, having taken a packet or powder of sandalwood, were to rub and anoint the body of the bodhisattva with a completely respectful mind, and some other beings, with a mind desiring to do harm, were to scatter impure fire-embers on their head—toward both of these, that bodhisattva, through the power of having meditated on phenomena as being selfless and the power of having trained in the awakening mind of equalizing and exchanging self and others, should generate an even and equal mind without attachment and aversion. If the bodhisattva, skilled in the meaning of the abiding nature, having practiced patience in that way, were to completely dedicate that generation of a mind endowed with patience to the supreme unsurpassable awakening for the welfare of others, that bodhisattva, a great hero who is brave in accomplishing awakening for the welfare of others, by meditating on a patience that benefits all sentient beings in the world, will outshine all the meditations on patience by śrāvaka arhats, pratyekabuddhas, and however many wandering beings there are in the realms of other sentient beings, because it is embraced by special skillful means. ^3-317

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-318]]

The one who practices patience should also generate their mind thinking this: "In the hells of sentient beings, the animal realm, and the world of Yama, which is the realm of hungry ghosts, there are many kinds of difficult-to-bear sufferings such as heat, cold, hunger, and thirst. All of those arise from the cause of attachment to contaminated sensory qualities, and although one experiences those harms without any freedom, they are experienced entirely meaninglessly, without even the slightest benefit to oneself or others. If this is so, for the sake of obtaining unsurpassable awakening, which brings the twofold welfare of oneself and others to perfection without exception, why should I not patiently endure the suffering of ascetic hardships now?" Having thought thus, thinking, "I will patiently endure and voluntarily accept being struck with iron, beaten with sticks, pierced by weapons such as swords, being deprived of life and killed, being bound with iron fetters, being struck with stones, fists, and so forth, having my head cut off, having my ears, hands, feet, nose, and so forth cut off, and in short, all the harms and however many sufferings there are in the world," that bodhisattva will abide in the perfection of patience just like Sadāprarudita. Thus it is said. From the commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection titled "The Jeweled Lamp that Illuminates the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate," this is the commentary on the thirtieth chapter, the chapter on Sadāprarudita. ^3-318

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-319]]

Thus, from the virtuous spiritual friend extensively granting profound and vast instructions to the suitable disciple who has properly relied upon and pleased the holy spiritual friend, since it is the foundation of all qualities, the instruction first on the perfection of moral discipline and then on the perfection of generosity and so forth is as follows. The meaning of what is stated in the eighteen verses starting with "By moral discipline": First, teaching the benefits by identifying the essence of moral discipline: "By moral discipline," such as the moral discipline of restraining faulty behavior, those who desire to obtain the state of peaceful nirvāṇa will obtain the exceptionally exalted qualities of the path, because that moral discipline is like a staircase leading to the mansion of liberation. Moreover, especially, the moral discipline of the bodhisattva "abides in the field of experience of the ten powers," which means it is the moral discipline of training in the footsteps of the Buddha, who possesses the ten powers, because it is not stained by the defilement of lower vehicle mental engagement and, since all faults and downfalls have been abandoned, the moral discipline is unimpaired. This teaches the moral discipline of restraining faulty behavior. As for "restraining," which is moral discipline, it follows after all virtuous phenomena, however many there are, such as the six perfections; because one guards against all discordant factors to be abandoned and practices their antidotes, virtuous phenomena, it is also called the moral discipline of gathering virtuous phenomena. Guarding in order to bring about benefit and happiness for all sentient beings, and dedicating that to great awakening, is the moral discipline of working for the welfare of sentient beings. Having briefly taught the essence of moral discipline in this way, just as the bodhisattva Dharmodgata, although occasionally appearing to joyously enjoy and revel in the five sensory qualities, had completely pure moral discipline, the purity or impurity of a bodhisattva's moral discipline must be posited on the basis of whether or not there is mental engagement in one's own welfare: If one guards moral discipline with a mind desiring to touch or obtain the awakening of a pratyekabuddha or śrāvaka arhat, relative to the path of the Great Vehicle, that person's moral discipline is flawed and they are unskilled in means, and likewise their intention and application are inferior, and they have fallen away from the conduct of a Conqueror's child, because they will fall into the inferior path. If, however, that very guarding of moral discipline is completely dedicated to the supreme unsurpassable awakening, the supreme pacification of the two obscurations, for the welfare of others, even if that bodhisattva abides earnestly enjoying the five sensory qualities, they abide in the perfection of moral discipline, because there is no falling away from the path of the Great Vehicle. ^3-319

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-320]]

Whichever phenomena to be trained in are practiced by the noble bodhisattvas, through which the qualities of the supreme unsurpassable awakening to be obtained will arise, that is stated to be the meaning of the moral discipline of the bodhisattvas, which is endowed with the virtuous phenomena of accomplishing the benefit and happiness of others. Whatever phenomena are tainted by the defilement of lower vehicle mental engagement, by which the minds that accomplish the benefit and happiness of others completely fall away from the result to be obtained, great awakening, this is stated by the Guide to be the flawed moral discipline of a bodhisattva. If any bodhisattva, although appearing from the perspective of worldly beings to joyously enjoy the five sensory qualities, has taken refuge from the depths of their heart in the Buddha, Dharma, and noble Saṅgha, and, thinking "I will accomplish the state of a Buddha for the welfare of other sentient beings," is not separated from mental engagement in omniscience, that bodhisattva, skilled in means, should be known to abide in the perfection of the moral discipline of the bodhisattva vows. ^3-320

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-321]]

Alternatively, if one practices the ten virtuous paths of action for hundreds of thousands of ten millions of eons, yet generates a desire to obtain for oneself the level of a śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha arhat, then a fault has occurred in the bodhisattva's moral discipline and the moral discipline uncommon to the Great Vehicle is impaired, because having abandoned the welfare of others, generating a mind desiring to obtain the awakening of a śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha for one's own welfare is a much heavier fault for that bodhisattva than the occurrence of the four root downfalls of a fully ordained monk, since they will fall away from unsurpassable awakening. ^3-321

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-322]]

If one guards moral discipline and completely dedicates it to supreme awakening, has no conceit due to adhering to that moral discipline as truly existent, does not praise oneself and deprecate others on account of having heard much, moral discipline, and so forth, and has completely abandoned the perception of a naturally established 'I' and the perception of a sentient being and so forth, that bodhisattva endowed with special means and wisdom is said to abide in the perfection of moral discipline. That one must not generate true adherence and conceit even regarding such moral discipline: If bodhisattvas practicing the phenomena of the Conqueror's path, the Great Vehicle, think, "These sentient beings are endowed with moral discipline and these have flawed moral discipline," and, having adhered to the objects to be adopted and abandoned as truly existent, engage with a diverse perception, because of being bound by the apprehension of signs they are said to have very flawed moral discipline; by the downfall of dividing self and other into factions and deprecating others and so forth, the bodhisattva's moral discipline is impaired, and that person does not have completely pure moral discipline. ^3-322

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-323]]

Therefore, explaining in order to teach that one must not manifestly adhere to true existence: Because of realizing all phenomena as empty of true existence, like an illusion, and realizing and familiarizing oneself with the selflessness of persons, which lacks the perception of self and the perception of other sentient beings with respect to any object, for that one who is free from the perception of self-apprehension and the attachment arisen by the power of that, how could there be non-vows, that is, flawed moral discipline? Because there is no cause to generate it. For whatever person there is no conceit of apprehending dualistically those endowed with vows and those with non-vows, flawed moral discipline, through knowing all phenomena as having no nature, this is stated by the Guide to be the vows of a bodhisattva's moral discipline. As for "unestablished by nature" or "empty by nature," since it means empty from its own side, it means that except for being merely designated by name and concept, it is not established upon the object or from the object. ^3-323

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-324]]

Therefore, for one who desires to enter the path of the supreme vehicle, in order to unmistakably generate the Middle Way view free from extremes in their mindstream, having properly relied on a learned spiritual friend, extensively seeking much hearing on the profound Middle Way texts and striving in accumulating merit, purifying obscurations, and so forth should be known as the supreme essence of the instructions. Explaining in order to teach that one endowed with moral discipline is able to also engage in the perfection of generosity: Whichever pure being whose mindstream is completely ripened, free from the perception of self-apprehension and the attachment arisen by the power of that just as explained earlier, and endowed with completely pure moral discipline, lacks the view of manifestly adhering to signs regarding all phenomena, attractive, i.e., agreeable, and unattractive, disagreeable. Therefore, they give even their head, limbs, and so forth to others, and at that time they do not have a mind of attachment or discouragement; by way of discarding oneself and cherishing others, they give whatever belongings or possessions one has to others, and at all times they also have no attachment to them, because, in this way, phenomena such as body, enjoyments, and so forth are natureless and selfless, arisen merely from a collection of causes and conditions; having known them as essenceless mirages, they give even the flesh of their own body to others, and if not even the slightest mind of discouragement arises regarding that, then what need is there to mention being able to give external things such as wealth, possessions, and so forth? If there were miserliness regarding those enjoyments, that is in all ways impossible, i.e., nonexistent and has no opportunity, because attachment and adherence are completely nonexistent. Teaching the drawbacks of miserliness along with the benefits of a bodhisattva's generosity: Despite 'I' or self being unestablished by nature, for fools who, perceiving a naturally established self, and, thinking 'mine' regarding things such as eyes and so forth, by apprehending them as one's own, are attached and adhere, how could they have a mind of giving away body, enjoyments, and so forth without regret? They do not; miserly ones will be born in the realms of hungry ghosts, and even if on a rare occasion they are born as humans by the power of other karma, at that time they will become poor, lacking wealth and enjoyments. ^3-324

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-325]]

When migrating beings are born in existence, bodhisattvas, having known that sentient beings have become poor by the power of being attached to enjoyments and acting with miserliness, and having themselves seen the faults of miserliness and the benefits of generosity, become inclined toward giving away all their body and enjoyments, and through application they actually give away all belongings at all times unobstructedly and exceptionally; even regarding the four continents completely filled with the seven precious things and excellently adorned with ornaments, without attachment and adherence, having known them to be essenceless like spittle and dust, in order to extract the essence of obtaining perfect awakening for the welfare of others, having given them to others, they themselves will become supremely joyful, but if they themselves were to find or obtain such continents, such joy would not arise, because they see them as impermanent and essenceless. ^3-325

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-326]]

The manner in which a bodhisattva skilled in means practices generosity is: Bodhisattvas of clear intelligence, skilled in means such as generating the mind, dedication, and so forth, and with wisdom realizing the non-apprehension of the three spheres, when practicing generosity, even if giving a single mouthful of food, by way of being preceded by the special motivation of compassion, having thought, 'May this gift be given, causing all temporary and ultimate benefit and happiness without exception to arise for all however many sentient beings there are in the expanse of the three existences below the earth, upon the earth, and above the earth!' give that gift, and they also completely dedicate the merit, the result of having given the gift, in order to obtain supreme unsurpassable awakening for the welfare of migrating beings. ^3-326

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-327]]

Having given a gift, those bodhisattvas do not abide in a manner of manifestly adhering to signs regarding any of the things of the three spheres—the giver, the substance to be given, and the recipient. Not only that, that bodhisattva never hopes, thinking, 'I will obtain great benefit and happiness as the ripening of that gift'; they give away the gift along with its result to all sentient beings. A bodhisattva who gives in the manner just explained, being endowed with special means and wisdom, is skilled in giving and gives away whatever belongings there are along with all their ripened results; by such means, even when giving a small thing, they will obtain many immeasurable ripened results. Spoken in order to teach, 'One should habituate oneself to complete dedication preceded by destroying miserliness and rejoicing': If, having completely considered to the ultimate limit however many sentient beings there are, without missing even one, existing in the three existences of desire, form, and formlessness, all of them were to give extensive gifts for infinite eons to special objects—the knowers of the world, the Buddha Bhagavāns, the śrāvaka arhats, and the pratyekabuddhas—and properly seek the awakening of a śrāvaka as the result of their giving, and if a conqueror's child, a bodhisattva, who is skilled in the meaning of the abiding nature, skilled in means, and endowed with great compassion, in a single moment of mind, having rejoiced in those merit-making things explained earlier, completely dedicates them for the welfare of all sentient beings in order to obtain the state of a buddha, the most supreme awakening, then, being endowed with such a special complete dedication, they outshine the virtuous roots of all migrating beings without exception; for example, just as even a huge heap of a multitude of piled-up glass jewels can all be outshone by a single precious lapis lazuli, a king of jewels, similarly, the exceedingly huge heap of generosity of all migrating sentient beings is entirely outshone by a bodhisattva by way of being embraced by a special dedication preceded by rejoicing. ^3-327

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-328]]

Having extensively taught the two perfections of moral discipline and generosity, similarly advising regarding the remaining perfections: If a bodhisattva, having given gifts extensively to migrating beings, does not appropriate the result of generosity as their own, and, without attachment or regret regarding the substance or thing to be given, practices the deeds of a conqueror's child, such as giving gifts in that way, from that, their great virtuous roots will increase higher and higher, perfecting the collections of merit and pristine wisdom and being endowed with great power to abandon obscurations; for example, just like the waxing face of the mandala of the moon endowed with exceedingly clear light rays due to lacking obscuring things such as clouds and so forth. Thus, advising also regarding the remaining perfections: 'One should know that the activity of a spiritual friend is like that of the Noble Dharmodgata.' This completes the commentary on the thirty-first chapter, the Chapter of Dharmodgata, from the Commentary on the Verses of the Precious Qualities Collection, the Jewel Lamp Illuminating the Suchness of Profound Dependent Arising, Fulfilling the Hopes of the Fortunate. ^3-328

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-329]]

The manner in which the practice of the ten perfections, generosity and so forth, exceptionally increases as the supreme fortunate beings—having obtained profound and extensive instructions and guidance from a spiritual friend in that way, and having generated the mind for supreme awakening—gradually traverse the ten grounds, such as the first ground, the Joyous, by practicing the deeds of a conqueror's child is as follows. The meaning of the five verses stated, beginning with 'By the generosity of a bodhisattva', is: Thus, because a bodhisattva abiding on the first ground, the Joyous, exceptionally obtains and makes principal the practice of the perfection of generosity among the ten perfections, through generosity without attachment to anything, they sever the continuum of miserliness, the cause for being born among the destinies of hungry ghosts, and they sever from the root being impoverished of resources, and likewise all afflictions such as attachment, anger, and so forth; and because by the generosity of Dharma and material things they pacify all afflictions, such as the ignorance, attachment, and anger of migrating beings, the bodhisattva, even when practicing the deeds of a conqueror's child, will in all lifetimes obtain the full expansion that increases without ever exhausting the infinite enjoyment of all desired things in harmony with the Dharma, and having first gathered them into their retinue by material generosity, by the generosity of Dharma they completely mature sentient beings who have become miserable in this state of cyclic existence by establishing them in the Great Vehicle and so forth. ^3-329

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-330]]

Likewise, bodhisattvas abiding on the second ground, the Stainless, through the eminent practice of the moral discipline of abandoning faulty discipline even in dreams, abandon the nature of the many various discordant types of animal destinies, and not only that, they also abandon all eight unfree states such as birth in border regions, and by that moral discipline they always find an excellent physical basis complete with the eight freedoms and ten endowments. By the bodhisattva abiding on the third ground, the Luminous, through patience free from all malice, a good, beautiful, wondrous, excellent, and extensive form will be obtained, as well as the body of a buddha adorned with the marks and signs, and having a golden color and excellent shape in all aspects, pleasing to look at and attractive, they become worthy to be looked upon by migrating beings. By the bodhisattva abiding on the fourth ground, the Radiant, through joyous effort free from all laziness, the qualities of white phenomena are not diminished, and by the power of all the qualities of the grounds and paths increasing and expanding higher and higher, the infinite pristine wisdom, the pristine wisdom of all-aspect omniscience, and the treasury of all the qualities of a conqueror's abandonments and realizations without exception will be found. ^3-330

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-331]]

By the one abiding on the fifth ground, the Difficult to Overcome, through the perfection of concentration free from all distractions, because sensory qualities lack essence and have great faults, they cast away those that have become objects of deprecation by holy beings; and they manifestly accomplish the three knowledges—the clairvoyance of recollecting former states, knowing the minds of others, and the exhaustion of defilements—and the clairvoyances of the divine eye, divine ear, and magical emanation, and so forth, as well as the heroic march samādhi and so forth. ^3-331

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-332]]

By the one abiding on the sixth ground, the Manifest, through the wisdom that perfectly discriminates phenomena, having thoroughly known the nature of all phenomena, emptiness, just as it is, and having abandoned the root of existence, grasping at true existence, they will pass beyond the three realms without exception in complete purity. Like the supreme leader of humanity, the Dharma King, the Buddha Bhagavān, having turned the precious wheel of the profound and extensive Dharma uninterruptedly, they will also reveal the supreme light of the holy Dharma to migrating beings in order to permanently exhaust all suffering. ^3-332

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-333]]

Although Buddhaśrī, having correlated this with the gradual training, made a chapter division from here, according to our own system, following the assertion of the Master: by the one abiding on the seventh ground, the Far-Reaching, through the power of exceptionally obtaining the perfection of skillful means, having completely perfected these phenomena of the Great Vehicle, the ten perfections and so forth, they accomplish the benefit of sentient beings. By that bodhisattva abiding on the eighth ground, the Immovable, through the power of exceptionally obtaining the perfection of aspiration, they take hold of a completely pure buddhafield—such as the completely pure realm, the environmental world of a buddha where they themselves will awaken, and the completely pure inhabiting sentient beings, being filled exclusively with bodhisattvas. ^3-333

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-334]]

By the one abiding on the ninth ground, the Excellent Intelligence, through the power of obtaining the eminent perfection of strength, having taught the Dharma in accordance with the four exact knowledges, they establish an inconceivable assembly of sentient beings on the ground of a buddha, and completely uphold the lineage of buddhas; and likewise, the bodhisattva abiding on the tenth ground, the Cloud of Dharma, having exceptionally obtained the perfection of pristine wisdom, completely upholds the lineage of the Dharma by means of uninterruptedly teaching the Dharma; and likewise, through wondrous awakened activities in accordance with a buddha, completely upholds the lineage of the Sangha by means of producing infinite ordinary and noble bodhisattvas; and abiding on the eleventh ground, the ground of a buddha, the All-Illuminating, having abandoned the two obscurations together with their latent tendencies without exception, they completely uphold all the excellent phenomena of benefit and bliss belonging to existence and peace, and for as long as space remains, like a wish-granting tree and a wish-fulfilling jewel, they remain accomplishing the benefit of migrating beings through awakened activities that are effortless and spontaneously accomplished. ^3-334

![[bo-khenrab-jamyang-norbu-dronme#^3-335]]

Thus, having extensively taught the Mother, the entrusting in order for it to remain for a long time: the meaning of the single stanza spoken, "healer of the sicknesses of migrating beings," and so forth, is: The supreme king of physicians who pacifies from the root and heals the physical and mental sicknesses of migrating beings, the sicknesses of suffering and afflictions, and so forth, is the Buddha Bhagavān, because the root of all sicknesses and suffering, the ignorance of self-grasping, and all the two obscurations together with their latent tendencies that arise in dependence upon it, are healed by the Tathāgatas through this very Dharma nectar of the perfection of wisdom. From the mouth of that very supreme king of physicians, the Capable One, the Buddha Bhagavān, having primarily taught the explicit subject matter, the perfection of wisdom realizing emptiness, and having implicitly taught all the stages of clear realization such as the eight topics and seventy points—in short, having spoken this path of supreme awakening, the path of the Great Vehicle, endowed with all profound and extensive phenomena—the name of that meaningful sūtra is called "The Precious Qualities Collection, the Path of Supreme Awakening," because all the precious qualities of the paths and fruits of the Great Vehicle without exception, such as the generation of the mind of awakening, are included and gathered herein.
